## Prologue / 楔子

===Original===
“And Mr. Joseph Walters is going to stay the night?” said the smooth clean-shaven man to his companion, an individual not of the most charming appearance, who had chosen to make his ginger-colored mustache merge into a pair of short chin-whiskers.

The two stood at the hall door, grinning evilly at each other; and presently a girl ran quickly down the stairs, and joined them. She was quite young, with a quaint and piquant rather than a beautiful face, and her eyes were of a shining hazel. She held a neat paper parcel in one hand, and laughed with her friends.
===Chinese===
“那么，约瑟夫·沃尔特斯先生今晚是要留下来过夜了？”那面皮光洁、剃得一脸干净的男人对同伴说道。他那同伴相貌谈不上讨喜，却偏偏蓄意让姜黄色的髭须同两绺短短的下巴须连成一片。

两人站在门厅门口，相视而笑，笑容里透着邪恶；不多时，一个姑娘快步跑下楼来，加入他们。她十分年轻，面容说不上美丽，却自有一种古雅俏皮的风致，一双眼睛是明亮的榛色。她一手提着个齐整的纸包，同两个伙伴笑作一团。

===Original===
“Leave the door open,” said the smooth man to the other, as they were going out. “Yes, by —﻿—﻿—,” he went on with an ugly oath. “We’ll leave the front door on the jar. He may like to see company, you know.”

The other man looked doubtfully about him. “Is it quite prudent do you think, Davies?” he said, pausing with his hand on the mouldering knocker. “I don’t think Lipsius would like it. What do you say, Helen?”

“I agree with Davies. Davies is an artist, and you are commonplace, Richmond, and a bit of a coward. Let the door stand open, of course. But what a pity Lipsius had to go away! He would have enjoyed himself.”

“Yes,” replied the smooth Mr. Davies, “that summons to the west was very hard on the doctor.”
===Chinese===
“把门开着，”出门时那白净的男人对另一人说。“是啊，凭———，”他接着啐出一声凶恶的咒骂。“咱们就让前门虚掩着。他没准儿乐意见客呢，你知道的。”

另一人迟疑地环顾四周。“你看这样稳妥么，戴维斯？”他说，一只手搭在朽坏的门环上，停住了。“我想利普修斯不会乐意的。你说呢，海伦？”

“我同意戴维斯。戴维斯是位艺术家，你呢，里士满，你平庸，还有点儿胆小。门当然要敞着。可惜利普修斯偏偏走了！他定会乐在其中。”

“是啊，”白净的戴维斯先生答道，“那一道西行的召唤，委实叫医生不好受。”

===Original===
The three passed out, leaving the hall door, cracked and riven with frost and wet, half open, and they stood silent for a moment under the ruinous shelter of the porch.

“Well,” said the girl, “it is done at last. I shall hurry no more on the track of the young man with spectacles.”

“We owe a great deal to you,” said Mr. Davies politely; “the doctor said so before he left. But have we not all three some farewells to make? I, for my part, propose to say goodbye, here, before this picturesque but mouldy residence, to my friend Mr. Burton, dealer in the antique and curious,” and the man lifted his hat with an exaggerated bow.
===Chinese===
三人出了门，任那扇被霜侵雨蚀、开裂绽坏的厅门半开着，在门廊残破的遮蔽下默默站了片刻。

“好了，”姑娘说，“总算办成了。我再也不必循着那戴眼镜的年轻人的踪迹奔命了。”

“我们欠你的情可着实不少，”戴维斯先生彬彬有礼地说，“医生临走前也是这么说的。不过，咱们三个难道不该互道几句告别么？至于我，我提议就在此地，在这座景致如画却霉气熏人的宅子门前，同我的朋友伯顿先生——那位经营古董珍玩的商人——道一声再见。”说着，他掀起帽子，夸张地鞠了一躬。

===Original===
“And I,” said Richmond, “bid adieu to Mr. Wilkins, the private secretary, whose company has, I confess, become a little tedious.”

“Farewell to Miss Lally, and to Miss Leicester also,” said the girl, making as she spoke a delicious courtesy. “Farewell to all occult adventure; the farce is played.”

Mr. Davies and the lady seemed full of grim enjoyment, but Richmond tugged at his whiskers nervously.
===Chinese===
“而我呢，”里士满说，“要向威尔金斯先生——那位私人秘书——告辞；我承认，此君的陪伴已变得有几分乏味了。”

“再会了，拉利小姐；也再会了，莱斯特小姐。”姑娘说着，娇媚地行了一个屈膝礼。“告别一切神秘的奇遇；闹剧收场了。”

戴维斯先生和那位女士似乎满怀阴森森的快意，里士满却紧张地捻扯着自己的颊须。

===Original===
“I feel a bit shaken up,” he said. “I’ve seen rougher things in the States, but that crying noise he made gave me a sickish feeling. And then the smell﻿—But my stomach was never very strong.”

The three friends moved away from the door, and began to walk slowly up and down what had been a gravel path, but now lay green and pulpy with damp mosses. It was a fine autumn evening, and a faint sunlight shone on the yellow walls of the old deserted house, and showed the patches of gangrenous decay, and all the stains, the black drift of rain from the broken pipes, the scabrous blots where the bare bricks were exposed, the green weeping of a gaunt laburnum that stood beside the porch, and ragged marks near the ground where the reeking clay was gaining on the worn foundations. It was a queer rambling old place, the centre perhaps two hundred years old, with dormer windows sloping from the tiled roof, and on each side there were Georgian wings; bow windows had been carried up to the first floor, and two dome-like cupolas that had once been painted a bright green were now gray and neutral. Broken urns lay upon the path, and a heavy mist seemed to rise from the unctuous clay; the neglected shrubberies, grown all tangled and unshapen, smelt dank and evil, and there was an atmosphere all about the deserted mansion that proposed thoughts of an opened grave. The three friends looked dismally at the rough grasses and the nettles that grew thick over lawn and flowerbeds; and at the sad water-pool in the midst of the weeds. There, above green and oily scum instead of lilies, stood a rusting Triton on the rocks, sounding a dirge through a shattered horn; and beyond, beyond the sunk fence and the far meadows, the sun slid down and shone red through the bars of the elm trees.
===Chinese===
“我觉得有点儿心神不宁，”他说，“在美国我见过更粗野的场面，可他发出的那种哭嚎，听得人一阵阵发呕。还有那股气味——罢了，我的肠胃向来不强。”

三位朋友离开宅门，开始在一条小径上缓缓踱来踱去；那原是一条砾石路，如今覆满湿漉漉的苔藓，绿得软烂。这是一个晴好的秋日黄昏，淡淡的日光照在废弃老宅的黄墙上，映出一片片坏疽般的朽烂，映出种种污渍：断管里雨水淌出的黑色流痕，裸砖外露处痂瘢般的黑斑，门廊旁一株嶙峋金链树渗淌的绿泪，还有近地面处一道道褴褛的痕迹——那里，湿臭的黏土正步步蚕食磨损的墙基。这是一座散漫失修的古怪老宅：正中的房舍年头或许已有二百年，瓦屋顶上斜伸出几扇屋顶窗，两侧各接一翼乔治王朝式的厢房；凸窗一直升到二楼，两座穹顶似的角楼从前漆得鲜绿，如今已灰暗得全无颜色。打碎的石瓮横陈在小径上，油亮的黏土仿佛蒸起一层沉重的雾气；久无人管的灌木丛长得纠结错乱、全无形制，散发着阴湿的恶臭；这座废宅的四周弥漫着一种气氛，教人想起一口敞开的墓穴。三位朋友怏怏地望着草坪花坛间密密丛生的野草与荨麻，望着杂草中央那一泓凄凉的水池。池上不见睡莲，只有一层绿油油的浮垢；浮垢间的礁石上立着一尊锈蚀的特里同，透过破裂的号角呜呜吹着挽歌；再远处，越过那道隐墙和迢迢草场，太阳沉落下去，红光透过榆树栅栏般的枝干条条映出。

===Original===
Richmond shivered and stamped his foot. “We had better be going soon,” he said; “there is nothing else to be done here.”

“No,” said Davies, “it is finished at last. I thought for some time we should never get hold of the gentleman with the spectacles. He was a clever fellow, but, Lord! he broke up badly at last. I can tell you he looked white at me when I touched him on the arm in the bar. But where could he have hidden the thing? We can all swear it was not on him.”
===Chinese===
里士满打了个寒噤，跺了跺脚。“咱们最好赶紧走，”他说，“这里再没有别的事可做了。”

“是啊，”戴维斯说，“总算完结了。有一阵子我还当咱们永远抓不住那位戴眼镜的先生了。他是个精明的角色，可是，天哪！他到最后可垮得真惨。不瞒你说，在酒吧间里我往他胳膊上一搭手，他瞧我那一眼，脸都白了。可他还能把那东西藏到哪儿去？咱们人人都可以发誓，那东西并不在他身上。”

===Original===
The girl laughed, and they turned away, when Richmond gave a violent start. “Ah!” he cried, turning to the girl, “what have you got there? Look, Davies, look! it’s all oozing and dripping.”

The young woman glanced down at the little parcel she was carrying, and partially unfolded the paper.

“Yes, look both of you,” she said; “it’s my own idea. Don’t you think it will do nicely for the doctor’s museum? It comes from the right hand, the hand that took the gold Tiberius.”

Mr. Davies nodded with a good deal of approbation, and Richmond lifted his ugly high-crowned bowler, and wiped his forehead with a dingy handkerchief.
===Chinese===
姑娘笑了；三人正要转身，里士满猛然一惊。“啊！”他叫道，转向姑娘，“你手里拿的是什么？看，戴维斯，快看！它在渗，在一滴一滴往下淌呢。”

年轻女子低头瞥了一眼手里提着的小包，把纸半掀开来。

“对，你们俩都瞧瞧，”她说，“这是我自己想出来的主意。你们不觉得，它正好给医生的陈列馆添件藏品么？它取自那只右手，那只接过提比略金币的手。”

戴维斯先生频频点头，深表赞许；里士满摘下他那顶难看的高顶圆礼帽，用一条脏手帕抹了抹额头。

===Original===
“I’m going,” he said; “you two can stay if you like.”

The three went round by the stable path, past the withered wilderness of the old kitchen garden, and struck off by a hedge at the back, making for a particular point in the road. About five minutes later two gentlemen, whom idleness had led to explore these forgotten outskirts of London, came sauntering up the shadowy carriage drive. They had spied the deserted house from the road, and as they observed all the heavy desolation of the place they began to moralize in the great style, with considerable debts to Jeremy Taylor.
===Chinese===
“我走了，”他说，“你们二位乐意就留下吧。”

三人取道马厩旁的小径，经过老菜园那片枯槁的荒芜，又从宅后一道树篱旁斜插出去，直奔大路上某个特定的地点。约莫五分钟之后，两位先生——闲散无聊，被引着来探看伦敦这些遭人遗忘的边隅——信步踱上了那条树影幢幢的马车道。他们从大路上便望见了这座废宅，待亲眼见得此地一派沉沉的荒凉，便开始以宏大的气派大发道德感慨，辞章间欠下杰里米·泰勒不少文债。

===Original===
“Look, Dyson,” said the one as they drew nearer, “look at those upper windows; the sun is setting, and though the panes are dusty, yet

> “The grimy sash an oriel burns.”

“Phillipps,” replied the elder and (it must be said) the more pompous of the two, “I yield to fantasy, I cannot withstand the influence of the grotesque. Here, where all is falling into dimness and dissolution, and we walk in cedarn gloom, and the very air of heaven goes mouldering to the lungs, I cannot remain commonplace. I look at that deep glow on the panes, and the house lies all enchanted; that very room, I tell you, is within all blood and fire.”
===Chinese===
“瞧，戴森，”走近时其中一人说道，“瞧那些高层的窗子；太阳正在落山，窗玻璃虽蒙着尘垢，却依然——

> “油腻的窗扉，燃烧如凸窗。”

“菲利普斯，”年长的那位、（必须承认）也是两人中更浮夸的那位答道，“我向幻想缴械了；我抵挡不住这怪诞的魅惑。在这里，万物都正没入昏昧与朽坏，我们行走于雪松般的幽暗，连天上的空气都霉烂入肺，我实在无法安于平庸。我望着窗玻璃上那团深沉的红光，整座宅子便如中了魔咒；我告诉你，正是那个房间，从里到外全是血与火。”

## Adventure of the Gold Tiberius / 提比略金币奇遇

===Original===
The acquaintance between Mr. Dyson and Mr. Charles Phillipps arose from one of those myriad chances which are every day doing their work in the streets of London. Mr. Dyson was a man of letters, and an unhappy instance of talents misapplied. With gifts that might have placed him in the flower of his youth among the most favored of Bentley’s favorite novelists, he had chosen to be perverse; he was, it is true, familiar with scholastic logic, but he knew nothing of the logic of life, and he flattered himself with the title of artist, when he was in fact but an idle and curious spectator of other men’s endeavors. Amongst many delusions, he cherished one most fondly, that he was a strenuous worker; and it was with a gesture of supreme weariness that he would enter his favorite resort, a small tobacco shop in Great Queen Street, and proclaim to anyone who cared to listen that he had seen the rising and setting of two successive suns. The proprietor of the shop, a middle-aged man of singular civility, tolerated Dyson partly out of good nature, and partly because he was a regular customer; he was allowed to sit on an empty cask, and to express his sentiments on literary and artistic matters till he was tired or the time for closing came; and if no fresh customers were attracted, it is believed that none were turned away by his eloquence. Dyson was addicted to wild experiments in tobacco; he never wearied of trying new combinations, and one evening he had just entered the shop and given utterance to his last preposterous formula, when a young fellow, of about his own age, who had come in a moment later, asked the shopman to duplicate the order on his account, smiling politely, as he spoke, to Mr. Dyson’s address. Dyson felt profoundly flattered, and after a few phrases the two entered into conversation, and in an hour’s time the tobacconist saw the new friends sitting side by side on a couple of casks, deep in talk.
===Chinese===
戴森先生与查尔斯·菲利普斯先生相识于机缘——那种在伦敦街头日日做着自己功课的千万种机缘之一。戴森先生是个舞文弄墨之人，也是才华误用的一个不幸例证。凭着那份天赋，他本可在风华正茂之年跻身本特利出版社最受宠的小说家之列，他却偏偏别扭着性子，弃正路而不由；诚然，他谙熟经院逻辑，却对人生的逻辑一窍不通；他自我陶醉地冠上艺术家的头衔，实际上不过是旁人劳碌的一个闲散而好奇的看客。在种种迷梦之中，他最痴心信守的一条，便是自认是个勤勉工作的人；他总是带着一副倦极欲仙的姿态，踱进他惯常的落脚处——大皇后街上的一家小烟草店——向一切愿意听的人宣告：他已经连着见过两轮太阳的升落了。店主是个中年人，礼数周全得出奇，容让戴森，一半出于好性子，一半也因为他是位常客；他获准坐在一只空桶上，就文学艺术诸事大发议论，直到说累了或者打烊的时辰到了；虽说他的高谈阔论不曾招徕什么新主顾，但据说也没有谁被他吓得掉头而去。戴森嗜好在烟草上做狂野的实验，调配新方子百试不厌；一天傍晚，他刚进店报出他那最新一剂荒唐的配方，一个年纪与他相仿的年轻人——比他晚到片刻——便请店家照方抓药，记在自己的账上，说话间还朝着戴森先生的方向客气地一笑。戴森深感荣幸之至；三言两语之后，两人便攀谈起来，不出一个钟头，烟草商便看见这对新朋友并肩坐在两只桶上，谈得入了港。

===Original===
“My dear sir,” said Dyson, “I will give you the task of the literary man in a phrase. He has got to do simply this: to invent a wonderful story, and to tell it in a wonderful manner.”

“I will grant you that,” said Mr. Phillipps, “but you will allow me to insist that in the hands of the true artist in words all stories are marvellous, and every circumstance has its peculiar wonder. The matter is of little consequence, the manner is everything. Indeed, the highest skill is shown in taking matter apparently commonplace and transmuting it by the high alchemy of style into the pure gold of art.”

“That is indeed a proof of great skill, but it is great skill exerted foolishly, or at least unadvisedly. It is as if a great violinist were to show us what marvellous harmonies he could draw from a child’s banjo.”

“No, no, you are really wrong. I see you take a radically mistaken view of life. But we must thresh this out. Come to my rooms; I live not far from here.”
===Chinese===
“亲爱的先生，”戴森说，“让我用一句话道出文学家的任务。他要做的无非是这个：编一个绝妙的故事，再以绝妙的方式把它讲出来。”

“这一点我姑且承认，”菲利普斯先生说，“但请容我坚持：在真正的语言艺术家手中，一切故事都是神奇的，每一个情境都有它独特的奇处。题材无足轻重，手法才是一切。的确，最高的技艺，正在于取用表面上平庸的题材，借文体这门高级的炼金术，把它点化成艺术的纯金。”

“那确是高超技艺的明证，可惜这技艺用得愚蠢，至少是失于轻率。这就好比一位小提琴大师，偏要向人炫耀他能从一个孩子的班卓琴上拉出怎样神妙的和声。”

“不，不，你实在错了。我看你对人生抱持着一种根本错误的见解。不过这事非得辩个水落石出不可。到我寓所来吧，我住得离这儿不远。”

===Original===
It was thus that Mr. Dyson became the associate of Mr. Charles Phillipps, who lived in a quiet square not far from Holborn. Thenceforth they haunted each other’s rooms at intervals, sometimes regular, and occasionally the reverse, and made appointments to meet at the shop in Queen Street, where their talk robbed the tobacconist’s profit of half its charm. There was a constant jarring of literary formulas, Dyson exalting the claims of the pure imagination, while Phillipps, who was a student of physical science and something of an ethnologist, insisted that all literature ought to have a scientific basis. By the mistaken benevolence of deceased relatives both young men were placed out of reach of hunger, and so, meditating high achievements, idled their time pleasantly away, and revelled in the careless joys of a Bohemianism devoid of the sharp seasoning of adversity.
===Chinese===
戴森先生与查尔斯·菲利普斯先生就这样结成了同伴；后者住在霍尔本不远处一座僻静的广场上。从此两人你来我往地串门，间隔时有时无，或规律或不规律，又约定在大皇后街那家烟草店碰头；他们的高谈阔论，把烟草商营生的一半乐趣都夺走了。两人的文学信条始终铿然相磕：戴森高举纯粹想象的旗帜，菲利普斯——他研习自然科学，还算得上半个民族学家——则坚称一切文学都应当有科学的基础。托已故亲眷错付的慈悲之福，两位青年都免于饥馁之虞，于是一边悬想着宏图伟业，一边把光阴悠闲愉快地消磨过去，安然陶醉于一种波希米亚生涯的无忧之乐——那里面全没有逆境这味辛辣的作料。

===Original===
One night in June Mr. Phillipps was sitting in his room in the calm retirement of Red Lion Square. He had opened the window, and was smoking placidly, while he watched the movement of life below. The sky was clear, and the afterglow of sunset had lingered long about it; and the flushing twilight of a summer evening, vying with the gas-lamps in the square, had fashioned a chiaroscuro that had in it something unearthly; and the children, racing to and fro upon the pavement, the lounging idlers by the public, and the casual passersby rather flickered, and hovered in the play of lights than stood out substantial things. By degrees in the houses opposite one window after another leaped out a square of light, now and again a figure would shape itself against a blind and vanish, and to all this semi-theatrical magic the runs and flourishes of brave Italian opera played a little distance off on a piano-organ seemed an appropriate accompaniment, while the deep-muttered bass of the traffic of Holborn never ceased. Phillipps enjoyed the scene and its effects; the light in the sky faded and turned to darkness, and the square gradually grew silent, and still he sat dreaming at the window, till the sharp peal of the house bell roused him, and looking at his watch he found that it was past ten o’clock. There was a knock at the door, and his friend Mr. Dyson entered, and, according to his custom, sat down in an armchair and began to smoke in silence.
===Chinese===
六月的一个夜晚，菲利普斯先生正坐在红狮广场幽静居所自己的房间里。他推开了窗，安闲地抽着烟，望着楼下生之动静。天空澄澈，落日的余烬久久不散；夏日黄昏红晕的微明与广场上的煤气灯争辉，调出一片明暗交错的光影，其中有某种非人间的意味；人行道上奔来跑去的孩童，酒馆旁懒散闲荡的游手，以及偶然路过的行人，与其说是些结结实实的存在，不如说在光的游戏里明灭浮沉。对面人家的窗子里，一方一方光亮渐次跃出，时不时有个人影在窗帘上倏然成形，随即隐没；不远处的手摇风琴奏起雄壮的意大利歌剧，那华彩流转的乐句，与这一切半剧场式的幻术正相匹配，而霍尔本街上车马之声，则像一支低沉絮语的低音声部，从不间断。菲利普斯玩味着这幅景象和它的光效；天光淡去，转为沉沉的夜色，广场渐渐静了下来，他仍坐在窗前出神，直到一声清脆的门铃把他惊醒，掏出表一看，已过十点钟。门上响起叩声，他的朋友戴森先生走了进来，照着他的老习惯，往扶手椅里一坐，闷头抽起烟来。

===Original===
“You know, Phillipps,” he said at length, “that I have always battled for the marvellous. I remember your maintaining in that chair that one has no business to make use of the wonderful, the improbable, the odd coincidence in literature, and you took the ground that it was wrong to do so, because, as a matter of fact, the wonderful and the improbable don’t happen, and men’s lives are not really shaped by odd coincidence. Now, mind you, if that were so, I would not grant your conclusion, because I think the ‘criticism-of-life’ theory is all nonsense; but I deny your premise. A most singular thing has happened to me tonight.”

“Really, Dyson, I am very glad to hear it. Of course I oppose your argument, whatever it may be; but if you would be good enough to tell me of your adventure I should be delighted.”
===Chinese===
“你是知道的，菲利普斯，”他终于开口道，“我一向为神奇而战。我记得你就坐在那把椅子上主张：文学家无权在作品里动用奇异的、不可能的、凑巧得古怪的东西；你的立足点是，这样做便是错，因为事实上，奇异之事与不可能之事根本不会发生，人的一生也并非真会被古怪的巧合所塑造。不过，听着，纵然如此，我也不会承认你的结论，因为我认为那套‘人生之批判’的理论全是胡说；但我否认你的前提。今天晚上，一件极其离奇的事落到我头上了。”

“当真么，戴森？听你这么说我很高兴。当然，不管你的论点是什么，我都要反驳；不过，倘蒙你把你的奇遇讲上一讲，我是不胜欣喜的。”

===Original===
“Well, it came about like this. I have had a very hard day’s work; indeed, I have scarcely moved from my old bureau since seven o’clock last night. I wanted to work out that idea we discussed last Tuesday, you know, the notion of the fetish-worshipper.”

“Yes, I remember. Have you been able to do anything with it?”
===Chinese===
“咳，事情是这么来的。我苦干了一整天；说实话，从昨晚七点起，我几乎就没挪过窝，一直伏在我的旧书桌上。我要把上星期二咱们讨论的那个意思写出来——你知道的，就是拜物教徒那个构想。”

“记得，记得。你写出什么名堂了么？”

===Original===
“Yes; it came out better than I expected; but there were great difficulties, the usual agony between the conception and the execution. Anyhow I got it done at about seven o’clock tonight, and I thought I should like a little of the fresh air. I went out and wandered rather aimlessly about the streets; my head was full of my tale, and I didn’t much notice where I was going. I got into those quiet places to the north of Oxford Street as you go west, the genteel residential neighborhood of stucco and prosperity. I turned east again without knowing it, and it was quite dark when I passed along a sombre little by-street, ill lighted and empty. I did not know at the time in the least where I was, but I found out afterwards that it was not very far from Tottenham Court Road. I strolled idly along, enjoying the stillness; on one side there seemed to be the back premises of some great shop; tier after tier of dusty windows lifted up into the night, with gibbet-like contrivances for raising heavy goods, and below large doors, fast closed and bolted, all dark and desolate. Then there came a huge pantechnicon warehouse; and over the way a grim blank wall, as forbidding as the wall of a jail, and then the headquarters of some volunteer regiment, and afterwards a passage leading to a court where wagons were standing to be hired. It was, one might almost say, a street devoid of inhabitants, and scarce a window showed the glimmer of a light. I was wondering at the strange peace and dimness there, where it must be close to some roaring main artery of London life, when suddenly I heard the noise of dashing feet tearing along the pavement at full speed, and from a narrow passage, a mews or something of that kind, a man was discharged as from a catapult under my very nose and rushed past me, flinging something from him as he ran. He was gone and down another street in an instant, almost before I knew what had happened, but I didn’t much bother about him, I was watching something else. I told you he had thrown something away; well, I watched what seemed a line of flame flash through the air and fly quivering over the pavement, and in spite of myself I could not help tearing after it. The impetus lessened, and I saw something like a bright halfpenny roll slower and slower, and then deflect towards the gutter, hover for a moment on the edge, and dance down into a drain. I believe I cried out in positive despair, though I hadn’t the least notion what I was hunting; and then to my joy I saw that, instead of dropping into the sewer, it had fallen flat across two bars. I stooped down and picked it up and whipped it into my pocket, and I was just about to walk on when I heard again that sound of dashing footsteps. I don’t know why I did it, but as a matter of fact I dived down into the mews, or whatever it was, and stood as much in the shadow as possible. A man went by with a rush a few paces from where I was standing, and I felt uncommonly pleased that I was in hiding. I couldn’t make out much feature, but I saw his eyes gleaming and his teeth showing, and he had an ugly-looking knife in one hand, and I thought things would be very unpleasant for gentleman number one if the second robber, or robbed, or what you like, caught him up. I can tell you, Phillipps, a fox hunt is exciting enough, when the horn blows clear on a winter morning, and the hounds give tongue, and the redcoats charge away, but it’s nothing to a man hunt, and that’s what I had a slight glimpse of tonight. There was murder in the fellow’s eyes as he went by, and I don’t think there was much more than fifty seconds between the two. I only hope it was enough.”
===Chinese===
“写出来了；比我想的顺手，可难题也不少——从构想到落笔之间惯常要受的那场罪。好歹今晚七点钟左右写完了，我想着该去透透气。我出了门，在街上漫无目的地闲逛；满脑子都是我那篇故事，也不大理会自己走到哪儿了。不知不觉走进了牛津街以北往西去的那些清静地段，灰泥墙面、殷实气象的体面住宅区。又不知不觉折向东，待我走过一条幽暗的小巷时，天已全黑了——那街灯光昏沉，空无一人。我当时全然不知身在何处，事后才查明，那地方离托特纳姆宫路不远。我懒懒地信步走着，享受那份静谧；一侧像是某家大店铺的后场，一层层蒙尘的窗户向夜空里叠升上去，窗外装着绞架似的吊重家伙什，底下是几扇大门，门闩紧锁，一片漆黑荒凉。接着是一座巨大的家具仓储行；马路对面一堵阴森森的空墙，森然不亚于监狱的高墙；再过去是某个义勇军团的本部，然后是一条通道，通向一个院子，院里停着待雇的货运马车。那几乎可以说是一条没有居民的街，难得有一扇窗透出灯光的微芒。我正诧异于此地古怪的安宁与昏昧——要知道这里必定紧邻着伦敦生活某条咆哮的大动脉——忽听得一阵疾足狂奔之声撕开人行道，紧接着，从一条窄巷里（马厩巷之类的所在），一个人像被弹弓射出来一般，正正弹到我的鼻子底下，从我身边疾冲而过，一路跑一路抛开一件什么东西。转眼间他已窜下另一条街，几乎不等我明白发生了什么；可我没大去理会他，我在盯着另一样东西。我说过他抛掉了一件东西；喏，我眼看着一线火光似的物事划破空气，颤巍巍地贴着人行道飞掠，我不由自主，撒腿就追。那股冲劲渐渐弱了，我看见一样像明亮的半便士铜板似的东西越滚越慢，随后偏向街沟，在沟沿上悬了一悬，旋着舞进了阴沟口。我相信我当真绝望地喊出声来了，尽管我压根儿不知道自己追的是什么；随即让我大喜过望的是，它并没有落进下水道，而是平搭在了两根铁栅上。我弯腰捡起它，一把塞进口袋，正待举步再走，又听见了那阵疾奔的脚步声。我不知道自己为何那样做，反正事实是，我一个箭步闪进了那条马厩巷——管它是什么——拣阴影最浓处站定。一个人疾冲而过，离我站的地方只有几步远，我暗自庆幸自己藏着没露行藏。面目我瞧不真切，只看见他双目放光，龇着牙，一只手里攥着一把凶相毕露的刀；我寻思，倘若第二位——那强盗，或被劫者，随你怎么称呼——追上了头一位绅士，事情就要非常不好看了。不瞒你说，菲利普斯，猎狐也够刺激的了——冬晨号角嘹亮，群犬齐吠，红衣猎手们纵马冲锋——可比起猎人来，那就算不得什么了；今晚我恰好瞥见了一眼猎人的滋味。那人擦身而过时，眼睛里全是杀气；我看前后两人相隔不过五十来秒。但愿这点工夫够他逃命。”

===Original===
Dyson leant back in his armchair and relit his pipe, and puffed thoughtfully. Phillipps began to walk up and down the room, musing over the story of violent death fleeting in chase along the pavement, the knife shining in the lamplight, the fury of the pursuer, and the terror of the pursued.

“Well,” he said at last, “and what was it, after all, that you rescued from the gutter?”
===Chinese===
戴森往扶手椅里一靠，重新点上烟斗，若有所思地喷吐着。菲利普斯开始在屋里踱来踱去，细细回味着这段故事——暴死追着人沿人行道飞掠而过，刀刃在灯光下闪亮，追人者的狂怒与被追者的惶恐。

“那么，”他终于开口，“你从街沟里抢救出来的，究竟是什么东西？”

===Original===
Dyson jumped up, evidently quite startled. “I really haven’t a notion. I didn’t think of looking. But we shall see.”

He fumbled in his waistcoat pocket and drew out a small and shining object, and laid it on the table. It glowed there beneath the lamp with the radiant glory of rare old gold; and the image and the letters stood out in high relief, clear and sharp, as if it had but left the mint a month before. The two men bent over it, and Phillipps took it up and examined it closely.
===Chinese===
戴森一跃而起，显然大为愕然。“我还真说不上来。我没想到要看一眼。不过咱们这就瞧瞧。”

他在马甲口袋里摸了摸，掏出一件小小的、亮闪闪的物件，搁在桌上。它在灯下熠熠生辉，焕着老黄金那种罕见的莹润光华；浮雕像与铭文高高凸起，清晰锐利，仿佛一个月前才离开造币厂的新币。两人俯身细看，菲利普斯把它拈起来，凑近端详。

===Original===
“Imp. Tiberius Caesar Augustus,” he read the legend, and then, looking at the reverse of the coin, he stared in amazement, and at last turned to Dyson with a look of exultation.

“Do you know what you have found?” he said.
===Chinese===
“Imp. Tiberius Caesar Augustus（提比略·凯撒·奥古斯都皇帝），”他念出铭文；再一看币的背面，他惊愕地瞪大了眼，末了转向戴森，一脸的狂喜。

“你可知道你捡到了什么？”他说。

===Original===
“Apparently a gold coin of some antiquity,” said Dyson, coolly.

“Quite so, a gold Tiberius. No, that is wrong. You have found *the* gold Tiberius. Look at the reverse.”
===Chinese===
“明摆着是枚有些年头的老金币。”戴森淡淡地说。

“正是，一枚提比略金币。不，这么说不对。你捡到的是*那枚*提比略金币。看背面。”

===Original===
Dyson looked and saw the coin was stamped with the figure of a faun standing amidst reeds and flowing water. The features, minute as they were, stood out in delicate outline; it was a face lovely and yet terrible, and Dyson thought of the well-known passage of the lad’s playmate, gradually growing with his growth and increasing with his stature, till the air was filled with the rank fume of the goat.

“Yes,” he said, “it is a curious coin. Do you know it?”
===Chinese===
戴森看了看，只见币面上打着一位法翁的立像，立在芦苇与流水之间。那眉目虽细微入毫，却以精致的轮廓凸现出来；那是一张既可爱又可怖的脸。戴森想起那段有名的文字：少年的玩伴怎样随他长大而长大、随他长高而长高，直到空气中弥漫了山羊的腥膻。

“是啊，”他说，“这是枚古怪的币。你认得它么？”

===Original===
“I know about it. It is one of the comparatively few historical objects in existence; it is all storied like those jewels we have read of. A whole cycle of legend has gathered round the thing; the tale goes that it formed part of an issue struck by Tiberius to commemorate an infamous excess. You see the legend on the reverse: ‘Victoria.’ It is said that by an extraordinary accident the whole issue was thrown into the melting pot, and that only this one coin escaped. It glints through history and legend, appearing and disappearing, with intervals of a hundred years in time and continents in place. It was discovered by an Italian humanist, and lost and rediscovered. It has not been heard of since 1727, when Sir Joshua Byrde, a Turkey merchant, brought it home from Aleppo, and vanished with it a month after he had shown it to the virtuosi, no man knew or knows where. And here it is!”
===Chinese===
“知其来历。它是存世之物中屈指可数的历史性器物之一；它浑身缀满了传说，就像我们读到过的那些宝石。整整一轮传说的循环在这件东西四周聚拢；据说它是提比略某次铸币中的一枚，为的是纪念一场声名狼藉的荒淫。你瞧背面的铭文：‘胜利’（Victoria）。据说因一场异乎寻常的变故，那次铸币尽数投了熔炉，只有这一枚幸免。它在历史与传说中时隐时现、倏明倏灭，一别便是一百年光阴、一大洲地域。它曾由一位意大利人文学者发掘出来，得而复失，失而复得。自 1727 年起，它便音讯杳然。那一年，土耳其货商乔舒亚·伯德爵士携它自阿勒颇归国，向古物鉴赏家们出示之后不出一月，便带着它一并失踪，去向何方，当时无人知晓，如今也无人知晓。而它，这不就在这儿了么！”

===Original===
“Put it into your pocket, Dyson,” he said, after a pause. “I would not let anyone have a glimpse of the thing, if I were you. I would not talk about it. Did either of the men you saw see you?”

“Well, I think not. I don’t think the first man, the man who was vomited out of the dark passage, saw anything at all; and I am sure that the second could not have seen me.”
===Chinese===
“把它收进口袋里吧，戴森。”他顿了顿，又说，“我要是你，就绝不让任何人瞧见这东西一眼，也绝口不提它。你看见的那两个人，有没有谁瞧见了你？”

“呃，我想没有。头一个人，就是从黑巷子里呕吐出来似的那位，我看他什么也没瞧见；第二个嘛，我敢肯定他不可能看见我。”

===Original===
“And you didn’t really see them. You couldn’t recognize either the one or the other if you met him in the street tomorrow?”

“No, I don’t think I could. The street, as I said, was dimly lighted, and they ran like madmen.”
===Chinese===
“你也没真正看清他们。明天在街上撞见，无论是这一个还是那一个，你都认不出来吧？”

“认不出，我想是认不出的。那街，我说过，灯光昏昏，他们又跑得像疯子。”

===Original===
The two men sat silent for some time, each weaving his own fancies of the story; but lust of the marvellous was slowly overpowering Dyson’s more sober thoughts.

“It is all more strange than I fancied,” he said at last. “It was queer enough what I saw; a man is sauntering along a quiet, sober, everyday London street, a street of gray houses and blank walls, and there, for a moment, a veil seems drawn aside, and the very fume of the pit steams up through the flagstones, the ground glows, red hot, beneath his feet, and he seems to hear the hiss of the infernal cauldron. A man flying in mad terror for his life, and furious hate pressing hot on his steps with knife drawn ready; here indeed is horror. But what is all that to what you have told me? I tell you, Phillipps, I see the plot thicken, our steps will henceforth be dogged with mystery, and the most ordinary incidents will teem with significance. You may stand out against it, and shut your eyes, but they will be forced open; mark my words, you will have to yield to the inevitable. A clue, tangled if you like, has been placed by chance in our hands; it will be our business to follow it up. As for the guilty person or persons in this strange case, they will be unable to escape us, our nets will be spread far and wide over this great city, and suddenly, in the streets and places of public resort, we shall in some way or other be made aware that we are in touch with the unknown criminal. Indeed, I almost fancy I see him slowly approaching this quiet square of yours; he is loitering at street corners, wandering, apparently without aim, down far-reaching thoroughfares, but all the while coming nearer and nearer, drawn by an irresistible magnetism, as ships were drawn to the Loadstone Rock in the Eastern tale.”
===Chinese===
两人默然对坐了好一会儿，各自在心里为这故事编织着想象；只是对神奇的贪恋，正渐渐压倒戴森较为清醒的念头。

“这可比我想的还要离奇，”他终于说，“我亲眼所见的那一幕已够古怪了：一个人正踱在一条清静、本分、日日如此的伦敦街上，一条灰房子与空墙的街；忽然之间，仿佛有一幅帷幕撩开了一角，深渊的浓烟竟从石板缝里蒸腾上来，地面在他脚下烧得通红发亮，他仿佛还听得见地狱大锅里沸腾的嘶响。一个人没命狂奔、魂飞魄散地逃命，一团狂怒的仇恨挟着出鞘的利刀，灼灼紧逼他的脚跟；这确乎是恐怖了。可这比起你告诉我的，又算得了什么？我告诉你，菲利普斯，我看见情节正日渐浓稠；从此以后，神秘将亦步亦趋地缀着咱们的脚跟，最寻常的琐事也将蓄满深意。你尽管抗拒，尽管闭眼，眼皮也会被人撬开；记住我的话，你终得向这不可回避的命运低头。一条线索——你尽可以说它纠缠凌乱——已经偶然落入我们掌中；追查到底，便是我们的事业。至于这桩奇案里有罪的一人或数人，他们休想逃出我们的手掌；我们的罗网将撒遍这座大都会的四面八方；倏忽之间，在街头，在人烟辐辏的场所，我们会以某种方式恍然知觉：我们已同那无名的罪人搭上了线。真的，我几乎仿佛看见他正缓缓逼近你这座清静的广场；他在街角徘徊，顺着长街远巷漫无目的地荡去，实则步步逼近，被一种不可抗拒的磁力吸着，恰如《天方夜谭》里那些船只被吸向磁石山一般。”

===Original===
“I certainly think,” replied Phillipps, “that, if you pull out that coin and flourish it under people’s noses as you are doing at the present moment, you will very probably find yourself in touch with the criminal, or a criminal. You will undoubtedly be robbed with violence. Otherwise, I see no reason why either of us should be troubled. No one saw you secure the coin, and no one knows you have it. I, for my part, shall sleep peacefully, and go about my business with a sense of security and a firm dependence on the natural order of things. The events of the evening, the adventure in the street, have been odd, I grant you, but I resolutely decline to have any more to do with the matter, and, if necessary, I shall consult the police. I will not be enslaved by a gold Tiberius, even though it swims into my ken in a manner which is somewhat melodramatic.”
===Chinese===
“我倒确乎认为，”菲利普斯答道，“倘若你把那枚金币掏出来，像你这会儿干的那样在人家鼻子底下挥舞，那你多半真能同罪人——至少某个罪人——搭上线。你会毫无疑问地遭到暴力抢劫。除此外，我看不出你我有什么可烦恼的理由。没人看见你捡起金币，也没人知道它在你手里。至于我，我自会安安稳稳地睡觉，心安理得地办我的事，笃定地信赖着万物的自然秩序。今晚的事，街上那场奇遇，确是古怪，我承认；但我断然拒绝同此事再有任何瓜葛，必要时，我会去请教警察。我决不愿被一枚提比略金币奴役，哪怕它闯进我视野的方式，未免有几分闹剧腔。”

===Original===
“And I for my part,” said Dyson, “go forth like a knight-errant in search of adventure. Not that I shall need to seek; rather adventure will seek me; I shall be like a spider in the midst of his web, responsive to every movement, and ever on the alert.”

Shortly afterwards Dyson took his leave, and Mr. Phillipps spent the rest of the night in examining some flint arrowheads which he had purchased. He had every reason to believe that they were the work of a modern and not a paleolithic man, still he was far from gratified when a close scrutiny showed him that his suspicions were well founded. In his anger at the turpitude which would impose on an ethnologist, he completely forgot Dyson and the gold Tiberius; and when he went to bed at first sunlight, the whole tale had faded utterly from his thoughts.
===Chinese===
“而我呢，”戴森说，“便要像游侠骑士一般出门寻奇历险了。倒也无须我去寻找；毋宁说是奇险会来寻我；我将像一只踞在网心的蜘蛛，对每一丝颤动都有感应，时时警戒，刻刻不怠。”

不久戴森便告辞了；菲利普斯先生把下半夜都用在检视他购得的几枚燧石箭镞上。他有一切理由相信，那是今人而非旧石器时代人的手笔；可是细细察验之下，证实他的疑心确凿有据时，他却远没有感到快慰。他愤然于竟有如此卑劣之徒敢蒙骗一位民族学家，把戴森和提比略金币忘了个一干二净；待到晨光初动时上床就寝，整个故事已从他心头褪得影踪全无。

## The Encounter of the Pavement / 人行道上的邂逅

===Original===
Mr. Dyson, walking leisurely along Oxford Street, and staring with bland inquiry at whatever caught his attention, enjoyed in all its rare flavors the sensation that he was really very hard at work. His observation of mankind, the traffic, and the shop-windows tickled his faculties with an exquisite bouquet; he looked serious, as one looks on whom charges of weight and moment are laid, and he was attentive in his glances to right and left, for fear lest he should miss some circumstance of more acute significance. He had narrowly escaped being run over at a crossing by a charging van, for he hated to hurry his steps, and indeed the afternoon was warm; and he had just halted by a place of popular refreshment, when the astounding gestures of a well dressed individual on the opposite pavement held him enchanted and gasping like a fish. A treble line of hansoms, carriages, vans, cabs, and omnibuses, was tearing east and west, and not the most daring adventurer of the crossings would have cared to try his fortune; but the person who had attracted Dyson’s attention seemed to rage on the very edge of the pavement, now and then darting forward at the hazard of instant death, and at each repulse absolutely dancing with excitement, to the rich amusement of the passersby. At last, a gap that would have tried the courage of a street-boy appeared between the serried lines of vehicles, and the man rushed across in a frenzy, and escaping by a hair’s breadth pounced upon Dyson as a tiger pounces on her prey. “I saw you looking about you,” he said, sputtering out his words in his intense eagerness; “would you mind telling me this? Was the man who came out of the Aerated Bread Shop and jumped into the hansom three minutes ago a youngish looking man with dark whiskers and spectacles? Can’t you speak, man? For Heaven’s sake can’t you speak? Answer me; it’s a matter of life and death.”

===Chinese===
戴森先生沿牛津街信步而行，凡入眼帘之物，皆报以温文的一探，把“自己当真是在辛勤工作”的滋味，尝了个淋漓尽致。他对人群、车马与橱窗的观察，如一缕精妙的酒香，撩动着他的心智；他神情严肃，一如身负重任者的神色，左右顾盼之际目光专注，唯恐错过什么意味更为深长的情状。在一处道口，一辆横冲直撞的货车险些把他碾在轮下——他不肯加快脚步，何况午后天气也着实燠热；他刚在一家大众饮食铺前站定，便看见对面人行道上一个衣着体面的人物做出种种骇人的手势，惊得他目瞪口呆，像一条出了水的鱼直喘气。三行首尾相衔的双轮马车、四轮马车、货车、街车与公共马车正东西对驶、呼啸而过，再胆大包天的穿街好汉也不敢在此刻碰运气；可这位吸住了戴森目光的人物，却像在人行道边上气得发狂，不时朝车流猛冲过去，全然豁出性命，每被逼回一次，便焦灼得手舞足蹈，逗得过路人大乐。终于，密匝匝的车阵之间裂开一道空隙——那空隙小得连街头顽童也要掂量掂量胆量——那人便疯了一般冲过街来，间不容发地躲过车马，猛扑到戴森跟前，恰似母虎扑食。“我看见您在东张西望，”他急不可耐，话都从嘴里迸出来，“可否告诉我一件事？三分钟前从充气面包店（Aerated Bread Shop）里出来、跳上一辆双轮马车的那个人，是不是个模样年轻的男人，黑黑的连鬓胡子，戴着眼镜？您倒是说话呀，老兄？看在老天爷份上，您倒是说话呀？回答我；这是人命关天的事。”

===Original===
The words bubbled and boiled out of the man’s mouth in the fury of his emotion, his face went from red to white, and the beads of sweat stood out on his forehead, and he stamped his feet as he spoke and tore with his hand at his coat, as if something swelled and choked him, stopping the passage of his breath.

“My dear sir,” said Dyson, “I always like to be accurate. Your observation was perfectly correct. As you say, a youngish man, a man, I should say, of somewhat timid bearing, ran rapidly out of the shop here, and bounced into a hansom that must have been waiting for him, as it went eastwards at once. Your friend also wore spectacles, as you say. Perhaps you would like me to call a hansom for you to follow the gentleman?”

“No, thank you; it would be waste of time.” The man gulped down something which appeared to rise in his throat, and Dyson was alarmed to see him shaking with hysterical laughter, and he clung hard to a lamppost and swayed and staggered like a ship in a heavy gale.

===Chinese===
这一番话在他狂烈的激动中从嘴里翻滚沸腾而出；他的脸由红转白，额上沁出涔涔汗珠，说话间直跺脚，一只手撕扯着自己的衣襟，仿佛有什么东西在胸中鼓胀起来，哽住了他的呼吸。

“亲爱的先生，”戴森说，“我一向讲究精确。您的观察分毫不差。正如您所言，一个模样年轻的男人——依我看，举止还有几分怯生生的——从这家铺子里疾奔而出，一头跳上一辆双轮马车；那车必定是专候着他的，因为即刻便向东去了。正如您所说，您那位朋友还戴着眼镜。要不要我替您叫一辆车，去追那位先生？”

“不必了，谢谢；那不过是白费工夫。”那人咽下一样仿佛从喉咙里涌上来的东西；接着戴森惊恐地看见，他浑身发着歇斯底里的笑，双手死死抱住一根灯柱，摇摇晃晃，颠颠倒倒，像一条顶着狂风巨浪的船。

===Original===
“How shall I face the doctor?” he murmured to himself. “It is too hard to fail at the last moment.” Then he seemed to recollect himself, and stood straight again, and looked quietly at Dyson. “I owe you an apology for my violence,” he said at last. “Many men would not be so patient as you have been. Would you mind adding to your kindness by walking with me a little way? I feel a little sick; I think it’s the sun.”

Dyson nodded assent, and devoted himself to a quiet scrutiny of this strange personage as they moved on together. The man was dressed in quiet taste, and the most scrupulous observer could find nothing amiss with the fashion or make of his clothes, yet, from his hat to his boots, everything seemed inappropriate. His silk hat, Dyson thought, should have been a high bowler of odious pattern worn with a baggy morning-coat, and an instinct told him that the fellow did not commonly carry a clean pocket-handkerchief. The face was not of the most agreeable pattern, and was in no way improved by a pair of bulbous chin-whiskers of a ginger hue, into which mustaches of light color merged imperceptibly. Yet in spite of these signals hung out by nature, Dyson felt that the individual beside him was something more than compact of vulgarity. He was struggling with himself, holding his feelings in check, but now and again passion would mount black to his face, and it was evidently by a supreme effort that he kept himself from raging like a madman. Dyson found something curious and a little terrible in the spectacle of an occult emotion thus striving for the mastery, and threatening to break out at every instant with violence, and they had gone some distance before the person whom he had met by so odd a hazard was able to speak quietly.

===Chinese===
“教我怎么去见医生？”他喃喃自语，“到了最后关头却功亏一篑，也太残酷了。”随即他似乎恢复了神志，重新站直，安静地望着戴森。“我为我的粗暴向您道歉，”他终于说，“换了别人，未必像您这样有耐性。可否再劳驾您的美意，陪我走一小段路？我有点儿恶心；只怕是太阳晒的。”

戴森点头应允，一面暗中细细打量这位奇怪的人物，两人一路走去。那人衣着素雅入时而不张扬，最挑剔的观察者也挑不出他服饰的式样与做工有任何差池；然而从帽子到靴子，浑身上下却处处透着不称。戴森想，他那顶丝质大礼帽，本该换成一顶式样恶俗的高筒圆顶帽，再配一件松松垮垮的晨礼服才对；一种本能还告诉他：此等角色，平素身上是决计不备干净手帕的。那张脸本就算不得讨喜的款式，颏下一蓬圆鼓鼓的姜黄连鬓胡子，更不曾替它添半分颜色——浅色的髭须不知不觉便融进那片胡子里去了。然而尽管天生挂出了这许多记号，戴森仍觉出身旁这人并不止是一团俗气了事。他在同自己搏斗，把情感死死按捺着，可激情仍不时黑沉沉地涌上脸来；他显然全凭一股超人的自制，才没有像疯子一般咆哮起来。眼见一种隐秘的激情这样争夺着主宰之权、时时刻刻威胁着要暴烈地迸发出来，戴森觉得其中颇有几分古怪，也颇有几分可怖；两人走出了好一段路，这位被他以如此古怪的机缘撞见的人物，才总算能够安静地开口说话了。

===Original===
“You are really very good,” he said. “I apologize again; my rudeness was really most unjustifiable. I feel my conduct demands an explanation, and I shall be happy to give it you. Do you happen to know of any place near here where one could sit down? I should really be very glad.”

“My dear sir,” said Dyson, solemnly, “the only café in London is close by. Pray do not consider yourself as bound to offer me any explanation, but at the same time I should be most happy to listen to you. Let us turn down here.”

===Chinese===
“您真是个大好人，”他说，“我再次道歉；我的鲁莽实在无可宽宥。我自知这番行径需要一番解释，我也乐于向您奉告。这附近可巧有能坐一坐的地方么？那就再好不过了。”

“亲爱的先生，”戴森一本正经地说，“伦敦独一无二的咖啡馆就在近旁。您千万不必觉得非解释不可；不过话说回来，我倒是极乐意听您讲。咱们打这儿拐进去吧。”

===Original===
They walked down a sober street and turned into what seemed a narrow passage past an iron-barred gate thrown back. The passage was paved with flagstones, and decorated with handsome shrubs in pots on either side, and the shadow of the high walls made a coolness which was very agreeable after the hot breath of the sunny street. Presently the passage opened out into a tiny square, a charming place, a morsel of France transplanted into the heart of London. High walls rose on either side, covered with glossy creepers, flowerbeds beneath were gay with nasturtiums, geraniums, and marigolds, and odorous with mignonette, and in the centre of the square a fountain hidden by greenery sent a cool shower continually plashing into the basin beneath, and the very noise made this retreat delightful. Chairs and tables were disposed at convenient intervals, and at the other end of the court broad doors had been thrown back; beyond was a long, dark room, and the turmoil of traffic had become a distant murmur. Within the room one or two men were sitting at the tables, writing and sipping, but the courtyard was empty.

===Chinese===
两人走过一条端庄安静的街，拐进一条窄巷模样的通道，一道铁栅门敞向一旁。巷道铺着石板，两侧摆着一盆盆秀气的灌木，高墙的阴影里透出一片清凉，在向阳大街的热浪之后，这份凉意着实宜人。不多时，巷道豁然开阔，显出一方小小的庭院——一处迷人的所在，一小片移栽到伦敦心脏里的法兰西。高墙峙立两侧，爬满了油亮的藤蔓；墙下花圃里旱金莲、天竺葵与万寿菊开得正闹，木樨草的芬芳四溢；庭院正中，一座被绿荫半掩的喷泉不住把清凉的水珠泼洒进下面的水池，单是那水声便令这幽居满生凉意。桌椅疏落有致地摆开，院子另一头几扇宽门尽数敞着，门内是一间幽暗的长屋，街市的喧嚣至此已化作遥远的低语。屋内一两个男人坐在桌边，一边写字一边啜饮，庭院里却空无一人。

===Original===
“You see, we shall be quiet,” said Dyson. “Pray sit down here, Mr.—?”

“Wilkins. My name is Henry Wilkins.”

“Sit here, Mr. Wilkins. I think you will find that a comfortable seat. I suppose you have not been here before? This is the quiet time; the place will be like a hive at six o’clock, and the chairs and tables will overflow into that little alley there.”

===Chinese===
“您瞧，咱们清静了，”戴森说，“请这边坐，——先生贵姓？”

“威尔金斯。在下亨利·威尔金斯。”

“请坐，威尔金斯先生。我看这位置很舒服。您从前没来过吧？眼下正是清静时辰；一到六点，这里便蜂巢一般热闹，桌椅还要漫到那边小巷子里去。”

===Original===
A waiter came in response to the bell; and after Dyson had politely inquired after the health of M. Annibault, the proprietor, he ordered a bottle of the wine of Champigny.

“The wine of Champigny,” he observed to Mr. Wilkins, who was evidently a good deal composed by the influence of the place, “is a Tourainian wine of great merit. Ah, here it is; let me fill your glass. How do you find it?”

===Chinese===
铃响处来了侍者；戴森客客气气地问候过店主阿尼博先生（M. Annibault）的贵体，便要了一瓶尚皮尼葡萄酒（Champigny）。

“这尚皮尼酒，”他对威尔金斯先生说——此地此景显然已令他安定了不少——“是都兰名酿，大有可取。啊，来了；让我给您斟上。您品品如何？”

===Original===
“Indeed,” said Mr. Wilkins, “I should have pronounced it a fine Burgundy. The bouquet is very exquisite. I am fortunate in lighting upon such a good Samaritan as yourself. I wonder you did not think me mad. But if you knew the terrors that assailed me, I am sure you would no longer be surprised at conduct which was certainly most unjustifiable.”

He sipped his wine, and leant back in his chair, relishing the drip and trickle of the fountain, and the cool greenness that hedged in this little port of refuge.

“Yes,” he said at last, “that is indeed an admirable wine. Thank you; you will allow me to offer you another bottle?”

The waiter was summoned, and descended through a trapdoor in the floor of the dark apartment, and brought up the wine. Mr. Wilkins lit a cigarette, and Dyson pulled out his pipe.

===Chinese===
“说实话，”威尔金斯先生说，“我竟要断定它是一瓶上好的勃艮第。这酒香精妙极了。我有幸遇见您这样一位乐善好施的善人，真是造化。难得您不曾把我当作疯子。可您若是知道方才袭上我心头的种种恐怖，我相信您对我那番无可宽宥的行径，就再也不会诧异了。”

他呷着酒，向椅背上一仰，品味着喷泉的珠滴与潺潺，品味着环拱着这一小片避风港湾的清凉绿意。

“是啊，”他终于说，“这确是一瓶可敬的酒。谢谢您；容我再叫一瓶？”

侍者又唤了来，从那间幽暗屋子的地板活门里下去，把酒取了上来。威尔金斯先生点上一支纸烟，戴森则掏出了他的烟斗。

===Original===
“Now,” said Mr. Wilkins, “I promised to give you an explanation of my strange behavior. It is rather a long story, but I see, sir, that you are no mere cold observer of the ebb and flow of life. You take, I think, a warm and an intelligent interest in the chances of your fellow-creatures, and I believe you will find what I have to tell not devoid of interest.”

Mr. Dyson signified his assent to these propositions, and though he thought Mr. Wilkins’s diction a little pompous, prepared to interest himself in his tale. The other, who had so raged with passion half an hour before, was now perfectly cool, and when he had smoked out his cigarette, he began in an even voice to relate the

===Chinese===
“言归正传，”威尔金斯先生说，“我答应过要向您解释我那奇怪的行径。这故事说来话长；但我看得出，先生，您绝非那种冷眼旁观人生潮汐之人。您对同类的际遇，怀着一副热心肠与聪明的兴味；我相信，我所要奉告的事，您听了不至于觉得无趣。”

戴森先生对这几句考语一一表示领教；他虽觉得威尔金斯先生的辞令未免有点浮夸，仍旧预备好生听他的故事。这位半个钟头前还怒不可遏的人物，此刻已冷静至极；一支纸烟燃尽，他便用平稳的嗓音，讲起了——

### Novel of the Dark Valley / 黑谷故事

===Original===
I am the son of a poor but learned clergyman in the West of England—but I am forgetting, these details are not of special interest. I will briefly state, then, that my father, who was, as I have said, a learned man, had never learnt the specious arts by which the great are flattered, and would never condescend to the despicable pursuit of self-advertisement. Though his fondness for ancient ceremonies and quaint customs, combined with a kindness of heart that was unequalled and a primitive and fervent piety, endeared him to his moorland parishioners, such were not the steps by which clergy then rose in the Church, and at sixty my father was still incumbent of the little benefice he had accepted in his thirtieth year. The income of the living was barely sufficient to support life in the decencies which are expected of the Anglican parson; and when my father died a few years ago, I, his only child, found myself thrown upon the world with a slender capital of less than a hundred pounds, and all the problem of existence before me. I felt that there was nothing for me to do in the country, and as usually happens in such cases, London drew me like a magnet. One day in August, in the early morning, while the dew still glittered on the turf, and on the high green banks of the lane, a neighbor drove me to the railway station, and I bade goodbye to the land of the broad moors and unearthly battlements of the wild tors. It was six o’clock as we neared London; the faint sickly fume of the brickfields about Acton came in puffs through the open window, and a mist was rising from the ground. Presently the brief view of successive streets, prim and uniform, struck me with a sense of monotony; the hot air seemed to grow hotter; and when we had rolled beneath the dismal and squalid houses, whose dirty and neglected back yards border the line near Paddington, I felt as if I should be stifled in this fainting breath of London. I got a hansom and drove off, and every street increased my gloom; gray houses with blinds drawn down, whole thoroughfares almost desolate, and the foot-passengers who seemed to stagger wearily along rather than walk, all made me feel a sinking at heart. I put up for the night at a small hotel in a street leading from the Strand, where my father had stayed on his few brief visits to town; and when I went out after dinner, the real gayety and bustle of the Strand and Fleet Street could cheer me but little, for in all this great city there was no single human being whom I could claim even as an acquaintance. I will not weary you with the history of the next year, for the adventures of a man who sinks are too trite to be worth recalling. My money did not last me long; I found that I must be neatly dressed, or no one to whom I applied would so much as listen to me; and I must live in a street of decent reputation if I wished to be treated with common civility. I applied for various posts, for which, as I now see, I was completely devoid of qualification; I tried to become a clerk without having the smallest notion of business habits, and I found, to my cost, that a general knowledge of literature and an execrable style of penmanship are far from being looked upon with favor in commercial circles. I had read one of the most charming of the works of a famous novelist of the present day, and I frequented the Fleet Street taverns in the hope of making literary friends, and so getting the introductions which I understood were indispensable in the career of letters. I was disappointed; I once or twice ventured to address gentlemen who were sitting in adjoining boxes, and I was answered, politely indeed, but in a manner that told me my advances were unusual. Pound by pound, my small resources melted; I could no longer think of appearances; I migrated to a shy quarter, and my meals became mere observances. I went out at one and returned to my room at two, but nothing but a milk-cake had occurred in the interval. In short, I became acquainted with misfortune; and as I sat amidst slush and ice on a seat in Hyde Park, munching a piece of bread, I realized the bitterness of poverty, and the feelings of a gentleman reduced to something far below the condition of a vagrant. In spite of all discouragement I did not desist in my efforts to earn a living. I consulted advertisement columns, I kept my eyes open for a chance, I looked in at the windows of stationers’ shops, but all in vain. One evening I was sitting in a Free Library, and I saw an advertisement in one of the papers. It was something like this: “Wanted, by a gentleman a person of literary taste and abilities as secretary and amanuensis. Must not object to travel.” Of course I knew that such an advertisement would have answers by the hundred, and I thought my own chances of securing the post extremely small; however, I applied at the address given, and wrote to Mr. Smith, who was staying at a large hotel at the West End. I must confess that my heart gave a jump when I received a note a couple of days later, asking me to call at the Cosmopole at my earliest convenience. I do not know, sir, what your experiences of life may have been, and so I cannot tell whether you have known such moments. A slight sickness, my heart beating rather more rapidly than usual, a choking in the throat, and a difficulty of utterance; such were my sensations as I walked to the Cosmopole. I had to mention the name twice before the hall porter could understand me, and as I went upstairs my hands were wet. I was a good deal struck by Mr. Smith’s appearance; he looked younger than I did, and there was something mild and hesitating about his expression. He was reading when I came in, and he looked up when I gave my name. “My dear sir,” he said, “I am really delighted to see you. I have read very carefully the letter you were good enough to send me. Am I to understand that this document is in your own handwriting?” He showed me the letter I had written, and I told him I was not so fortunate as to be able to keep a secretary myself. “Then, sir,” he went on, “the post I advertised is at your service. You have no objection to travel, I presume?” As you may imagine, I closed pretty eagerly with the offer he made, and thus I entered the service of Mr. Smith. For the first few weeks I had no special duties; I had received a quarter’s salary, and a handsome allowance was made me in lieu of board and lodging. One morning, however, when I called at the hotel according to instructions, my master informed me that I must hold myself in readiness for a sea-voyage, and, to spare unnecessary detail, in the course of a fortnight we had landed at New York. Mr. Smith told me that he was engaged on a work of a special nature, in the compilation of which some peculiar researches had to be made; in short, I was given to understand that we were to travel to the far West.

===Chinese===
我是英格兰西部一位贫穷而博学的牧师之子——啊，我差点忘了，这些细枝末节未必特别有趣。那就长话短说：家父正如我所说，是个有学问的人，却始终不曾学会那些奉承权贵的虚浮伎俩，更绝不肯屈尊去干自我标榜那种卑劣的营生。他虽因酷爱古礼与奇俗，加之举世无双的仁心与一种质朴而炽热的虔敬，深得荒原教区教民的爱戴；但那样的品性，终究不是当年教会上进的阶梯，六十岁上，家父仍守着他而立之年领受的那份小小圣俸。那圣俸的收入，仅够维持一位英国国教牧师体面度日；几年前家父谢世，我——他的独子——便被抛到人海之中，手头不足一百镑的微薄本钱，整个人生的难题横在眼前。我自觉在乡间无可作为，而这类情形的常例，便是伦敦如磁石般把我吸了去。八月里的一天清晨，露水还在草皮上、在小巷高高的青绿土坡上闪着光，一位邻居驾车送我到火车站，我便告别了那片旷莽的荒原，和野岭危岩上恍非人世的雉堞。将近六点钟，我们驶近伦敦；阿克顿一带砖窑场上淡淡的、令人作呕的烟气，一阵阵从敞开的车窗里灌进来，地面正腾起薄雾。不多时，一街又一街刻板划一的房舍接连掠过眼前，那单调令我悚然；热空气仿佛愈来愈热；待到火车钻过帕丁顿附近沿线那些阴郁破败的屋子——只见肮脏荒芜的后院紧贴着铁路——我只觉自己要在这伦敦昏晕的呼吸里窒息了。我雇了一辆双轮马车驶去，一条街又一条街，只添我心中的阴郁：灰房子垂着窗帘，整条通衢几乎荒凉无人，行人仿佛不是在走路，而是困顿蹒跚地挪；这一切都教我心头一沉。当夜我在一条从斯特兰德街岔出的小街上的一家小旅馆落脚——家父往日几度短暂进京，宿的便是这一家；晚饭后我出门走走，斯特兰德街与舰队街真正的繁华热闹也几乎宽慰不了我，因为在这座大都会里，没有一个人是我能认作相识的。此后一年的经历，恕我不再絮叨；一个步步下沉之人的遭遇，无非老生常谈，不值一提。我的钱没有撑多久；我发现自己必须衣冠楚楚，否则我求职的对象连听都不肯听我一句；我还必须住在名声体面的街上，人家才肯以寻常的礼数待我。我谋过各式差事——如今看来，那些差事我全然不具备资格；我想去当一名文书，却对商界的规矩一窍不通；我还吃了苦头才明白：文学上的泛泛之知，加一手鬼画符似的字，在商界眼里远远算不得青睐。我读过当今一位名小说家最迷人的作品之一，便常去舰队街的酒馆里盘桓，指望结识文坛的朋友，好讨得那些引荐——我听说那是在文字这行里不可或缺的东西。我失望了；我一两次斗胆向邻座的先生们搭话，承蒙他们客气作答，只是那态度分明告诉我，我的殷勤唐突了。一镑又一镑，我那点微薄的家底融化了；我再也顾不得体面，迁到一个僻陋的地段，一日三餐成了聊备一格的仪式。我一点钟出门，两点钟回房，这中间发生的，不过是一块奶糕。总而言之，我与厄运相识了；当我坐在海德公园的雪水泥泞里啃一块面包时，我尝到了贫穷的苦味，尝到了一位绅士沦落到远不如流浪汉的境地时是什么心情。尽管处处碰壁，我谋生的努力却不曾停歇。我翻检报上的广告栏，眼观六路寻个机会，一家家文具店橱窗里张望，全归徒劳。一天傍晚，我坐在一家免费图书馆里，在一份报纸上看见一则广告，大意如此："今聘：一位绅士诚延具有文学趣味与才具者一名，任秘书兼笔录员。须不拒远行。"我自然知道，这样一则广告保准有上百人应征，自觉得聘的希望微乎其微；然而我还是按广告上的地址去了函，写给下榻西区一家大旅馆的史密斯先生。不瞒您说，两天后接获一纸短简，请我尽早过访科斯莫波尔饭店（the Cosmopole），我的心当时便是一跳。先生，我不知道您的阅历如何，故而猜不出您可曾尝过那样的时刻：些微的恶心，心跳得比平常快些，喉头哽塞，出语艰难——这便是我走向科斯莫波尔饭店时的滋味。我报了两次名字，门房才听明白；上楼时，我的两手全是汗。史密斯先生的样子叫我暗暗吃惊：他看上去比我还年轻，神色之间有一种温软与迟疑。我进去时他正在读书，听见我通名，便抬起头来。"亲爱的先生，"他说，"见到您实在高兴。您惠赐的那封信，我已经仔细读过了。我可以理解为，这封手札是您亲笔所书么？"他把我的信递给我看，我答说，我还没有那份福气，自己雇得起一位秘书。"那么，先生，"他接着说，"我登报招聘的职位，便听凭您效力了。您不反对远行吧？"您可以想见，我急切地把他开的条件应承了下来；就这样，我进了史密斯先生的门下。最初几个星期我并无专门职守；我预支了一个季度的薪金，另发我一笔可观的津贴，以抵膳宿。然而一天早晨，我按吩咐到旅馆去见雇主，他通知我说，我须有出海远航的准备；不烦细述——不出两个星期，我们已在纽约登岸。史密斯先生告诉我，他正从事一部性质特殊的著作，编纂之中须做若干异乎寻常的考察；简而言之，我得到的话风是：我们将远赴西部。

===Original===
After about a week had been spent in New York we took our seats in the cars, and began a journey tedious beyond all conception. Day after day, and night after night, the great train rolled on, threading its way through cities the very names of which were strange to me, passing at slow speed over perilous viaducts, skirting mountain ranges and pine forests, and plunging into dense tracts of wood, where mile after mile and hour after hour the same monotonous growth of brushwood met the eye, and all along the continual clatter and rattle of the wheels upon the ill-laid lines made it difficult to hear the voices of our fellow-passengers. We were a heterogeneous and ever-changing company; often I woke up in the dead of night with the sudden grinding jar of the brakes, and looking out found that we had stopped in the shabby street of some frame-built town, lighted chiefly by the flaring windows of the saloon. A few rough-looking fellows would often come out to stare at the cars, and sometimes passengers got down, and sometimes there was a party of two or three waiting on the wooden sidewalk to get on board. Many of the passengers were English; humble households torn up from the moorings of a thousand years, and bound for some problematical paradise in the alkali desert or the Rockies. I heard the men talking to one another of the great profits to be made on the virgin soil of America, and two or three, who were mechanics, expatiated on the wonderful wages given to skilled labor on the railways and in the factories of the States. This talk usually fell dead after a few minutes, and I could see a sickness and dismay in the faces of these men as they looked at the ugly brush or at the desolate expanse of the prairie, dotted here and there with frame-houses, devoid of garden, or flowers or trees, standing all alone in what might have been a great gray sea frozen into stillness. Day after day the waving sky line, and the desolation of a land without form or color or variety, appalled the hearts of such of us as were Englishmen, and once in the night as I lay awake I heard a woman weeping and sobbing, and asking what she had done to come to such a place. Her husband tried to comfort her in the broad speech of Gloucestershire, telling her the ground was so rich that one had only to plough it up and it would grow sunflowers of itself, but she cried for her mother and their old cottage and the beehives, like a little child. The sadness of it all overwhelmed me, and I had no heart to think of other matters; the question of what Mr. Smith could have to do in such a country, and of what manner of literary research could be carried on in the wilderness, hardly troubled me. Now and again my situation struck me as peculiar; I had been engaged as a literary assistant at a handsome salary, and yet my master was still almost a stranger to me; sometimes he would come to where I was sitting in the cars and make a few banal remarks about the country, but for the most part of the journey he sat by himself, not speaking to anyone, and so far as I could judge, deep in his thoughts. It was I think on the fifth day from New York when I received the intimation that we should shortly leave the cars; I had been watching some distant mountains which rose wild and savage before us, and I was wondering if there were human beings so unhappy as to speak of home in connection with those piles of lumbered rock, when Mr. Smith touched me lightly on the shoulder. “You will be glad to be done with the cars, I have no doubt, Mr. Wilkins,” he said. “You were looking at the mountains, I think? Well, I hope we shall be there tonight. The train stops at Reading, and I dare say we shall manage to find our way.”

===Chinese===
在纽约盘桓了约莫一星期，我们便上了火车，开始一段沉闷得超乎一切想象的旅程。日复一日，夜复一夜，那长大的列车滚滚向前，穿行在一座座连名字于我都陌生的城市之间，以缓慢的速度驶过险峻的高架桥，绕着山脉与松林蜿蜒，一头扎进密匝匝的林地——在那里面，一英里又一英里、一个钟头又一个钟头，扑入眼帘的永远是同样单调的灌木；车轮碾过铺得不善的铁轨，那经久不息的哐当轰响，吵得人连同车旅伴的话音也听不真切。我们这一车，是怎样一群驳杂不居的旅伴；我常在夜半被刹车的猛然一顿震醒，往外一望，只见火车停在某座木板小镇寒伧的街上，照亮那镇子的，主要是酒馆里火光熊熊的窗子。常有几个相貌粗横的家伙出来盯着火车瞧，时有乘客下车，也时有两三个人候在木板人行道上预备登车。乘客里有不少英国人：一户户卑微的人家，从千年泊地的锚链上被生生拔起，奔向碱滩荒漠或落基山（Rockies）间某个渺茫难凭的天堂。我听见男人们彼此谈论美洲处女地上可图的大利，有两三个匠人还就美国铁路与工厂里给熟练工人的惊人工钱大发议论。这类谈话不出几分钟便冷场；我看得见这些人望着丑陋的灌木、望着荒凉大草原时脸上的恹恹与惶惑——草原上稀稀落落点缀着木板房，没有园圃，没有花，也没有树，孤零零立在一片仿佛冻结成死寂的灰色大海之中。日复一日，那起伏摇曳的天际线，那片无形状、无色彩、无变化的荒凉，慑住了我们这辈英国人的心；一天夜里我醒着躺在铺上，听见一个女人哭泣呜咽，问自己造了什么孽，要落到这般地方。她的丈夫用格洛斯特郡宽腔大调的土话安慰她，说这地土肥得流油，只消犁上一犁，向日葵自己就长出来了；她却像个小孩子似的，哭着要妈妈，要他们的老屋，要那几箱蜂。这一切的悲愁将我压倒，我再无心思想别的事；史密斯先生到这样的地方来能有什么事，在这莽莽荒野里又能做什么样的文学考察——这类疑问几乎全不曾搅扰我。只是偶尔，我的处境会显得离奇：我以可观的薪水受聘为文学助手，雇主于我却仍近乎陌生人；有时他会走到我在车厢里坐的地方，就乡野讲几句不着边际的闲话，但大半旅程他总独自坐着，不同任何人交谈，据我判断，是深深浸在自己的思绪里。我想是从纽约起程的第五天，我得到知会，说我们不久便要下车了；我一直望着远处一片荒蛮险恶、迎面拔起的群山，正自忖度，世上可有不幸到把"家"字同这堆堆乱石相联的人类，史密斯先生在我肩上轻轻一触。"您一定巴不得快点熬完这趟车了吧，这我毫不怀疑，威尔金斯先生，"他说，"您方才在看那些山，是吧？呃，但愿今夜我们便能到得那里。火车在雷丁（Reading）停，我想咱们总能寻着路。"

===Original===
A few hours later the brakeman brought the tram to a standstill at the Reading depot and we got out. I noticed that the town, though of course built almost entirely of frame-houses, was larger and busier than any we had passed for the last two days. The depot was crowded, and as the bell and whistle sounded, I saw that a number of persons were preparing to leave the cars, while an even greater number were waiting to get on board. Besides the passengers, there was a pretty dense crowd of people, some of whom had come to meet or to see off their friends and relatives, while others were mere loafers. Several of our English fellow passengers got down at Reading, but the confusion was so great that they were lost to my sight almost immediately. Mr. Smith beckoned to me to follow him, and we were soon in the thick of the mass; and the continual ringing of bells, the hubbub of voices, the shrieking of whistles, and the hiss of escaping steam, confused my senses, and I wondered dimly as I struggled after my employer, where we were going, and how we should be able to find our way through an unknown country. Mr. Smith had put on a wide-brimmed hat, which he had sloped over his eyes, and as all the men wore hats of the same pattern, it was with some difficulty that I distinguished him in the crowd. We got free at last, and he struck down a side street, and made one or two sharp turns to right and left. It was getting dusk, and we seemed to be passing through a shy portion of the town, there were few people about in the ill-lighted streets, and these few were men of the most unprepossessing pattern. Suddenly we stopped before a corner house, a man was standing at the door, apparently on the lookout for someone, and I noticed that he and Smith gave sharp glances one to the other.

===Chinese===
几个钟头后，司闸员把火车停在雷丁的站上，我们下了车。我留意到，这座镇子虽也几乎全由木板房构成，却比我们两天来经过的任何一处都更大、更热闹。站上人头攒动；铃响笛鸣之间，我看见一批乘客预备下车，等着上车的为数更多。除旅客之外，还密密匝匝围着一大群人：有来迎送亲友的，也有纯属闲汉的。我们同车的几位英国旅伴在雷丁下了车，无奈人太杂乱，转眼便不见了踪影。史密斯先生招手让我跟他走，我们很快便陷进人堆当中；铃声不绝，人语嘈嘈，汽笛尖啸，蒸汽嘶嘶，搅得我神志昏乱；我在雇主身后跌跌撞撞地挤着，心里昏昏昧昧地纳罕：我们要往哪里去？在一片生疏的地界里，又怎寻得出路？史密斯先生戴上一顶宽檐帽，压得遮住了眼睛；此地男人又一律戴同样式样的帽子，我在人群里好一番工夫才把他辨认出来。我们终于挤出重围，他一头扎进一条小街，又利落地左右拐了两三个弯。天色渐暝，我们走的似乎已是镇上僻陋的一角；路灯昏沉的街上人迹寥寥，仅有的几个，也尽是面目最不讨喜之辈。忽然，我们在一座街角房前停下；一个男人立在门口，显然在守望什么人，我注意到他与史密斯交换了迅捷的眼色。

===Original===
“From New York City, I expect, mister?”

“From New York!”

“All right; they’re ready, and you can have ’em when you choose. I know my orders, you see, and I mean to run this business through.”

“Very well, Mr. Evans, that is what we want. Our money is good, you know. Bring them round.”

===Chinese===
"是打纽约城来的吧，先生？"

"正是从纽约来！"

"好咧；东西都齐了，您随时来取。您瞧，我是得了吩咐的，这桩买卖我打算一办到底。"

"很好，埃文斯先生（Evans），我们要的正是这个。不瞒您说，我们的钱是响当当的。把它们牵过来吧。"

===Original===
I had stood silent, listening to this dialogue, and wondering what it meant. Smith began to walk impatiently up and down the street, and the man Evans was still standing at his door. He had given a sharp whistle, and I saw him looking me over in a quiet leisurely way, as if to make sure of my face for another time. I was thinking what all this could mean, when an ugly, slouching lad came up a side passage, leading two rawboned horses.

“Get up, Mr. Wilkins, and be quick about it,” said Smith. “We ought to be on our way.”

===Chinese===
我一言不发地立在一旁，听着这段对话，心里揣不透它的意思。史密斯在街上来回踱步，显得很不耐烦；那叫埃文斯的男人仍立在自家门口。他打了一声呼哨，我看见他不慌不忙、从容不迫地把我从头到脚打量了一遍，仿佛要把我的相貌记牢，留待下回。我正自寻思这一切究竟是什么名堂，一个面目凶顽、吊儿郎当的少年从旁边一条夹道里牵出两匹瘦骨嶙峋的马。

"上马吧，威尔金斯先生，麻利点儿，"史密斯说，"咱们该上路了。"

===Original===
We rode off together into the gathering darkness, and before long I looked back and saw the far plain behind us, with the lights of the town glimmering faintly; and in front rose the mountains. Smith guided his horse on the rough track as surely as if he had been riding along Piccadilly, and I followed him as well as I could. I was weary and exhausted, and scarcely took note of anything; I felt that the track was a gradual ascent, and here and there I saw great boulders by the road. The ride made but little impression on me; I have a faint recollection of passing through a dense black pine forest, where our horses had to pick their way among the rocks, and I remember the peculiar effect of the rarefied air as we kept still mounting higher and higher. I think I must have been half asleep for the latter half of the ride, and it was with a shock that I heard Smith saying—

“Here we are, Wilkins. This is Blue-Rock Park. You will enjoy the view tomorrow. Tonight we will have something to eat, and then go to bed.”

===Chinese===
我们并辔驰进愈聚愈浓的夜色里；不多时我回头一望，身后远野苍茫，镇上的灯火隐约闪烁；前方则群山拔起。史密斯在崎岖的径上控马而行，稳当得如同驰骋在皮卡迪利（Piccadilly）大街上；我竭力紧随着他。我疲惫已极，几乎万念昏沉，只依稀觉出道路渐渐上坡，沿途不时见到巨石累累。这一程骑旅在我心上没留下多少印记；我只依稀记得穿过一片漆黑茂密的松林，马匹须在乱石间择路而行；还记得愈攀愈高时，那稀薄空气给人的奇异感觉。后半程我想必是半睡半醒，待听得史密斯说话，不由一个激灵——

"到了，威尔金斯。这儿就是蓝岩园（Blue-Rock Park）。明儿您有风景好赏。今夜咱们吃点东西，就睡觉。"

===Original===
A man came out of a rough-looking house and took the horses, and we found some fried steak and coarse whiskey awaiting us inside. I had come to a strange place. There were three rooms—the room in which we had supper, Smith’s room and my own. The deaf old man who did the work slept in a sort of shed, and when I woke up the next morning and walked out I found that the house stood in a sort of hollow amongst the mountains; the clumps of pines and some enormous bluish-gray rocks that stood here and there between the trees had given the place the name of Blue-Rock Park. On every side the snow-covered mountains surrounded us, the breath of the air was as wine, and when I climbed the slope and looked down, I could see that, so far as any human fellowship was concerned I might as well have been wrecked on some small island in mid-Pacific. The only trace of man I could see was the rough log-house where I had slept, and in my ignorance I did not know that there were similar houses within comparatively easy distance, as distance is reckoned in the Rockies. But at the moment, the utter, dreadful loneliness rushed upon me, and the thought of the great plain and the great sea that parted me from the world I knew, caught me by the throat, and I wondered if I should die there in that mountain hollow. It was a terrible instant, and I have not yet forgotten it. Of course I managed to conquer my horror; I said I should be all the stronger for the experience, and I made up my mind to make the best of everything. It was a rough life enough, and rough enough board and lodging. I was left entirely to myself. Smith I scarcely ever saw, nor did I know when he was in the house. I have often thought he was far away, and have been surprised to see him walking out of his room, locking the door behind him and putting the key in his pocket; and on several occasions when I fancied he was busy in his room, I have seen him come in with his boots covered with dust and dirt. So far as work went I enjoyed a complete sinecure; I had nothing to do but to walk about the valley, to eat, and to sleep. With one thing and another I grew accustomed, to the life, and managed to make myself pretty comfortable, and by degrees I began to venture farther away from the house, and to explore the country. One day I had contrived to get into a neighboring valley, and suddenly I came upon a group of men sawing timber. I went up to them, hoping that perhaps some of them might be Englishmen; at all events they were human beings, and I should hear articulate speech, for the old man I have mentioned, besides being half blind and stone deaf, was wholly dumb so far as I was concerned. I was prepared to be welcomed in a rough and ready fashion, without much of the forms of politeness, but the grim glances and the short gruff answers I received astonished me. I saw the men glancing oddly at each other, and one of them who had stopped work began fingering a gun, and I was obliged to return on my path uttering curses on the fate which had brought me into a land where men were more brutish than the very brutes. The solitude of the life began to oppress me as with a nightmare, and a few days later I determined to walk to a kind of station some miles distant, where a rough inn was kept for the accommodation of hunters and tourists. English gentlemen occasionally stopped there for the night, and I thought I might perhaps fall in with someone of better manners than the inhabitants of the country. I found as I had expected a group of men lounging about the door of the log-house that served as a hotel, and as I came nearer I could see that heads were put together and looks interchanged, and when I walked up the six or seven trappers stared at me in stony ferocity, and with something of the disgust that one eyes a loathsome and venomous snake. I felt that I could bear it no longer, and I called out:—

===Chinese===
一个人从一所粗陋的房子里出来把马牵了去，屋内已有煎牛排和劣质威士忌等着我们。我来到了一个奇怪的地方。房子共三间——我们吃晚饭的这间、史密斯的房间和我的一间。做活的那个聋老头睡在一间棚屋似的披屋里。第二天早晨我醒来走出屋去，才发现这房子坐落在群山环抱的一片洼地里；一丛丛松树，和散立林间、青灰泛蓝的巨大岩块，给了这地方"蓝岩园"之名。四面八方，积雪的山峰将我们围在当中，空气的气息醇厚如酒；我爬上坡顶俯瞰，心中明白：就人间的往来而论，我不啻是搁浅在太平洋正中一座小岛上。目力所及，人迹只有我过夜的那所粗陋木屋；我孤陋寡闻，并不知道照落基山的里程算，比这近便得多的地方还有同样的住家。可是在那一刻，那彻底的、骇人的孤独劈头压了下来；一想起隔着我和旧日世界的那片大平原、那片大海，喉咙便被扼住；我不禁自问，我会不会就死在这山的洼地里。那真是可怕的一刻，至今我未能忘怀。当然，我终究镇住了我的恐怖；我对自己说，经此一遭，我反该更强壮些；我打定主意，凡事往好里将就。这生活够粗粝的了，膳宿也够粗粝的了。我全然被打发给了自己。史密斯我几乎从不照面，连他在不在屋里也不知道。我常以为他远在百里之外，却冷不丁看见他从自己房里踱出来，随手锁门，把钥匙揣进兜里；又有几回，我还道他在屋里忙着，却眼见他走进来，靴子上蒙满尘土。至于职守，我端的是一份十足的闲差；除了在谷中走走、吃饭、睡觉，我无所事事。这样那样，我渐渐惯于这种生活，也把自己的日子调理得颇为安适，并且渐渐敢于走得离屋子更远，去踏勘这一带的乡野。有一天，我设法翻进了邻谷，蓦地撞见一群人在锯木材。我朝他们走去，指望其中或有英国人；退一步说，他们总是人，我总听得着清晰的人言——我提过的那老头，除了半瞎兼全聋之外，于我更是十足的哑巴。我原本预备受一顿粗直爽利的招呼，客套是不会多的；可我得到的那几道阴森的目光和几句生硬简短的回话，却叫我愕然。我看见那些人彼此使着眼色，其中一个停了活计，手指在一支枪上摩挲起来；我只得循原路退回，一路咒骂那把我带进这片国土的命运——在这里，人比畜生还要畜生。这生活的孤寂开始像梦魇一般压住我；几天之后，我决意步行到几英里外一处驿站模样的地方，那里开着一家人称客栈的粗陋屋子，以供猎户与游人歇脚。英国绅士偶尔也在那里过夜，我想我或许能遇上一位比此地居民更有教养的人。到得那里，果然如我所料，一群人正懒懒地聚在那充作旅馆的木屋门口；我走近时，看得见他们交头接耳、彼此递着眼色；待我走到跟前，六七个捕兽人便以石块般的凶蛮瞪视我，还带着几分厌恶，像看一条既可憎又歹毒的蛇。我觉得再难忍受，便扬声喊道：——

===Original===
“Is there such a thing as an Englishman here, or anyone with a little civilization?”

One of the men put his hand to his belt, but his neighbor checked him and answered me.

“You’ll find we’ve got some of the resources of civilization before very long, mister, and I expect you’ll not fancy them extremely. But anyway, there’s an Englishman tarrying here, and I’ve no doubt he’ll be glad to see you. There you are, that’s Mr. D’Aubernoun.”

===Chinese===
"此地可有英国人么？或者随便哪一位，稍稍沾点儿教化的？"

人群中一个把手按向了腰带，被他旁边的拦住，那人回答了我。

"不消多时，你自会领教我们这儿几样教化的家伙什，先生；我料你也未必怎么喜欢它们。不过不管怎么说，这儿倒真逗留着一个英国人，我毫不怀疑，他见到你会高兴的。呶，那位就是达伯农先生（D'Aubernoun）。"

===Original===
A young man, dressed like an English country squire, came and stood at the door, and looked at me. One of the men pointed to me and said:—

“That’s the individual we were talking about last night. Thought you might like to have a look at him, squire, and here he is.”

The young fellow’s good-natured English face clouded over, and he glanced sternly at me, and turned away with a gesture of contempt and aversion.

===Chinese===
一个衣着如同英国乡绅的年轻人走到门口站定，望着我。人群中一个指了指我，说：——

"这就是咱们昨儿夜里说起的那个家伙。想着您兴许乐意瞧他一眼，老爷，喏，人在这儿呢。"

那青年一副英国人的厚道面孔阴沉下来，他严厉地扫了我一眼，带着轻蔑与嫌恶的神色转过身去。

===Original===
“Sir,” I cried, “I do not know what I have done to be treated in this manner. You are my fellow-countryman, and I expected some courtesy.”

He gave me a black look and made as if he would go in, but he changed his mind, and faced me.

===Chinese===
"先生，"我喊道，"我不知道自己做了什么，要受这般对待。您是我的同胞，我还指望您念几分同乡之谊呢。"

他恶狠狠地瞪了我一眼，作势便要进门，却又转念，回身正对着我。

===Original===
“You are rather imprudent, I think, to behave in this manner. You must be counting on a forbearance which cannot last very long; which may last a very short time, indeed. And let me tell you this, sir, you may call yourself an Englishman and drag the name of England through the dirt, but you need not count on any English influence to help you. If I were you, I would not stay here much longer.”

===Chinese===
"我看，你还这般的行止，未免太不知死活。你必是指望着人家容让你；可这份容让决计长不了；说实在的，兴许转眼便完。还有一句话奉告，先生：你尽可以自称英国人，把英格兰的名字拖进泥里，可休想指望什么英国人的面子来搭救你。我要是你，就不在这儿多待了。"

===Original===
He went into the inn, and the men quietly watched my face, as I stood there, wondering whether I was going mad. The woman of the house came out and stared at me as if I were a wild beast or a savage, and I turned to her, and spoke quietly.

“I am very hungry and thirsty, I have walked a long way. I have plenty of money. Will you give me something to eat and drink?”

“No, I won’t,” she said. “You had better quit this.”

===Chinese===
他进了客栈；我立在那里，疑心自己是不是要疯了，那些人只静静窥着我脸上的神色。老板娘走出来，直盯盯地瞧我，仿佛我是一头野兽、一个野人；我转向她，平静地开口。

"我又饥又渴，走了很远的路。我有的是钱。您肯给我一点吃的喝的么？"

"不给，"她说，"你顶好趁早滚。"

===Original===
I crawled home like a wounded beast, and lay down on my bed. It was all a hopeless puzzle to me. I knew nothing but rage and shame and terror, and I suffered little more when I passed by a house in an adjacent valley, and some children who were playing outside ran from me shrieking. I was forced to walk to find some occupation. I should have died if I had sat down quietly in Blue Rock Park and looked all day at the mountains; but wherever I saw a human being I saw the same glance of hatred and aversion, and once as I was crossing a thick brake I heard a shot, and the venomous hiss of a bullet close to my ear.

===Chinese===
我像一头负伤的野兽爬回家去，倒在床上。这一切于我是一团无望的乱谜。我只识得狂怒、羞辱与恐怖；后来我打邻谷一户人家门前经过，在外头玩耍的几个孩子尖叫着从我身边逃开，我所受的也不过如此了。为了觅一件事分心，我不得不出去走动。倘若安安静静坐在蓝岩园里整日望山，我早就死了；可无论我在哪里见到人，见到的总是同一副憎恨与嫌恶的目光；有一回穿过一片茂密的灌木丛，我还听见一声枪响，一颗子弹贴着我的耳朵，恶毒地嘶啸而过。

===Original===
One day I heard a conversation which astounded me; I was sitting behind a rock resting, and two men came along the track and halted. One of them had got his feet entangled in some wild vines, and swore fiercely, but the other laughed, and said they were useful things sometimes.

“What the hell do you mean?”

“Oh, nothing much. But they’re uncommon tough, these here vines, and sometimes rope is skerse and dear.”

The man who had sworn chuckled at this, and I heard them sit down and light their pipes.

===Chinese===
有一天，我听见一段叫我骇然的谈话；我正坐在一块岩石后歇脚，两个人顺着小径走来，站住了。其中一个叫野藤绊住了脚，破口大骂；另一个却笑了，说这东西有时倒有用处。

"你他娘的是什么意思？"

"哦，没什么。不过这藤可是结实得很，赶上绳子缺货又金贵的时候，就派上用场了。"

那骂人的听了嘿嘿一笑；我听见他们坐下，点着了烟斗。

===Original===
“Have you seen him lately?” asked the humorist.

“I sighted him the other day, but the darned bullet went high. He’s got his master’s luck, I expect, sir, but it can’t last much longer. You heard about him going to Jinks’s and trying his brass, but the young Britisher downed him pretty considerable, I can tell you.”

===Chinese===
"近来见过他么？"那位说笑话的问。

"前几天我瞄见他来着，可那颗该死的子弹打高了。我瞧他是沾了他家主子的运气，先生；不过这运气长不了。你听说他上金克斯那儿逞威风的事了吧？那个英国小伙子可结结实实把他收拾了一顿，这我告诉你。"

===Original===
“What the devil is the meaning of it?”

“I don’t know, but I believe it’ll have to be finished, and done in the old style, too. You know how they fix the niggers?”

“Yes, sir, I’ve seen a little of that. A couple of gallons of kerosene’ll cost a dollar at Brown’s store, but I should say it’s cheap anyway.”

===Chinese===
"这他妈到底是怎么一档子事？"

"不知道。不过我看这事早晚得有个了断，还得照老法子来。你知道他们是怎么收拾那些黑鬼的？"

"见过，先生，那场面我见识过一点儿。布朗店里两加仑煤油卖一块钱，可要我说，怎么算都便宜。"

===Original===
They moved off after this, and I lay still behind the rock, the sweat pouring down my face. I was so sick that I could barely stand, and I walked home as slowly as an old man, leaning on my stick. I knew that the two men had been talking about me, and I knew that some terrible death was in store for me. That night I could not sleep. I tossed on the rough bed and tortured myself to find out the meaning of it all. At last in the very dead of night I rose from the bed, and put on my clothes, and went out. I did not care where I went, but I felt that I must walk till I had tired myself out. It was a clear moonlight night, and in a couple of hours I found I was approaching a place of dismal reputation in the mountains, a deep cleft in the rocks, known as Black Gulf Canyon. Many years before, an unfortunate party of Englishmen and Englishwomen had camped here and had been surrounded by Indians. They were captured, outraged, and put to death with almost inconceivable tortures, and the roughest of the trappers or woodsmen gave the canyon a wide berth even in the daytime. As I crushed through the dense brushwood which grew above the canyon, I heard voices, and wondering who could be in such a place at such a time, I went on, walking more carefully and making as little noise as possible. There was a great tree growing on the very edge of the rocks, and I lay down and looked out from behind the trunk. Black Gulf Canyon was below me, the moonlight shining bright into its very depths from midheaven, and casting shadows as black as death from the pointed rock, and all the sheer rock on the other side, overhanging the canyon, was in darkness. At intervals a light veil obscured the moonlight, as a filmy cloud fleeted across the moon; and a bitter wind blew shrill across the gulf. I looked down as I have said, and saw twenty men standing in a semicircle round a rock; I counted them one by one, and knew most of them. They were the very vilest of the vile, more vile than any den in London could show, and there was murder and worse than murder on the heads of not a few. Facing them and me stood Mr. Smith with the rock before him, and on the rock was a great pair of scales, such as are used in the stores. I heard his voice ringing down the canyon as I lay beside the tree, and my heart turned cold as I heard it.

===Chinese===
说完这些，他们走远了；我伏在岩石后一动不动，汗水顺着脸直淌。我恶心得几乎立不住，拄着杖，像个老头似的挨回家去。我知道那两人说的正是我，也知道某种骇人的死法正在前头等我。那一夜我不能成眠。我在粗硬的床上辗转，苦苦拷问这一切究竟是什么意思。到了夜阑最深之时，我终于起身，穿上衣服，走了出去。去哪里我全不计较，只觉得非走到力竭不可。那是一个澄澈的月夜；走了两个来钟头，我发觉自己正走近山里一处凶名昭著的所在——岩间一道深深裂开的豁口，人称黑渊峡（Black Gulf Canyon）。多年以前，一队不幸的英国男女曾在此扎营，被印第安人团团围住；他们被掳、被凌辱，以几乎不可思议的酷刑处死；因此最粗豪的捕兽人、伐木人，连白昼也绕着这峡谷远远走。我拨开峡上密生的灌木丛，听见了人语；纳罕此时此地谁会在这里，我又往前走，脚下放轻，尽量不出声响。岩缘上正生着一株大树，我伏下身，从树干后面望出去。黑渊峡便在我脚下：中天的月光直照进峡底最深处，从尖岩投下的影子黑得如同死亡；对面那面悬临峡谷的峭壁，整个沉在黑暗里。时有薄纱似的一层遮暗月光，那是一缕浮云掠过月面；一阵凛冽的风呼啸着刮过深渊。我朝下望去，如我所说，看见二十个人站成半环，围着一方岩石；我一个一个数过去，大半都识得。他们全是恶中之恶，比伦敦任何黑窟能拿得出来的货色还要恶，其中不少人头上背着人命，还背着比杀人更重的罪孽。面对着他们、也面对着我，立着史密斯先生，身前便是那方岩石；岩上搁着一副大天平，就是铺子里称货用的那种。我伏在树旁，听见他的声音顺着峡谷朗朗传下；我一听，心便凉透了。

===Original===
“Life for gold,” he cried, “a life for gold. The blood and the life of an enemy for every pound of gold.”

A man stepped out and raised one hand, and with the other flung a bright lump of something into the pan of the scales, which clanged down, and Smith muttered something in his ear. Then he cried again:—

“Blood for gold; for a pound of gold, the life of an enemy. For every pound of gold upon the scales, a life.”

===Chinese===
"命换金子，"他高喊，"一条命换金子。每一磅金子，换一个仇人的血和性命。"

一个人跨出队列，举起一只手，另一只手把一块亮晶晶的什么东西抛进天平的秤盘；秤盘哐当一声沉了下去，史密斯凑在他耳边低语了几句。随后他又高喊：——

"血换金子；一磅金子，一条仇人的命。天平上每一磅金子，一条命。"

===Original===
One by one the men came forward, each lifting up his right hand; and the gold was weighed in the scales, and each time Smith leaned forward and spoke to each man in his ear. Then he cried again:—

“Desire and lust, for gold on the scales. For every pound of gold, enjoyment of desire.”

I saw the same thing happen as before; the uplifted hand, and the metal weighed, and the mouth whispering, and black passion on every face.

===Chinese===
人们一个接一个走上前来，个个举起右手；金子在天平上称过，每一回，史密斯都俯身向前，对着那人耳语。随后他又高喊：——

"欲念，换秤上的金子。每一磅金子，偿一场欲念的欢畅。"

我看见方才的情形重演：举起的手，称过的金子，喁喁低语的嘴，以及每一张脸上黑沉沉的激情。

===Original===
Then, one by one, I saw the men again step up to Smith. A muttered conversation seemed to take place; I could see that Smith was explaining and directing, and I noticed that he gesticulated a little as one who points out the way, and once or twice he moved his hands quickly as if he would show that the path was clear and could not be missed. I kept my eyes so intently on his figure that I noted little else, and at last it was with a start that I realized that the canyon was empty. A moment before I thought I had seen the group of villainous faces, and the two standing, a little apart by the rock; I had looked down a moment, and when I glanced again into the canyon there was no one there. In dumb terror I made my way home, and I fell asleep in an instant from exhaustion. No doubt I should have slept on for many hours, but when I woke up, the sun was only rising, and the light shone in on my bed. I had started up from sleep with the sensation of having received a violent shock, and as I looked in confusion about me I saw to my amazement that there were three men in the room. One of them had his hand on my shoulder and spoke to me.

===Chinese===
随后，我看见那些人又一个一个地走到史密斯跟前。似乎进行着一番喁喁的低谈；我看得出史密斯在讲解、在吩咐，还留意到他微微打着手势，像给人指点路径的样子，有一两回他的手快速地一挥，仿佛要表明道路通畅，绝不会走岔。我的目光那样专注地钉在他的身影上，旁的几乎一概不见；到后来我才猛然惊觉：峡谷已经空了。方才我还看见那一片恶棍的嘴脸，看见那两个人稍稍离开人群、立在岩石边；我低头只一瞬，再往峡中望去，已经空无一人。我怀着哑然的恐怖摸回家去，因力竭而倒头即睡。若无变故，我本该一睡多个时辰；可我醒来时，太阳才刚升起，晨光正照在我的床上。我是从睡梦中惊跳起来的，像受了狠狠一击；我惶惑四顾，骇然看见房中站着三个男人。其中一个把手按在我肩上，对我说话。

===Original===
“Come, mister, wake up. Your time’s up now, I reckon, and the boys are waiting for you outside, and they’re in a big hurry. Come on; you can put on your clothes, it’s kind of chilly this morning.”

I saw the other two men smiling sourly at each other, but I understood nothing. I simply pulled on my clothes, and said I was ready.

“All right, come on then. You go first, Nichols, and Jim and I will give the gentleman an arm.”

===Chinese===
"来吧，先生，醒醒。我估摸着你的时辰到了；弟兄们在外头候着呢，都急得不行。走吧；衣裳只管穿上，今儿早上怪冷的。"

我看见另外两人彼此酸溜溜地笑着，可我什么也不明白。我只顾穿上衣服，说我准备好了。

"好咧，那就走吧。尼科尔斯，你头里走，我和吉姆来搀这位先生一把。"

===Original===
They took me out into the sunlight, and then I understood the meaning of a dull murmur that had vaguely perplexed me while I was dressing. There were about two hundred men waiting outside, and some women too, and when they saw me there was a low muttering growl. I did not know what I had done, but that noise made my heart beat and the sweat come out on my face. I saw confusedly, as through a veil, the tumult and tossing of the crowd, discordant voices were speaking, and amongst all those faces there was not one glance of mercy, but a fury of lust that I did not understand. I found myself presently walking in a sort of procession up the slope of the valley, and on every side of me there were men with revolvers in their hands. Now and then a voice struck me, and I heard words and sentences of which I could form no connected story. But I understood that there was one sentence of execration; I heard scraps of stories that seemed strange and improbable. Someone was talking of men, lured by cunning devices from their homes and murdered with hideous tortures, found writhing like wounded snakes in dark and lonely places, only crying for someone to stab them to the heart, and so end their torments; and I heard another voice speaking of innocent girls who had vanished for a day or two, and then had come back and died, blushing red with shame even in the agonies of death. I wondered what it all meant, and what was to happen, but I was so weary that I walked on in a dream, scarcely longing for anything but sleep. At last we stopped. We had reached the summit of the hill, overlooking Blue Rock Valley, and I saw that I was standing beneath a clump of trees where I had often sat. I was in the midst of a ring of armed men, and I saw that two or three men were very busy with piles of wood, while others were fingering a rope. Then there was a stir in the crowd, and a man was pushed forward. His hands and feet were tightly bound with cord, and though his face was unutterably villainous I pitied him for the agony that worked his features and twisted his lips.
