I knew him; he was amongst those that had gathered round Smith in Black Gulf Canyon. In an instant he was unbound, and stripped naked; and borne beneath one of the trees, and his neck encircled by a noose that went around the trunk. A hoarse voice gave some kind of order; there was a rush of feet, and the rope tightened; and there before me I saw the blackened face and the writhing limbs and the shameful agony of death. One after another, half a dozen men, all of whom I had seen in the canyon the night before, were strangled before me, and their bodies were flung forth on the ground. Then there was a pause, and the man who had roused me a short while before, came up to me and said:—

===Chinese===
他们把我带到日光里；穿衣时隐隐搅扰着我的那阵沉闷的嗡嗡声，此刻我才明白是什么意思。外面候着约莫两百个男人，还有几个女人；他们一看见我，便起了一阵低低的、咕噜作响的吼声。我不知道自己做了什么，可那声浪逼得我心跳加急，冷汗满面。我恍恍惚惚，如同隔着一层薄纱，看见人群的骚动与翻腾；错杂的人声纷纷嚷嚷；在那所有的面孔中间，没有一瞥是怜悯，只有一股我不懂的狂怒的欲焰。不多时我发觉自己已走在一支行列似的队伍里，顺着谷坡往上走；我四围全是手握左轮枪的人。不时有一句话刺进我耳中，我听见一些字句，却串不成一个连贯的故事。可我听懂了，那是一句句的诅咒；我听见几则离奇得近乎不经的传闻的碎片。有人在讲：一些男人被奸计诱出家门，遭骇人的酷刑杀害，被人发现时，正在幽暗荒僻之处如伤蛇一般翻滚扭动，只嚷着求人给他们心口一刀，好终结他们的苦楚；我又听见另一个声音讲起一些清白的姑娘，失踪一两日，回来之后便死了，纵在濒死的剧痛中，也羞得满面通红。我不知道这一切是什么意思，也不知道将有什么临头；我只是倦极了，恍如梦中地往前走，什么都不盼，只盼睡觉。终于我们停下了。我们已到山顶，俯瞰着蓝岩谷；我看见自己正立在一丛树下——那是我常坐的地方。我在一圈持枪的人当中，看见两三个人正忙着堆柴，另几个人则在手里捻弄一根绳子。随后人群里一阵骚动，一个人被推上前来。他手脚被绳索捆得死紧；那张脸虽邪恶得无以复加，我却怜悯他：苦痛扯动着他的五官，绞扭着他的嘴唇。我认得他；他是昨夜在黑渊峡围聚在史密斯身边的人之一。顷刻间他被解开，剥得赤条条的，架到一棵树下，一条套索绕着树干勒住了他的脖颈。一个嘶哑的嗓音下达了什么口令；脚步一拥而上，绳索收紧；于是我亲眼看见那张憋黑的脸、那些抽搐的肢体，和死亡那副羞耻的惨相。一个接一个，六个人先后在我面前被绞杀——全是我前一夜在峡谷里见过的——尸首一具具抛掷在地。随后是一个间歇；不久前把我从床上唤醒的那个人走到我跟前，说：——

===Original===
“Now, mister, it’s your turn. We give you five minutes to cast up your accounts, and when that’s clocked, by the living God we will burn you alive at that tree.”

It was then I awoke and understood. I cried out:—

“Why, what have I done? Why should you hurt me? I am a harmless man, I never did you any wrong.” I covered my face with my hands; it seemed so pitiful, and it was such a terrible death.

===Chinese===
"现在，先生，轮到你了。给你五分钟结一结你一生的账；时辰一到，凭着永生的上帝起誓，我们就把你活活烧死在那棵树上。"

直到那时，我才如梦方醒，明白了过来。我喊道：——

"哎呀，我做了什么？你们凭什么害我？我是个无害的人，从没做过一件对不起你们的事。"我双手掩住脸；这情形太凄惨了，那死法又太可怕了。

===Original===
“What have I done?” I cried again. “You must take me for some other man. You cannot know me.”

“You black-hearted devil,” said the man at my side, “we know you well enough. There’s not a man within thirty miles of this that won’t curse Jack Smith when you are burning in hell.”

“My name is not Smith,” I said, with some hope left in me. “My name is Wilkins. I was Mr. Smith’s secretary, but I knew nothing of him.”

===Chinese===
"我做了什么？"我又喊道，"你们一定是把我认成别人了。你们不可能认得我。"

"你这黑心肠的恶魔，"我身旁那人说，"我们认你认得够清楚了。等你下了地狱在火里烧的时候，方圆三十英里内，没有一个人不会咒骂杰克·史密斯。"

"我不姓史密斯，"我说，心里还存着几分指望，"我叫威尔金斯。我是史密斯先生的秘书，可我对他一无所知。"

===Original===
“Hark at the black liar,” said the man. “Secretary be damned! You were clever enough, I dare say, to slink out at night, and keep your face in the dark, but we’ve tracked you out at last. But your time’s up. Come along.”

I was dragged to the tree and bound to it with chains, and I saw the piles of wood heaped all about me, and shut my eyes. Then I felt myself drenched all over with some liquid, and looked again, and a woman grinned at me. She had just emptied a great can of petroleum over me and over the wood. A voice shouted, “Fire away,” and I fainted and knew nothing more.

===Chinese===
"听听这条黑心谎虫，"那人说，"什么秘书，见鬼去吧！你够机灵的，我敢说，夜里偷偷溜出去，专拣黑地里亮那张脸；可我们到底把你追查出来了。你的时辰到了。走吧。"

我被拖到那棵树下，用铁链捆在树上；我看见柴堆四面堆拢过来，便闭上了眼。随后我感到一样什么液体兜头浇了我一身；睁眼再看，一个女人正朝我龇牙咧嘴地笑。她刚把一大罐煤油泼遍我和柴堆。一个声音高喊："放火！"我便晕了过去，人事不知了。

===Original===
When I opened my eyes I was lying on a bed in a bare comfortless room. A doctor was holding some strong salts to my nostrils, and a gentleman standing by the bed, whom I afterwards found to be the sheriff, addressed me:—

“Say, mister,” he began, “you’ve had an uncommon narrow squeak for it. The boys were just about lighting up when I came along with the posse, and I had as much as I could do to bring you off, I can tell you. And, mind you, I don’t blame them; they had made up their minds, you see, that you were the head of the Black Gulf gang, and at first nothing I could say would persuade them you weren’t Jack Smith. Luckily, a man from here named Evans, that came along with us, allowed he had seen you with Jack Smith, and that you were yourself. So we brought you along and jailed you, but you can go if you like, when you’re through with this faint turn.”

===Chinese===
再睁眼时，我正躺在一间空落落的、毫无暖意的房里的床上。一位医生正把猛烈的嗅盐举到我鼻端；床边立着一位绅士——我后来才知道是县治安官（sheriff）——向我开口道：——

"我说，先生，"他开口道，"你这一遭可真是险到毫巅。我带着民团赶到的时候，弟兄们正要动手点火了；费了多大的劲才把你抢下来，我告诉你。还有，你可别见怪，我并不怪他们；他们打定了主意，认定你是黑渊峡帮的头子，起先任我说破嘴皮，也没法叫他们相信你不是杰克·史密斯。多亏本地一个姓埃文斯的人跟我们一起来的，他说他见过你和杰克·史密斯在一起，说你确实是你本人。这样我们才把你带走关了起来；等你这阵晕劲过去，愿意走就走吧。"

===Original===
I got on the cars the next day, and in three weeks I was in London; again almost penniless. But from that time my fortune seemed to change. I made influential friends in all directions; bank directors courted my company, and editors positively flung themselves into my arms. I had only to choose my career, and after a while I determined that I was meant by nature for a life of comparative leisure. With an ease that seemed almost ridiculous I obtained a well-paid position in connection with a prosperous political club. I have charming chambers in a central neighborhood close to the parks; the club chef exerts himself when I lunch or dine, and the rarest vintages in the cellar are always at my disposal. Yet, since my return to London, I have never known a day’s security or peace; I tremble when I awake lest Smith should be standing at my bed, and every step I take seems to bring me nearer to the edge of the precipice. Smith, I knew, had escaped free from the raid of the vigilantes, and I grew faint at the thought that he would in all probability return to London, and that suddenly and unprepared I should meet him face to face. Every morning as I left my house, I would peer up and down the street, expecting to see that dreaded figure awaiting me; I have delayed at street corners, my heart in my mouth, sickening at the thought that a few quick steps might bring us together; I could not bear to frequent the theatres or music halls, lest by some bizarre chance he should prove to be my neighbor. Sometimes, I have been forced, against my will, to walk out at night, and then in silent squares the shadows have made me shudder, and in the medley of meetings in the crowded thoroughfares, I have said to myself, “It must come sooner or later; he will surely return to town, and I shall see him when I feel most secure.” I scanned the newspapers for hint or intimation of approaching danger, and no small type nor report of trivial interest was allowed to pass unread. Especially I read and reread the advertisement columns, but without result. Months passed by and I was undisturbed till, though I felt far from safe, I no longer suffered from the intolerable oppression of instant and ever present terror. This afternoon as I was walking quietly along Oxford Street, I raised my eyes, and looked across the road, and then at last I saw the man who had so long haunted my thoughts.

===Chinese===
第二天我便上了火车；不出三个星期，我已回到伦敦——又一次几乎身无分文。可从那时起，我的时运仿佛转了。我四面八方交上了有权有势的朋友；银行董事们争相与我结交，各家编辑简直是自己投进我的怀抱。前程任我拣选；过了些时日，我断定天生我材，合该过一份较为清闲的日子。我以近乎可笑的轻易，在一家兴旺的政党俱乐部里谋得一个报酬丰厚的差事。我在近公园的市中心地段有一套称心的寓所；每逢我在俱乐部午膳晚宴，厨师长总要卖弄手段，酒窖里最珍稀的陈酿也总是听凭我取用。然而，自从回到伦敦，我不曾有过一日安稳与太平；每天醒来我便发颤，生怕史密斯正立在我床前；我走的每一步，都仿佛把我向悬崖边上更送近一寸。我知道，史密斯已从自警会（Vigilance Committee）的突袭中全身而退；一想到他十有八九要回伦敦，一想到我会猝不及防、与他迎面撞上，我便遍体发软。每天早晨出门，我总要顺着街两头张望，预感会看见那个可怕的身影正等着我；我在街角迁延，心提到嗓子眼，一想到快步几步我们便会撞上，便觉恶心；剧院和音乐厅我不敢常去，生怕鬼使神差，他偏偏成了我的邻座。有时我身不由己，须得夜里出门；静悄悄的广场上，那些黑影教我战栗；在熙攘大街的纷纷人丛中，我对自己说："早晚有这一遭；他必定回城里来，而我要在我自觉最安稳的时候看见他。"我细读报纸，搜寻危险临近的暗示与征兆；再小的铅字、再琐屑的报道，也不容它漏过不读。尤其是广告栏，我读了又读，却一无所获。几个月过去，我未曾受到惊扰；虽说远谈不上安全，那种即时紧迫、无时不在的恐怖重压，总算不再折磨我了。今天下午，我正安安静静沿牛津街走着，抬起眼来往马路对面一望——终于，我看见了那个在我心头萦绕了这么久的人。

---

===Original===
Mr. Wilkins finished his wine, and leaned back in his chair, looking sadly at Dyson; and then, as if a thought struck him, fished out of an inner pocket a leather letter case, and handed a newspaper cutting across the table.

Dyson glanced closely at the slip, and saw that it had been extracted from the columns of an evening paper. It ran as follows:—

===Chinese===
威尔金斯先生喝干了酒，向椅背上一仰，凄然望着戴森；随即，仿佛蓦地想起什么，他从内袋里摸出一只皮制信夹，隔着桌子递过来一角剪报。

戴森凑近细看那纸条，看出它是从一份晚报的版面上剪下来的。内容如下：——

===Original===
> A Dalziel telegram from Reading (Colorado) states that advices received there from Blue Rock Park report a frightful instance of popular vengeance. For some time the neighborhood has been terrorized by the crimes of a gang of desperadoes, who, under the cover of a carefully planned organization, have perpetrated the most infamous cruelties on men and women. A Vigilance Committee was formed, and it was found that the leader of the gang was a person named Smith, living in Blue Rock Park. Action was taken, and six of the worst in the band were summarily strangled in the presence of two or three hundred men and women. Smith is said to have escaped.

===Chinese===
> 雷丁（科罗拉多）达尔齐尔通讯社（Dalziel）电讯：据该地接获蓝岩园方面的消息，当地发生一起骇人听闻的民众复仇。一时以来，该地区为一伙亡命之徒的罪行所慑；这伙人借着周密筹划的组织作掩护，对男女犯下了最伤天害理的暴行。当地遂成立自警会，查明匪首系一名叫史密斯者，居于蓝岩园。自警会随即行动，当着两三百名男女之面，将匪帮中最凶恶的六人就地绞杀。据称史密斯已脱逃。

===Original===
“This is a terrible story,” said Dyson; “I can well believe that your days and nights are haunted by such fearful scenes as you have described. But surely you have no need to fear Smith? He has much more cause to fear you. Consider: you have only to lay your information before the police, and a warrant would be immediately issued for his arrest. Besides, you will, I am sure, excuse me for what I am going to say.”

“My dear sir,” said Mr. Wilkins, “I hope you will speak to me with perfect freedom.”

“Well, then, I must confess that my impression was that you were rather disappointed at not being able to stop the man before he drove off. I thought you seemed annoyed that you could not get across the street.”

===Chinese===
"这故事够可怕的，"戴森说，"我完全相信，您所描述的那些骇人情景日夜缠绕着您。可您断然不必害怕史密斯吧？他怕您才更有理由。想想看：您只消把情报往警察面前一递，逮捕他的拘票立时便会签发。另外——我接下来说的话，还望您多多包涵。"

"亲爱的先生，"威尔金斯先生说，"但愿您对我畅所欲言，百无禁忌。"

"那么，恕我直言，我的印象是：没能赶在那人驱车离开之前拦住他，您未免相当失望。我看您似乎因为过不了街而懊恼。"

===Original===
“Sir, I did not know what I was about. I caught sight of the man, but it was only for a moment, and the agony you witnessed was the agony of suspense. I was not perfectly certain of the face; and the horrible thought that Smith was again in London overwhelmed me. I shuddered at the idea of this incarnate fiend, whose soul is black with shocking crimes, mingling free and unobserved amongst the harmless crowds, meditating perhaps a new and more fearful cycle of infamies. I tell you, sir, that an awful being stalks through the streets, a being before whom the sunlight itself should blacken, and the summer air grow chill and dank. Such thoughts as these rushed upon me with the force of a whirlwind; I lost my senses.”

===Chinese===
"先生，我那时已不知自己身在何处。我瞥见了那人，不过一瞬；您目睹的那阵苦痛，乃是悬而未决的煎熬。那张脸，我并没有十成的把握；而史密斯重又回到伦敦这一可怕的念头，把我整个压倒了。一想到这个化身的恶魔——他的灵魂已被骇人的罪行熏得乌黑——竟逍遥自在、无人察觉地混迹于无害的人群之中，也许正在酝酿新一轮更加可怖的恶行，我便不寒而栗。我告诉您，先生，一个可怕的生灵正阔步走过条条街道；在那生灵面前，日光本身也当为之黯黑，夏日的空气也当为之阴冷潮腥。这样的念头挟着旋风之力扑到我身上；我便失了知觉。"

===Original===
“I see. I partly understand your feelings, but I would impress on you that you have nothing really to fear. Depend upon it, Smith will not molest you in any way. You must remember he himself has had a warning; and indeed from the brief glance I had of him, he seemed to me to be a frightened-looking man. However, I see it is getting late, and if you will excuse me, Mr. Wilkins, I think I will be going. I dare say we shall often meet here.”

Dyson walked off smartly, pondering the strange story chance had brought him, and finding on cool reflection that there was something a little strange in Mr. Wilkins’s manner, for which not even so weird a catalogue of experiences could altogether account.

===Chinese===
"我明白了。您的感受，我大致能够领会；不过我要向您进一言：您实在没有什么可怕的。放心吧，史密斯断不会来搅扰您。您须记得，他自己也已领过一回教训；而且，凭我对他那匆匆一瞥，依我看，他倒像个受了惊吓的人。好了，天色不早了；您若不见怪，威尔金斯先生，我想我该走了。想必咱们会常在这儿见面。"

戴森脚步轻快地去了，一路咀嚼着机缘送上门来的这段奇事；冷静回味之下，他又发觉威尔金斯先生的举止之中，有几分说不出的古怪——纵然是这么一份离奇万状的历险清单，也断断乎解释它不尽。

## Adventure of the Missing Brother / 失踪的兄弟奇遇

===Original===
Mr. Charles Phillipps was, as has been hinted, a gentleman of pronounced scientific tastes. In his early days he had devoted himself with fond enthusiasm to the agreeable study of biology, and a brief monograph on the Embryology of the Microscopic Holothuria had formed his first contribution to the belles lettres. Later, he had somewhat relaxed the severity of his pursuits, and had dabbled in the more frivolous subjects of paleontology and ethnology; he had a cabinet in his sitting-room whose drawers were stuffed with rude flint implements, and a charming fetish from the South Seas was the dominant note in the decorative scheme of the apartment. Flattering himself with the title of materialist, he was in truth one of the most credulous of men, but he required a marvel to be neatly draped in the robes of science before he would give it any credit, and the wildest dreams took solid shape to him if only the nomenclature were severe and irreproachable; he laughed at the witch, but quailed before the powers of the hypnotist, lifting his eyebrows when Christianity was mentioned, but adoring protyle and the ether. For the rest, he prided himself on a boundless scepticism; the average tale of wonder he heard with nothing but contempt, and he would certainly not have credited a word or syllable of Dyson’s story of the pursuer and pursued unless the gold coin had been produced as visible and tangible evidence. As it was he half suspected that Dyson had imposed on him; he knew his friend’s disordered fancies, and his habit of conjuring up the marvellous to account for the entirely commonplace; and on the whole he was inclined to think that the so-called facts in the odd adventure had been gravely distorted in the telling. Since the evening on which he had listened to the tale, he had paid Dyson a visit, and had delivered himself of some serious talk on the necessity of accurate observation, and the folly, as he put it, of using a kaleidoscope instead of a telescope in the view of things, to which remarks his friend had listened with a smile that was extremely sardonic. “My dear fellow,” Dyson had remarked at last, “you will allow me to tell you that I see your drift perfectly. However, you will be astonished to hear that I consider you to be the visionary, while I am a sober and serious spectator of human life. You have gone round the circle, and while you fancy yourself far in the golden land of new philosophies, you are in reality a dweller in a metaphorical Clapham; your scepticism has defeated itself and become a monstrous credulity; you are in fact in the position of the bat or owl, I forget which it was, who denied the existence of the sun at noonday, and I shall be astonished if you do not one day come to me full of contrition for your manifold intellectual errors, with a humble resolution to see things in their true light for the future.” This tirade had left Mr. Phillipps unimpressed; he considered Dyson as hopeless, and he went home to gloat over some primitive stone implements that a friend had sent him from India. He found that his landlady, seeing them displayed in all their rude formlessness upon the table, had removed the collection to the dustbin, and had replaced it by lunch; and the afternoon was spent in malodorous research. Mrs. Brown, hearing these stones spoken of as very valuable knives, had called him in his hearing “poor Mr. Phillipps,” and between rage and evil odors he spent a sorry afternoon. It was four o’clock before he had completed his work of rescue; and, overpowered with the flavors of decaying cabbage-leaves, Phillipps felt that he must have a walk to gain an appetite for the evening meal. Unlike Dyson, he walked fast, with his eyes on the pavement, absorbed in his thoughts and oblivious of the life around him; and he could not have told by what streets he had passed, when he suddenly lifted up his eyes and found himself in Leicester Square. The grass and flowers pleased him, and he welcomed the opportunity of resting for a few minutes, and glancing round, he saw a bench which had only one occupant, a lady, and as she was seated at one end, Phillipps took up a position at the other extremity, and began to pass in angry review the events of the afternoon. He had noticed as he came up to the bench that the person already there was neatly dressed, and to all appearance young; her face he could not see, as it was turned away in apparent contemplation of the shrubs, and moreover shielded with her hand; but it would be doing wrong to Mr. Phillipps to imagine that his choice of a seat was dictated by any hopes of an affair of the heart; he had simply preferred the company of one lady to that of five dirty children, and having seated himself was immersed directly in thoughts of his misfortunes. He had meditated changing his lodgings; but now, on a judicial review of the case in all its bearings, his calmer judgment told him that the race of landladies is like to the race of the leaves, and that there was but little to choose between them. He resolved, however, to talk to Mrs. Brown, the offender, very coolly and yet severely, to point out the extreme indiscretion of her conduct, and to express a hope for better things in the future. With this decision registered in his mind, Phillipps was about to get up from the seat and move off, when he was intensely annoyed to hear a stifled sob, evidently from the lady, who still continued her contemplation of the shrubs and flowerbeds. He clutched his stick desperately, and in a moment would have been in full retreat, when the lady turned her face towards him, and with a mute entreaty bespoke his attention. She was a young girl with a quaint and piquant rather than a beautiful face, and she was evidently in the bitterest distress, and Mr. Phillipps sat down again, and cursed his chances heartily. The young lady looked at him with a pair of charming eyes of a shining hazel, which showed no trace of tears, though a handkerchief was in her hand; she bit her lip, and seemed to struggle with some overpowering grief, and her whole attitude was all beseeching and imploring. Phillipps sat on the edge of the bench gazing awkwardly at her, and wondering what was to come next, and she looked at him still without speaking.

===Chinese===
查尔斯·菲利普斯先生，如前文所暗示，是一位科学趣味极为鲜明的绅士。早年间，他曾满怀热忱地投身于生物学这门可人的学问，一篇论微观海参之胚胎学（Embryology of the Microscopic Holothuria）的简短专著，便是他献给美文殿堂的处女作。后来，他略微松弛了治学的严苛，又涉猎了古生物学与民族学这些较为轻浮的科目；他起居室里立着一口柜子，抽屉里塞满粗陋的燧石器物，一件来自南海的可爱拜物偶，则是全屋装饰格局中的主调。他自诩唯物主义者的头衔，实则是天下最轻信的人之一；只是他要求一件奇迹先得齐齐整整地披上科学的袍子，方肯予它半分信任；而只要名目严峻得无可指摘，再狂乱的梦到他眼里也凝成坚实的形体。他嘲笑女巫，却在催眠师的法力面前发抖；提起基督教他便扬起眉毛，对始质（protyle）与以太却顶礼膜拜。此外，他引以为傲的是自己无边的怀疑主义；寻常的奇谈他听来只有轻蔑；若不是那枚金币作为看得见摸得着的证据摆在了眼前，戴森那追逐与被追的故事，他断然一个字、一个音节也不肯信。即便如此，他仍有几分疑心戴森捉弄了他；他深知这位朋友那颠倒错乱的幻想，深知他惯于召来种种奇观，好解释全然平常不过的事；大体而言，他倾向于认为，那场离奇遭遇中的所谓事实，在转述之间已被严重歪曲。自那晚听完这个故事，他去拜访过戴森一回，郑重其事地发了一通议论，讲精确观察之必要，讲——照他的说法——观看世事时以万花筒代望远镜的愚蠢；对这番高论，他的朋友面带极端讥诮的微笑听完了。“亲爱的朋友，”戴森末了说，“请容我直言，你的用意我看得一清二楚。不过，你听了定会吃惊：依我看，你才是那个空想家，而我，是人类生活清醒而郑重的旁观者。你已兜了一个圈子；你自以为远在新哲学的黄金之邦，实则不过住在一个比喻意义上的克拉珀姆（Clapham）；你的怀疑主义打败了它自己，长成了一头怪异的轻信；你如今的处境，正如那只蝙蝠——还是猫头鹰来着，我记不清了——正午时分否认太阳的存在；倘若有一天你不曾满怀悔恨地来见我，痛陈自己累累的智识之过，谦卑地立志从此看清事物的本来面目，我倒要奇怪了。”这篇雄辞未在菲利普斯先生心里留下什么印象；他认为戴森已无可救药，便回家去赏玩一位朋友从印度寄给他的原始石器。他发现，女房东见这些石头以其粗陋无形之姿陈在桌上，已把整批收藏挪进了垃圾箱，换上了午餐；于是那个下午便在恶臭的翻检中度过。布朗太太听说这些石头竟被唤作十分贵重的刀子，当着他的面叹他“可怜的菲利普斯先生”；他在怒火与臭气之间，熬过了一个凄惨的下午。四点钟，他方才完成这场抢救的工程；烂菜叶的气味熏得他无力招架，菲利普斯觉得非得走一走，才生得出晚餐的胃口。与戴森不同，他走路极快，眼睛盯着人行道，沉在自己的思绪里，对周遭的生活浑然不觉；他说不清自己走过的是哪几条街，蓦然抬眼，已置身莱斯特广场。草地与花木悦他的目，他也乐得歇上几分钟；环顾之间，他看见一张长椅上只坐着一个人，一位女士，且坐在椅子一端，菲利普斯便在另一端落座，开始怒气冲冲地检阅下午的种种变故。走向长椅时他已留意到，先在的那位衣着整洁，看模样还年轻；她的脸他望不见，那张脸正别向树丛，似在出神，且用手遮着；但若以为菲利普斯先生的择座是出于什么桃色指望，那便是冤枉他了；他不过是宁与一位女士做伴，也不愿与五个脏孩子为邻；一坐定，他便径自沉入自己不幸的思虑。他曾动念换个寓所；可如今将全案种种关节依理推究一遍，较为冷静的判断告诉他：女房东这一族类，正如树叶那一族类，彼此之间原没有多少可挑的。不过他决计要同那位冒犯者布朗太太谈一谈，冷冷的、却又严厉地谈，指出她的行止何其失于轻率，并表达对来日改善的期望。这项决断刚在心里登记完毕，菲利普斯正要从椅上起身离去，忽听得一声压抑的啜泣，分明出自那位女士——她却仍在凝望树丛花坛。他绝望地攥紧手杖，再过片刻便要全线撤退，那女士却把脸转向了他，无言地恳求他的注意。她是位年轻姑娘，一张古雅俏皮、说不上美丽的脸，显然正陷于至深的悲苦；菲利普斯先生重新坐下，把自己的时运咒骂了个够。年轻姑娘望着他，一双明媚的榛色眼睛，虽则手中还握着手帕，眼里却没有泪痕；她咬着唇，似在与一股压倒一切的哀恸搏斗，整个姿态满是祈求与恳望。菲利普斯坐在长椅边上，局促地望着她，不知接下来会有什么；而她仍旧望着他，一言不发。

===Original===
“Well, madam,” he said at last, “I understood from your gesture that you wished to speak to me. Is there anything I can do for you? Though, if you will pardon me, I cannot help saying that that seems highly improbable.”

“Ah, sir,” she said in a low murmuring voice, “do not speak harshly to me. I am in sore straits, and I thought from your face that I could safely ask your sympathy, if not your help.”

===Chinese===
“那么，夫人，”他终于开口，“我从您的手势看出来，您有话要对我说。有什么我能效劳的吗？不过——恕我直言——我不得不承认，这看上去极不可能。”

“啊，先生，”她低声呢喃道，“请不要对我出言冷硬。我的处境艰窘之至；我从您的脸上看出，向您求一份同情——若不能求一份援手——必定不会错。”

===Original===
“Would you kindly tell me what is the matter?” said Phillipps. “Perhaps you would like some tea?”

“I knew I could not be mistaken,” the lady replied. “That offer of refreshment bespeaks a generous mind. But tea, alas! is powerless to console me. If you will let me, I will endeavor to explain my trouble.”

===Chinese===
“可否见告，究竟出了什么事？”菲利普斯说，“要不，先来点茶？”

“我就知道我不会看错，”女士答道，“这一句请用茶点，足见一副慷慨的心肠。可是茶啊，唉！茶安慰不了我。您若容许，我就尽力把我的苦处讲给您听。”

===Original===
“I should be glad if you would.”

“I will do so, and I will try and be brief, in spite of the numerous complications which have made me, young as I am, tremble before what seems the profound and terrible mystery of existence. Yet the grief which now racks my very soul is but too simple; I have lost my brother.”

“Lost your brother! How on earth can that be?”

===Chinese===
“愿闻其详。”

“我就讲吧，并且力求简短——尽管重重纠葛，已使得我这样年轻的人，在存在那看似深不可测、可怖非常的神秘面前战栗。然而此刻煎熬我灵魂的悲恸，却再简单不过：我失去了我的兄弟。”

“失去了您的兄弟！这究竟是怎么一回事？”

===Original===
“I see I must trouble you with a few particulars. My brother, then, who is by some years my elder, is a tutor in a private school in the extreme north of London. The want of means deprived him of the advantages of a University education; and lacking the stamp of a degree, he could not hope for that position which his scholarship and his talents entitled him to claim. He was thus forced to accept the post of classical master at Dr. Saunderson’s Highgate Academy for the sons of gentlemen, and he has performed his duties with perfect satisfaction to his principal for some years. My personal history need not trouble you; it will be enough if I tell you that for the last month I have been governess in a family residing at Tooting. My brother and I have always cherished the warmest mutual affection; and though circumstances into which I need not enter have kept us apart for some time, yet we have never lost sight of one another. We made up our minds that unless one of us was absolutely unable to rise from a bed of sickness, we would never let a week pass by without meeting, and some time ago we chose this square as our rendezvous on account of its central position and its convenience of access. And indeed, after a week of distasteful toil, my brother felt little inclination for much walking, and we have often spent two or three hours on this bench, speaking of our prospects and of happier days, when we were children. In the early spring it was cold and chilly; still we enjoyed the short respite, and I think that we were often taken for a pair of lovers, as we sat close together, eagerly talking. Saturday after Saturday we have met each other here, and though the doctor told him it was madness, my brother would not allow the influenza to break the appointment. That was some time ago; last Saturday we had a long and happy afternoon, and separated more cheerfully than usual, feeling that the coming week would be bearable, and resolving that our next meeting should be if possible still more pleasant. I arrived here at the time agreed upon, four o’clock, and sat down and watched for my brother, expecting every moment to see him advancing towards me from that gate at the north side of the square. Five minutes passed by, and he had not arrived; I thought he must have missed his train, and the idea that our interview would be cut short by twenty minutes, or perhaps half an hour, saddened me; I had hoped we should be so happy together today. Suddenly, moved by I know not what impulse, I turned abruptly round, and how can I describe to you my astonishment when I saw my brother advancing slowly towards me from the southern side of the square, accompanied by another person. My first thought, I remember, had in it something of resentment that this man, whoever he was, should intrude himself into our meeting; I wondered who it could possibly be, for my brother had, I may say, no intimate friends. Then as I looked still at the advancing figures, another feeling took possession of me; it was a sensation of bristling fear, the fear of the child in the dark, unreasonable and unreasoning, but terrible, clutching at my heart as with the cold grip of a dead man’s hands. Yet I overcame the feeling, and looked steadily at my brother, waiting for him to speak, and more closely at his companion. Then I noticed that this man was leading my brother rather than walking arm-in-arm with him; he was a tall man, dressed in quite ordinary fashion. He wore a high bowler hat, and, in spite of the warmth of the day, a plain black overcoat, tightly buttoned, and I noticed his trousers, of a quiet black and gray stripe. The face was commonplace too, and indeed I cannot recall any special features, or any trick of expression; for though I looked at him as he came near, curiously enough his face made no impression on me, it was as though I had seen a well-made mask. They passed in front of me, and to my unutterable astonishment I heard my brother’s voice speaking to me, though his lips did not move, nor his eyes look into mine. It was a voice I cannot describe, though I knew it, but the words came to my ears as if mingled with plashing water and the sound of a shallow brook flowing amidst stones. I heard, then, the words, ‘I cannot stay,’ and for a moment the heavens and the earth seemed to rush together with the sound of thunder, and I was thrust forth from the world into a black void without beginning and without end. For, as my brother passed me, I saw the hand that held him by the arm, and seemed to guide him, and in one moment of horror I realized that it was as a formless thing that has mouldered for many years in the grave. The flesh was peeled in strips from the bones, and hung apart dry and granulated, and the fingers that encircled my brother’s arm were all unshapen, claw-like things, and one was but a stump from which the end had rotted off. When I recovered my senses I saw the two passing out by that gate. I paused for a moment, and then with a rush as of fire to my heart I knew that no horror could stay me, but that I must follow my brother and save him, even though all hell rose up against me. I ran out and looked up the pavement, and saw the two figures walking amidst the crowd. I ran across the road, and saw them turn up that side street, and I reached the corner a moment later. In vain I looked to right and left, for neither my brother nor his strange guardian was in sight; two elderly men were coming down arm-in-arm, and a telegraph boy was walking lustily along whistling. I remained there a moment horror-struck, and then I bowed my head and returned to this seat, where you found me. Now, sir, do you wonder at my grief? Oh, tell me what has happened to my brother, or I feel I shall go mad.”

===Chinese===
“看来，我必须拿几件细节来烦扰您了。那么，我的兄弟比我年长几岁，在伦敦极北的一所私立学校里任教。家境的匮乏，夺去了他受大学教育的种种便宜；既没有学位的印记，他便无望谋得他那学识与才具本可企求的位置。于是他只得屈就桑德森博士（Dr. Saunderson）的海盖特绅士子弟学校（Highgate Academy），充任古典语文教师，多年来供职尽忠，深得校长满意。我个人的履历，不必累赘您；只消告诉您，这一个月来，我在图廷（Tooting）一户人家当家庭女教师。我与兄弟素来怀着最深厚的骨肉之情；虽然境遇——个中情由无须细说——使我们分离了一时，我们却从未失去彼此的音讯。我们拿定主意：除非哪一个当真病得离不了病榻，绝不容一个星期空空过去而不相见；又因这广场地处要冲、来去方便，不久前我们便选定它作会面的地方。的确，一个星期叫人厌烦的劳碌之后，兄弟本也不大想多走路，我们时常就在这张长椅上消磨两三个钟头，谈谈各自的前程，谈谈儿时更快乐的日子。早春时节天还冷冽；我们仍享受这短促的喘息；而且我想，我们紧挨着坐、急切交谈的样子，常被人认作一对情侣。一个星期六接着一个星期六，我们在这里相会；医生说他这是发疯，他却不容流感坏了这桩约会。那是些时日之前的事了；上个星期六，我们过了一个漫长而快乐的下午，分别时比往常更觉宽慰，只觉未来这一周总算熬得过，还彼此约定，下次相见要尽可能更快活些。我按约定的时间——四点钟——到了这里，坐下守望我的兄弟，时刻盼着见他从广场北边那道门朝我走来。五分钟过去了，他没有来；我想他必定误了火车；一念及我们的会面要被削去二十分钟、或许半个钟头，我便黯然——我原指望今天我们该多快活啊。蓦地，不知被什么冲动驱使，我猛然回头——我该怎样向您形容我的惊骇：我看见我的兄弟正从广场南侧缓步朝我走来，身边还跟着另一个人。我记得，我的第一个念头里竟有几分怨怼，怨这个人——不管他是谁——闯进我们的相聚；我疑惑那会是谁，因为可以说，我的兄弟并没有什么至交。随后，我仍望着那两个走近的身影，另一种感觉攫住了我：那是一种毛发倒竖的恐惧，是黑暗中的孩子所有的恐惧，无理亦无由，却可怕之至，攫住我的心，仿佛用死人两只冰冷的手紧紧扼住。但我压下了这感觉，定睛望着我的兄弟，等他开口，又更仔细地打量他的同伴。这时我才注意到，那人并非与我兄弟挽臂同行，毋宁说是在牵引着他；他是个高个子，衣着十分寻常。他戴一顶圆顶高帽，尽管天气和暖，还穿一件黑色素面大衣，扣得严严实实；我又留意到他的裤子，黑灰相间的暗条子。那张脸也平庸之至；说实话，我竟想不起任何特别的五官、任何特别的神情；因为虽说他走近时我一直望着他，说来奇怪，那张脸却没有在我心里留下印象，就仿佛我看过的只是一张做工精巧的面具。两人从我面前经过，而令我难以言喻地惊愕的是，我听见了我兄弟的声音在对我说——嘴唇却未动，眼睛也未看我。那嗓音我说不出所以然，虽则我认得它；可那些字句传到我耳中，仿佛混着哗哗的水声，混着浅溪在乱石间流淌的声响。于是我听见了那几个字：‘我留不得了’；刹那之间，天与地挟着雷霆之声轰然合拢，我被逐出世界，掷进一片无始无终的黑色虚空。因为，我的兄弟走过我身旁时，我看见了那只扶着他手臂、似在引导他的手；惊怖的一瞬，我看清了：那是一件在坟墓里朽坏了许多年的、无形无状的东西。皮肉一条条从骨头上剥落，干枯成粒，各自垂挂着；箍住我兄弟手臂的那些手指，尽是些不成形状、爪子似的物件，其中一根不过是截残桩，指端早已烂掉。待我神志恢复，只见两人正从那道门走出去。我怔了片刻，随即一股火似的热血涌上心头：我知道，什么恐怖也拦不住我了，我必须跟上我的兄弟，救下他，哪怕全体地狱起来与我为敌。我奔出去，沿人行道望去，看见那两个人影正走在人群中间。我跑过马路，见他们拐上了那条横街，片刻之后，我赶到街角。左右张望，全归徒劳：我的兄弟和他那古怪的守护者，都不见了影踪；两位老者挽着臂膀迎面走来，一个电报童边走边起劲地吹着口哨。我在那里立了一刻，惊魂未定，然后垂下头，回到这张椅子上，也就是您找到我的地方。如今，先生，您还奇怪我的悲恸么？哦，告诉我，我的兄弟到底遭了什么，不然我怕我要疯了。”

===Original===
Mr. Phillipps, who had listened with exemplary patience to this tale, hesitated a moment before he spoke.

“My dear madam,” he said at length, “you have known how to engage me in your service, not only as a man, but as a student of science. As a fellow-creature I pity you most profoundly; you must have suffered extremely from what you saw, or rather from what you fancied you saw. For, as a scientific observer, it is my duty to tell you the plain truth, which, indeed, besides being true, must also console you. Allow me to ask you then to describe your brother.”

===Chinese===
菲利普斯先生以堪为楷模的耐心听完了这个故事，开口之前迟疑了片刻。

“亲爱的夫人，”他终于说，“您真懂得如何把我收归您的麾下：不仅作为一个人，还作为一个科学的研究者。作为同类，我对您深表怜悯；您所见的——毋宁说您自以为所见的一切，想必使您饱受折磨。因为，作为一个科学的观察者，我有责任告诉您朴素的真相；而这真相呢，除了真实之外，想必还能宽慰您。那么，请容我请您描述一下您的兄弟。”

===Original===
“Certainly,” said the lady, eagerly; “I can describe him accurately. My brother is a somewhat young-looking man; he is pale, has small black whiskers, and wears spectacles. He has rather a timid, almost a frightened expression, and looks about him nervously from side to side. Think, think! Surely you must have seen him. Perhaps you are an habitué of this engaging quarter; you may have met him on some previous Saturday. I may have been mistaken in supposing that he turned up that side street; he may have gone on, and you may have passed each other. Oh, tell me, sir, whether you have not seen him?”

“I am afraid I do not keep a very sharp lookout when I am walking,” said Phillipps, who would have passed his mother unnoticed; “but I am sure your description is admirable. And now will you describe the person, who, you say, held your brother by the arm?”

===Chinese===
“当然，”女士急切地说，“我能说得分毫不差。我的兄弟是个显得相当年轻的人；脸色苍白，蓄着两撇黑色小髭鬓，戴一副眼镜。他神情颇为怯懦，几乎可以说惊惶，紧张地左右张望。想想，请您想想！您一定见过他。也许您是这个迷人地段的常客；也许前几个星期六您曾同他打过照面。我也许看错了，以为他拐进了那条横街；他也许一直在前走，您同他交臂而过也未可知。哦，先生，告诉我，您当真没有见过他么？”

“恐怕我走路时，观察得并不怎么仔细，”菲利普斯说——他便是迎面撞上自己的母亲，也未必会察觉；“不过我敢说，您的描述精妙之至。那么现在，可否请您描述一下那个人——您说，是他挽着您兄弟手臂的那位？”

===Original===
“I cannot do so. I told you, his face seemed devoid of expression or salient feature. It was like a mask.”

“Exactly; you cannot describe what you have never seen. I need hardly point out to you the conclusion to be drawn; you have been the victim of an hallucination. You expected to see your brother, you were alarmed because you did not see him, and unconsciously, no doubt, your brain went to work, and finally you saw a mere projection of your own morbid thoughts; a vision of your absent brother, and a mere confusion of terrors incorporated in a figure which you can’t describe. Of course your brother has been in some way prevented from coming to meet you as usual. I expect you will hear from him in a day or two.”

===Chinese===
“我描述不了。我告诉过您，那张脸似乎既无神情，也无醒目的五官。它就像一张面具。”

“正是；您无法描述您从未见过的东西。无须我向您指出该下的结论：您是一场幻觉的受害者。您盼着见到您的兄弟，因为见不着而惊慌，于是——无疑是无意识地——您的大脑自行动作起来，末了您看见的，不过是您自己病态念头的投影：一个是缺席的兄弟的幻影，一个是种种恐怖胡乱捏合而成的一个您描述不出的形体。您的兄弟，自然是因了什么缘故，未能照常前来会您。我想，一两天内您就会得到他的音信。”

===Original===
The lady looked seriously at Mr. Phillipps, and then for a second there seemed almost a twinkling as of mirth about her eyes, but her face clouded sadly at the dogmatic conclusions to which the scientist was led so irresistibly.

“Ah,” she said, “you do not know. I cannot doubt the evidence of my waking senses. Besides, perhaps I have had experiences even more terrible. I acknowledge the force of your arguments, but a woman has intuitions which never deceive her. Believe me, I am not hysterical; feel my pulse, it is quite regular.”

===Chinese===
女士神色郑重地望着菲利普斯先生，刹那间她眼里几乎闪过一丝近乎笑意的微光；可听到这位科学家被一步步无可抗辩地引向的那些武断结论，她的脸又哀伤地暗了下来。

“唉，”她说，“您有所不知。我清醒时的感官所提供的证据，我无法怀疑。何况，我或许还有过更加可怖的经历。我承认您的论证有力；但女人自有直觉，直觉是从不欺骗她的。相信我，我并没有癔症；请摸摸我的脉搏，它再规律不过。”

===Original===
She stretched out her hand with a dainty gesture, and a glance that enraptured Phillipps in spite of himself. The hand held out to him was soft and white and warm, and as, in some confusion, he placed his fingers on the purple vein, he felt profoundly touched by the spectacle of love and grief before him.

“No,” he said, as he released her wrist, “as you say, you are evidently quite yourself. Still, you must be aware that living men do not possess dead hands. That sort of thing doesn’t happen. It is, of course, barely possible that you did see your brother with another gentleman, and that important business prevented him from stopping. As for the wonderful hand, there may have been some deformity, a finger shot off by accident, or something of that sort.”

===Chinese===
她以一个娇雅的姿势伸出手来，投来的一瞥使菲利普斯不由自主地心醉神迷。递到他面前的手柔软、白皙而温暖；他略带慌乱地把手指搭上那青紫色的血管，一时深为眼前这场爱与哀的景象所动。

“不，”他放开她的手腕，说道，“正如您所说，您显然全然清醒。不过，您总该如何，活人不会有死人的手。那一类事情是不会发生的。当然，也并非全无可能：您确实看见您的兄弟同另一位先生在一起，要紧的事务使他不得停留。至于那只奇妙的手，或许是某种残缺，一根指头偶然被枪打掉了，或诸如此类。”

===Original===
The lady shook her head mournfully.

“I see you are a determined rationalist,” she said. “Did you not hear me say that I have had experiences even more terrible? I too was once a sceptic, but after what I have known I can no longer affect to doubt.”

===Chinese===
女士哀哀地摇了摇头。

“我看得出，您是位死心塌地的理性主义者，”她说，“您没有听见我说，我还有过更加可怖的经历么？我也曾是个怀疑论者；但在我所知的一切之后，我再不能假装怀疑了。”

===Original===
“Madam,” replied Mr. Phillipps, “no one shall make me deny my faith. I will never believe, nor will I pretend to believe, that two and two make five, nor will I on any pretences admit the existence of two-sided triangles.”

“You are a little hasty,” rejoined the lady. “But may I ask you if you ever heard the name of Professor Gregg, the authority on ethnology and kindred subjects?”

===Chinese===
“夫人，”菲利普斯先生答道，“谁也不能叫我背弃我的信仰。我永远不信、也不会假装相信二加二等于五；无论借什么名目，我也不肯承认两边三角形的存在。”

“您未免性急了些，”女士应道，“不过我可否请问，您可曾听过格雷格教授（Professor Gregg）的大名——那位民族学及相邻学科的权威？”

===Original===
“I have done much more than merely hear of Professor Gregg,” said Phillipps. “I always regarded him as one of our most acute and clearheaded observers; and his last publication, the *Textbook of Ethnology*, struck me as being quite admirable in its kind. Indeed, the book had but come into my hands when I heard of the terrible accident which cut short Gregg’s career. He had, I think, taken a country house in the West of England for the summer, and is supposed to have fallen into a river. So far as I remember, his body was never recovered.”

===Chinese===
“对格雷格教授，我岂止是听说过，”菲利普斯说，“我一向把他看作我们最敏锐、头脑最清晰的观察者之一；他最后出版的那部《民族学教科书》（*Textbook of Ethnology*），在我看来于其同类中堪称绝妙。说实话，那书刚到我手里，我便听说了那场斩断格雷格生涯的可怕意外。我记得，他夏天在英格兰西部租了一所乡宅，想必是坠入了一条河。就我所忆，他的遗体始终未曾寻获。”

===Original===
“Sir, I am sure that you are discreet. Your conversation seems to declare as much, and the very title of that little work of yours which you mentioned, assures me that you are no empty trifler. In a word, I feel that I may depend on you. You appear to be under the impression that Professor Gregg is dead; I have no reason to believe that that is the case.”

“What?” cried Phillipps, astonished and perturbed. “You do not hint that there was anything disgraceful? I cannot believe it. Gregg was a man of clearest character; his private life was one of great benevolence; and though I myself am free from delusions, I believe him to have been a sincere and devout Christian. Surely you cannot mean to insinuate that some disreputable history forced him to flee the country?”

===Chinese===
“先生，我确信您是个谨慎的人。您的言谈已然宣告了这一点；而您方才提到的那部小著，单是它的题目，便向我担保您绝非空疏的轻浮子。一言以蔽之，我觉得我可以信赖您。您似乎以为格雷格教授已经死了；我却没有理由相信事情如此。”

“什么？”菲利普斯又惊又乱，失声道，“您不是在暗示其中有什么见不得人的事吧？我无法相信。格雷格为人清白坦荡；他的私生活充满仁善；虽然我自己不涉妄想，我却相信他是个诚挚而虔诚的基督徒。您总不至于意在影射，说是某段不名誉的往事，逼得他亡命出国吧？”

===Original===
“Again you are in a hurry,” replied the lady. “I said nothing of all this. Briefly, then, I must tell you that Professor Gregg left his house one morning in full health both of mind and body. He never returned, but his watch and chain, a purse containing three sovereigns in gold and some loose silver, with a ring that he wore habitually, were found three days later on a wild and savage hillside, many miles from the river. These articles were placed beside a limestone rock of fantastic form; they had been wrapped into a parcel with a kind of rough parchment which was secured with gut. The parcel was opened, and the inner side of the parchment bore an inscription done with some red substance; the characters were undecipherable, but seemed to be a corrupt cuneiform.”

===Chinese===
“您又性急了，”女士答道，“这些话我可一个字也没说。那就长话短说：一天早晨，格雷格教授身心俱健地离开了他的住所。他一去不返；但三天之后，他的怀表和表链、一只装着三枚金镑和几枚零银的钱包，还有他惯常戴的一枚戒指，在距那条河许多英里的一片荒蛮山坡上被人发现了。这些物件搁在一块形状奇诡的石灰岩旁；它们被人用一张粗羊皮纸裹成一个包裹，以肠线扎紧。包裹打开后，羊皮纸内侧有一行以某种红色物质写就的铭文；那些字迹无从辨认，只看似一种讹变的楔形文字。”

===Original===
“You interest me intensely,” said Phillips. “Would you mind continuing your story? The circumstance you have mentioned seems to me of the most inexplicable character, and I thirst for an elucidation.”

The young lady seemed to meditate for a moment, and she then proceeded to relate the

===Chinese===
“您叫我大感兴趣，”菲利普斯说，“可否请您继续讲下去？您提到的这桩情形，在我看来扑朔迷离之至，我渴盼一层解释。”

年轻姑娘似是沉吟了片刻，随后便开始讲述这篇

### Novel of the Black Seal / 黑印故事

===Original===
I must now give you some fuller particulars of my history. I am the daughter of a civil engineer, Steven Lally by name, who was so unfortunate as to die suddenly at the outset of his career, and before he had accumulated sufficient means to support his wife and her two children. My mother contrived to keep the small household going on resources which must have been incredibly small; we lived in a remote country village, because most of the necessaries of life were cheaper than in a town, but even so we were brought up with the severest economy. My father was a clever and well-read man, and left behind him a small but select collection of books, containing the best Greek, Latin, and English classics, and these books were the only amusement we possessed. My brother, I remember, learned Latin out of Descartes’ *Meditationes*, and I, in place of the little tales which children are usually told to read, had nothing more charming than a translation of the *Gesta Romanorum*. We grew up thus, quiet and studious children, and in course of time my brother provided for himself in the manner I have mentioned. I continued to live at home; my poor mother had become an invalid, and demanded my continual care, and about two years ago she died after many months of painful illness. My situation was a terrible one; the shabby furniture barely sufficed to pay the debts I had been forced to contract, and the books I despatched to my brother, knowing how he would value them. I was absolutely alone. I was aware how poorly my brother was paid; and though I came up to London in the hope of finding employment, with the understanding that he would defray my expenses, I swore it should only be for a month, and that if I could not in that time find some work, I would starve rather than deprive him of the few miserable pounds he had laid by for his day of trouble. I took a little room in a distant suburb, the cheapest that I could find. I lived on bread and tea, and I spent my time in vain answering of advertisements, and vainer walks to addresses I had noted. Day followed on day, and week on week, and still I was unsuccessful, till at last the term I had appointed drew to a close, and I saw before me the grim prospect of slowly dying of starvation. My landlady was good-natured in her way; she knew the slenderness of my means, and I am sure that she would not have turned me out of doors. It remained for me then to go away, and to try and die in some quiet place. It was winter then, and a thick white fog gathered in the early part of the afternoon, becoming more dense as the day wore on; it was a Sunday, I remember, and the people of the house were at chapel. At about three o’clock I crept out and walked away as quickly as I could, for I was weak from abstinence. The white mist wrapped all the streets in silence, and a hard frost had gathered thick upon the bare branches of the trees, and frost crystals glittered on the wooden fences, and on the cold cruel ground beneath my feet. I walked on, turning to right and left in utter haphazard, without caring to look up at the names of the streets, and all that I remember of my walk on that Sunday afternoon seems but the broken fragments of an evil dream. In a confused vision I stumbled on, through roads half town and half country; gray fields melting into the cloudy world of mist on one side of me, and on the other comfortable villas with a glow of firelight flickering on the walls; but all unreal, red brick walls, and lighted windows, vague trees, and glimmering country, gas-lamps beginning to star the white shadows, the vanishing perspectives of the railway line beneath high embankments, the green and red of the signal lamps﻿—all these were but momentary pictures flashed on my tired brain and senses numbed by hunger. Now and then I would hear a quick step ringing on the iron road, and men would pass me well wrapped up, walking fast for the sake of warmth, and no doubt eagerly foretasting the pleasures of a glowing hearth, with curtains tightly drawn about the frosted panes, and the welcomes of their friends; but as the early evening darkened and night approached, foot-passengers got fewer and fewer, and I passed through street after street alone. In the white silence I stumbled on, as desolate as if I trod the streets of a buried city; and as I grew more weak and exhausted, something of the horror of death was folding thickly round my heart. Suddenly, as I turned a corner, someone accosted me courteously beneath the lamppost, and I heard a voice asking if I could kindly point the way to Avon Road. At the sudden shock of human accents I was prostrated and my strength gave way, and I fell all huddled on the sidewalk and wept and sobbed and laughed in violent hysteria. I had gone out prepared to die, and as I stepped across the threshold that had sheltered me, I consciously bade adieu to all hopes and all remembrances; the door clanged behind me with the noise of thunder, and I felt that an iron curtain had fallen on the brief passages of my life, and that henceforth I was to walk a little way in a world of gloom and shadow; I entered on the stage of the first act of death. Then came my wandering in the mist, the whiteness wrapping all things, the void streets, and muffled silence, till when that voice spoke to me, it was as if I had died and life returned to me. In a few minutes I was able to compose my feelings, and as I rose I saw that I was confronted by a middle-aged gentleman of specious appearance, neatly and correctly dressed. He looked at me with an expression of great pity, but before I could stammer out my ignorance of the neighborhood, for indeed I had not the slightest notion of where I had wandered, he spoke.

===Chinese===
此刻，我必须把我的身世讲得更详细些。我是一位土木工程师的女儿，父亲名叫史蒂文·拉利（Steven Lally），不幸在事业刚起步时猝然离世，未曾积攒下足以养活妻子和两个孩子的家资。母亲勉力维持着这个小家，靠着那份想必微薄得难以置信的进项；我们住在一个偏僻的乡村，因为过活的大部分必需品都比城里便宜；纵然如此，我们仍是在最严苛的节俭中被抚养大的。父亲是个聪明而博览的人，身后留下一批数量不多却精心拣择的藏书，尽是希腊、拉丁与英文典籍中的上品；这些书便是我们仅有的消遣。我记得，我的兄弟是捧着笛卡儿的《沉思录》（*Meditationes*）学会拉丁文的；而我，代替孩子们常读的那些小故事，手边最迷人的，不过是一部《罗马人功业录》（*Gesta Romanorum*）的译本。我们就这样长成了一双安静好学的孩子；年岁渐长，兄弟便按我方才说过的方式自谋生路去了。我继续住在家里；可怜的母亲已成残疾之人，须我时时照料；大约两年前，她在缠绵数月的病痛之后故去。我的处境可怕之至：寒伧的家具，仅仅够抵我被迫欠下的债务；那些书，我寄给了兄弟，因为我知道他会如何珍视它们。我是彻底地孤身一人了。我深知兄弟的薪俸何其微薄；虽说怀着觅得职业的希望进了伦敦，讲定由他为我垫付开销，我却发誓这只能维持一个月；倘若一个月内寻不到差事，我宁可饿死，也不肯夺去他为自己患难之日积下的那可怜的几镑。我在远郊租了个小间，是我能寻到的最便宜的。我靠面包和茶过活，把光阴耗费在徒然的应征作答，和更加徒然的、按记下的地址逐处走访之中。一日复一日，一星期复一星期，我依然一无所成，直到自定的期限将满，眼前现出那幅慢慢饿死的惨淡前景。我的女房东为人心地不坏；她知道我手头如何拮据，我确信她绝不会把我扫地出门。那么，剩下的便只有我自己走开，去寻个僻静的地方默默死去。那时正是冬天，午后刚过半，便聚起一片浓白的大雾，天色愈晚，雾也愈沉；我记得那是个星期日，房东一家都上礼拜堂去了。三点钟光景，我悄悄溜出门，尽快地朝前走去——长期的绝食已使我虚弱不堪。白雾把条条街道裹进寂静里；浓霜厚厚地凝在树木光秃的枝干上，凝在木栅栏上，凝在我脚下冰冷无情的地面上。我一路走去，忽而向右、忽而向左，全凭瞎碰，也懒得抬头去看街名；那个星期日下午我一路的所见，如今忆来，无非是一场噩梦的残章断片。我恍恍惚惚地跌撞前行，穿过半城半乡的道路：一侧是灰色的田野，消融进雾气茫茫的天地；另一侧是温暖舒适的别墅，炉火的光晕在墙上摇曳；然而一切都是虚影——红砖墙、亮着灯的窗、朦胧的树、微光的郊野，煤气灯开始在白茫茫的阴影里点起星子，高高的路堤下铁路线的透视渐渐消失，信号灯的绿与红——这一切，不过是闪在我疲惫的头脑和饿木的感官上的瞬息画面罢了。时而有一阵急促的脚步在石路上铿然作响，裹得严严实实的男人们为取暖而疾行，从我身边走过，无疑正热切地预尝着家的滋味：炉火熊熊，窗帘把结霜的窗格遮得严严实实，还有亲友的欢迎；可随着暮色四合、夜幕将临，行人愈来愈少，我一条街又一条街地独自穿行。我在白色的寂静里踉跄前行，凄凉得仿佛踩着一座湮没之城的街道；而随着我愈发虚弱疲惫，死亡的恐怖也一层层厚厚地裹上我的心头。蓦地，我一拐过街角，路灯下有人客气地同我搭话，一个声音问我，可否劳驾指点去埃文路（Avon Road）的路。骤然听见人声，我一下子瘫软了，力气尽失，蜷缩着跌倒在人行道上，又哭又抽又笑，癔症发作得一塌糊涂。我出门时本是抱着求死之心；跨出那道庇护过我的门槛时，我有意识地同一切希望、一切记忆诀别；门在身后铿然关上，声如雷霆；我感到一道铁幕已落在我生命短短的几幕之上，从此我只须在幽暗与阴影的世界里再走一小程；我踏上了死亡第一幕的舞台。随后便是在雾中的游荡，白色裹住万物，空洞的街道，窒闷的寂静——直到那个声音向我开口，我才仿佛死而复生，生命重新回到我身上。几分钟之后，我镇定下来；起身时，只见面前立着一位人到中年的绅士，外表看似可靠，衣着整洁而得体。他以满怀怜悯的神情望着我；不等我结结巴巴说出自己对左近的全然陌生——我实在全然不知自己游荡到了何处——他先开了口。

===Original===
“My dear madam,” he said, “you seem in some terrible distress. You cannot think how you alarmed me. But may I inquire the nature of your trouble? I assure you that you can safely confide in me.”

“You are very kind,” I replied; “but I fear there is nothing to be done. My condition seems a hopeless one.”

“Oh, nonsense, nonsense! You are too young to talk like that. Come, let us walk down here, and you must tell me your difficulty. Perhaps I may be able to help you.”

===Chinese===
“亲爱的夫人，”他说，“您似乎陷在某种可怕的痛苦里。您想不到您把我吓成了什么样。可否容我问一句，您遭了什么难？我向您担保，放心把心事告诉我，绝无妨碍。”

“您真好，”我答道，“可是我怕，已经没有什么可做的了。我的处境，怕是无望的了。”

“哦，胡说，胡说！您这般年轻，不该说这样的话。来，咱们往这边走走，您务必把您的难处告诉我。或许我还帮得上忙呢。”

===Original===
There was something very soothing and persuasive in his manner, and as we walked together, I gave him an outline of my story, and told of the despair that had oppressed me almost to death.

“You were wrong to give in so completely,” he said, when I was silent. “A month is too short a time in which to feel one’s way in London. London, let me tell you, Miss Lally, does not lie open and undefended; it is a fortified place, fossed and double-moated with curious intricacies. As must always happen in large towns, the conditions of life have become hugely artificial; no mere simple palisade is run up to oppose the man or woman who would take the place by storm, but serried lines of subtle contrivances, mines, and pitfalls which it needs a strange skill to overcome. You, in your simplicity, fancied you had only to shout for these walls to sink into nothingness, but the time is gone for such startling victories as these. Take courage; you will learn the secret of success before very long.”

===Chinese===
他的态度里，有一种极能安人、极善劝诱的东西；一路同行，我把自己的遭遇讲了个大概，也讲了那几乎把我压垮的绝望。

“你这样彻底缴械，就错了，”我讲完了，他说，“一个月，要在伦敦里摸出路来，实在太短。让我告诉你吧，拉利小姐（Miss Lally），伦敦并不是敞开大门、不设防的地方；它是一座要塞，环着一重壕堑，又围着错综至极的双重护城河。大都会的通例如此：生活的条件已变得极端造作；要拦住那些想强攻进城的男人女人，再不是竖一道简单的栅栏，而是一列又一列精巧的机关、地雷与陷阱，须得一身奇技方能越渡。你呢，凭你的天真，还以为只消喊上一声，这些城墙便会应声夷为平地；可惜那样惊人的大捷，如今的时代已经过去了。鼓起勇气来；用不了多久，你就会学到成功的秘诀。”

===Original===
“Alas, sir,” I replied, “I have no doubt your conclusions are correct, but at the present moment I seem to be in a fair way to die of starvation. You spoke of a secret; for heaven’s sake, tell it me, if you have any pity for my distress.”

He laughed genially. “There lies the strangeness of it all. Those who know the secret cannot tell it if they would; it is positively as ineffable as the central doctrine of Freemasonry. But I may say this, that you yourself have penetrated at least the outer husk of the mystery,” and he laughed again.

“Pray do not jest with me,” I said. “What have I done, que sais-je? I am so far ignorant that I have not the slightest idea of how my next meal is to be provided.”

===Chinese===
“唉，先生，”我答道，“您的高论，我毫不怀疑是对的；可眼下，我眼看就要饿死了。您说到一个秘诀；看在老天爷的分上，告诉我吧，倘若您对我这苦境还有半分怜悯。”

他和蔼地笑了。“奥妙正在这里。知道秘诀的人，想说也说不出；它简直就像共济会（Freemasonry）的核心教义一样，不可言传。不过我可以说一句：您自己呢，至少已经钻透了这重神秘的外壳。”说罢，他又笑了。

“请别拿我打趣了，”我说，“我做过什么呢，我哪知道（que sais-je）？我无知得很，连下一顿饭从何而来，都毫无头绪。”

===Original===
“Excuse me. You ask what you have done? You have met me. Come, we will fence no longer. I see you have self-education, the only education which is not infinitely pernicious, and I am in want of a governess for my two children. I have been a widower for some years; my name is Gregg. I offer you the post I have named, and shall we say a salary of a hundred a year?”

I could only stutter out my thanks, and slipping a card with his address and a banknote by way of earnest into my hand, Mr. Gregg bade me goodbye, asking me to call in a day or two.

===Chinese===
“恕我直言。您问您做过什么？您遇见了我。来吧，咱们不必再击剑了。我看出您受过自我教育——唯一一种不是遗害无穷的教育——而我正为我的两个孩子觅一位家庭女教师。我鳏居多年了；敝姓格雷格。我方才说的职位，奉献于您；年薪，我们就算一百镑，如何？”

我只会结结巴巴地道谢；格雷格先生把一张写着地址的名片，还有一张权充定金的钞票，塞进我手里，向我道了别，请我一两天内过访。

===Original===
Such was my introduction to Professor Gregg, and can you wonder that the remembrance of despair and the cold blast that had blown from the gates of death upon me, made me regard him as a second father? Before the close of the week I was installed in my new duties; the professor had leased an old brick manor house in a western suburb of London, and here, surrounded by pleasant lawns and orchards, and soothed with the murmur of the ancient elms that rocked their boughs above the roof, the new chapter of my life began. Knowing as you do the nature of the professor’s occupations, you will not be surprised to hear that the house teemed with books; and cabinets full of strange and even hideous objects filled every available nook in the vast low rooms. Gregg was a man whose one thought was for knowledge, and I too before long caught something of his enthusiasm, and strove to enter into his passion for research. In a few months I was perhaps more his secretary than the governess of the two children, and many a night I have sat at the desk in the glow of the shaded lamp while he, pacing up and down in the rich gloom of the firelight, dictated to me the substance of his *Textbook of Ethnology*. But amidst these more sober and accurate studies I always detected a something hidden, a longing and desire for some object to which he did not allude, and now and then he would break short in what he was saying and lapse into revery, entranced, as it seemed to me, by some distant prospect of adventurous discovery. The textbook was at last finished, and we began to receive proofs from the printers, which were entrusted to me for a first reading, and then underwent the final revision of the professor. All the while his weariness of the actual business he was engaged on increased, and it was with the joyous laugh of a schoolboy when term is over that he one day handed me a copy of the book. “There,” he said, “I have kept my word; I promised to write it, and it is done with. Now I shall be free to live for stranger things; I confess it, Miss Lally, I covet the renown of Columbus. You will, I hope, see me play the part of an explorer.”

===Chinese===
我同格雷格教授的相识便是如此；想到那场绝望，想到从死亡之门吹到我身上的那阵冷风，您还会奇怪我把他视作第二个父亲么？不出一星期，我已就任新职；教授在伦敦西郊租下一所古老的砖造庄园宅邸；在这里，四周是宜人的草坪与果园，古榆的喧声安抚着屋顶上摇曳的枝柯，我生命的新一章开始了。您既知道教授治学的路数，便不会奇怪听说这宅子里书满为患；一间间低矮宽敞的屋子里，凡是能落脚的角落，都立满了装着奇异、甚至可怖之物的柜子。格雷格这个人，一心只在学问上；不久我也沾染了他的热忱，勉力领会他研究的痴兴。不出数月，我与其说是两个孩子的家庭教师，毋宁说更是他的秘书；多少个夜晚，我在灯罩的柔光里伏案而坐，他在火光沉沉的红晕中踱来踱去，向我口授《民族学教科书》的内容。然而在这些冷静而精确的研治之外，我始终察觉有一样藏着的东西，一桩他从不提起的渴慕与期求；时不时，他会说到半途蓦地收住，坠入出神，仿佛被某幅探险发现的远景迷住了。教科书终于完稿，印厂的校样开始寄到，交我初读，再由教授终审定夺。与此同时，他对手头这份实际差事的厌倦与日俱增；待到有一天他把一册新书递到我手里，那份欢喜的笑，竟像学期告终的学童。“喏，”他说，“我不曾失信；我答应了写它，如今写完了，从此撒手。往后我可要为更奇异的事物活着了；我承认，拉利小姐，我觊觎哥伦布的荣名。您将有幸看到我扮演一个探险家。”

===Original===
“Surely,” I said, “there is little left to explore. You have been born a few hundred years too late for that.”

“I think you are wrong,” he replied; “there are still, depend upon it, quaint undiscovered countries and continents of strange extent. Ah, Miss Lally, believe me, we stand amidst sacraments and mysteries full of awe, and it doth not yet appear what we shall be. Life, believe me, is no simple thing, no mass of gray matter and congeries of veins and muscles to be laid naked by the surgeon’s knife; man is the secret which I am about to explore, and before I can discover him I must cross over weltering seas indeed, and oceans and the mists of many thousand years. You know the myth of the lost Atlantis; what if it be true, and I am destined to be called the discoverer of that wonderful land?”

I could see excitement boiling beneath his words, and in his face was the heat of the hunter; before me stood a man who believed himself summoned to tourney with the unknown. A pang of joy possessed me when I reflected that I was to be in a way associated with him in the adventure, and I too burned with the lust of the chase, not pausing to consider that I knew not what we were to unshadow.

===Chinese===
“真的么，”我说，“这世上还有什么可探险的。您生晚了那么几百年，赶不上那个时代了。”

“我看你错了，”他答道，“你尽管相信：世上仍有未被发现的奇妙国度，仍有疆域奇广的大陆。啊，拉利小姐，相信我，我们正站在满是敬畏的圣礼与奥秘之中，而我们将来如何，还未显明。生命，请相信我，绝不是一件简单的东西，不是一团灰质、一堆静脉与肌肉，任凭外科医生的刀一剖便赤裸摊开；人才是我要去探究的秘密；而要寻到他，我须横渡的何止是翻腾的大海，还有重洋，还有几千年的迷雾。你知道失落的亚特兰蒂斯（Atlantis）的神话；倘若它是真的呢？倘若命定由我来做那片奇土的发现者呢？”

我看得见他言语底下沸腾的兴奋；他脸上有猎人的灼热；立在我面前的，是一个自信奉了召唤、要去同未知决斗的人。想到我在这场奇险里多少要与他为伴，一阵欢悦攫住了我；我也燃起追猎的欲火，全不曾停下来想想：我们要揭开的究竟是什么，我一无所知。

===Original===
The next morning Professor Gregg took me into his inner study, where ranged against the wall stood a nest of pigeonholes, every drawer neatly labelled, and the results of years of toil classified in a few feet of space.

“Here,” he said, “is my life; here are all the facts which I have gathered together with so much pains, and yet it is all nothing. No, nothing to what I am about to attempt. Look at this”; and he took me to an old bureau, a piece fantastic and faded, which stood in a corner of the room. He unlocked the front and opened one of the drawers.

===Chinese===
第二天早晨，格雷格教授把我领进他的内书房；靠墙立着一组鸽笼式的分格柜，每个抽屉都贴着整齐的标签，多年辛劳的成果，分门别类收在这几英尺见方的空间里。

“喏，”他说，“这是我的一生；我煞费苦心搜罗来的全部事实都在这里，然而这全是虚无。不，同我即将着手的事业相比，什么也不是。看这个。”他领我到一张旧写字柜前——一件样式奇异、颜色黯淡的家具，立在屋角。他打开柜面上的锁，拉开一只抽屉。

===Original===
“A few scraps of paper,” he went on, pointing to the drawer, “and a lump of black stone, rudely annotated with queer marks and scratches﻿—that is all that drawer holds. Here you see is an old envelope with the dark red stamp of twenty years ago, but I have pencilled a few lines at the back; here is a sheet of manuscript, and here some cuttings from obscure local journals. And if you ask me the subject matter of the collection, it will not seem extraordinary. A servant girl at a farmhouse, who disappeared from her place and has never been heard of, a child supposed to have slipped down some old working on the mountains, some queer scribbling on a limestone rock, a man murdered with a blow from a strange weapon; such is the scent I have to go upon. Yes, as you say, there is a ready explanation for all this; the girl may have run away to London, or Liverpool, or New York; the child may be at the bottom of the disused shaft; and the letters on the rock may be the idle whims of some vagrant. Yes, yes, I admit all that; but I know I hold the true key. Look!” and he held me out a slip of yellow paper.

“Characters found inscribed on a limestone rock on the Gray Hills,” I read, and then there was a word erased, presumably the name of a county, and a date some fifteen years back. Beneath was traced a number of uncouth characters, shaped somewhat like wedges or daggers, as strange and outlandish as the Hebrew alphabet.

“Now the seal,” said Professor Gregg, and he handed me the black stone, a thing about two inches long, and something like an old-fashioned tobacco stopper, much enlarged.

I held it up to the light, and saw to my surprise the characters on the paper repeated on the seal.

===Chinese===
“几片碎纸，”他指着抽屉继续说，“还有一块黑石头，粗粗刻满了古怪的记号与划痕——那抽屉里装的，便尽在于此。您看，这里是一只旧信封，贴着二十年前的暗红邮票，背面我用铅笔注了几行；这里是一页手稿，这里是从偏僻地方报刊上剪下的几段。您若问我这批收藏所涉何事，听来绝不会觉得离奇。一个农庄上的女仆，从岗位上失踪，从此杳无音讯；一个孩子，据说是失足滑进了山上的某口旧矿坑；石灰岩上几行古怪的涂写；一个人被一件古怪的兵器一击毙命——这便是我凭以追踪的气味。是啊，如您所说，这一切都不乏现成的解释：那姑娘或许是私逃去了伦敦、利物浦或纽约；那孩子或许就沉在那口废矿坑的底上；岩上的字迹，或许不过是哪个流浪汉无聊的胡写。是，是，这些我统统承认；可我知道，我握着真正的钥匙。看！”他向我递来一张发黄的纸条。

“石灰岩上所见之铭文，灰山（the Gray Hills），”我念道；随后有一个被涂去的词，想必是某郡的郡名，再往下是约莫十五年前的日期。底下描着一行拙劣的字形，略似楔子，又似短剑，正如希伯来字母那样古怪而异样。

“现在看这方印，”格雷格教授说着，把那黑石头递给我：约莫两英寸长，样子像一枚老式的压烟器，只是放大了许多。

我把它举向光处，惊讶地看见，纸上那些字形，在印上一模一样地重现着。

===Original===
“Yes,” said the professor, “they are the same. And the marks on the limestone rock were made fifteen years ago, with some red substance. And the characters on the seal are four thousand years old at least. Perhaps much more.”

“Is it a hoax?” I said.

“No, I anticipated that. I was not to be led to give my life to a practical joke. I have tested the matter very carefully. Only one person besides myself knows of the mere existence of that black seal. Besides, there are other reasons which I cannot enter into now.”

“But what does it all mean?” I said. “I cannot understand to what conclusion all this leads.”

===Chinese===
“是的，”教授说，“它们是一样的。石灰岩上那些字迹，是十五年前用某种红色物质写下的。而印上的这些字符，至少已有四千年。或许远不止。”

“这是不是一场骗局？”我说。

“不，这一层我早料到了。我还不至于被人牵着，把一生押给一个恶作剧。这事我验证得极其仔细。除我之外，只有一个人知道那方黑印的存在。此外还有别样缘故，眼下无从细说。”

“可这一切究竟是什么意思？”我说，“我实在不明白，这一切要引向怎样的结论。”

===Original===
“My dear Miss Lally, that is a question I would rather leave unanswered for some little time. Perhaps I shall never be able to say what secrets are held here in solution; a few vague hints, the outlines of village tragedies, a few marks done with red earth upon a rock, and an ancient seal. A queer set of data to go upon? Half-a-dozen pieces of evidence, and twenty years before even so much could be got together; and who knows what mirage or terra incognita may be beyond all this? I look across deep waters, Miss Lally, and the land beyond may be but a haze after all. But still I believe it is not so, and a few months will show whether I am right or wrong.”

He left me, and alone I endeavored to fathom the mystery, wondering to what goal such eccentric odds and ends of evidence could lead. I myself am not wholly devoid of imagination, and I had reason to respect the professor’s solidity of intellect; yet I saw in the contents of the drawer but the materials of fantasy, and vainly tried to conceive what theory could be founded on the fragments that had been placed before me. Indeed, I could discover in what I had heard and seen but the first chapter of an extravagant romance; and yet deep in my heart I burned with curiosity, and day after day I looked eagerly in Professor Gregg’s face for some hint of what was to happen.

===Chinese===
“亲爱的拉利小姐，这个问题，我宁可再搁一搁。或许我永远说不出，这里面溶解着怎样的秘密：几缕模糊的暗示，几桩乡村惨剧的轮廓，几笔红土画在岩石上的记号，还有一方古印。凭这么一批材料起步，岂不荒唐？六件证据，前后竟用了二十年才凑齐；而这一切之外，谁知道是海市蜃楼，还是一片未知之地（terra incognita）呢？我隔着深水眺望，拉利小姐；彼岸的陆地，或许终究只是一片朦胧。但我仍然相信并非如此；不出几个月，便见分晓，看我是对是错。”

他离我而去；我独自试着参透这重神秘，纳闷这样一堆离奇的零碎证据，究竟能通向何处。我并非全无想象力的人，也自有理由敬重教授智识的坚实；然而在我眼里，抽屉里那些东西，终究只是幻想的素材；我徒然揣想，面前这几件残片之上，究竟能立起怎样一套理论。的确，我在所见所闻之中，只寻出一部荒唐传奇的第一章；然而我心深处，好奇之火灼灼燃烧，日复一日，我急切地在格雷格教授脸上搜寻，盼寻出一点大事件将临的征兆。

===Original===
It was one evening after dinner that the word came.

“I hope you can make your preparations without much trouble,” he said suddenly to me. “We shall be leaving here in a week’s time.”

“Really!” I said in astonishment. “Where are we going?”

“I have taken a country house in the west of England, not far from Caermaen, a quiet little town, once a city, and the headquarters of a Roman legion. It is very dull there, but the country is pretty, and the air is wholesome.”

===Chinese===
事情是在一天晚饭之后传来的。

“但愿您收拾行装不至太费事，”他忽然对我说，“不出一个星期，我们就要离开这里了。”

“真的！”我惊异地说，“我们要去哪里？”

“我在英格兰西部租了一所乡宅，离凯尔马恩（Caermaen）不远；那是个安静的小镇，从前是座城市，还是罗马一个军团的驻地。那里很沉闷，但乡野秀美，空气宜人。”

===Original===
I detected a glint in his eyes, and guessed that this sudden move had some relation to our conversation of a few days before.

“I shall just take a few books with me,” said Professor Gregg, “that is all. Everything else will remain here for our return. I have got a holiday,” he went on, smiling at me, “and I shan’t be sorry to be quit for a time of my old bones and stones and rubbish. Do you know,” he went on, “I have been grinding away at facts for thirty years; it is time for fancies.”

===Chinese===
我察觉他眼中一闪，猜到这趟突然的迁居，同几天前我们的那番谈话不无干系。

“我只带几本书去，”格雷格教授说，“就这些。其余一切原封不动，留着我们回来。我得了个假期，”他含笑望着我，继续说，“暂时撇开我那些老骨头、老石头和破烂货，我求之不得。你可知道，”他又说，“我在事实上磨了三十年；该轮到幻想了。”

===Original===
The days passed quickly; I could see that the professor was all quivering with suppressed excitement, and I could scarce credit the eager appetence of his glance as we left the old manor house behind us, and began our journey. We set out at midday, and it was in the dusk of the evening that we arrived at a little country station. I was tired and excited, and the drive through the lanes seems all a dream. First the deserted streets of a forgotten village, while I heard Professor Gregg’s voice talking of the Augustan Legion and the clash of arms, and all the tremendous pomp that followed the eagles; then the broad river swimming to full tide with the last afterglow glimmering duskily in the yellow water, the wide meadows, and the cornfields whitening, and the deep lane winding on the slope between the hills and the water. At last we began to ascend, and the air grew rarer; I looked down and saw the pure white mist tracking the outline of the river like a shroud, and a vague and shadowy country, imaginations and fantasy of swelling hills and hanging woods, and half-shaped outlines of hills beyond, and in the distance the glare of the furnace fire on the mountain, growing by turns a pillar of shining flame, and fading to a dull point of red. We were slowly mounting a carriage drive, and then there came to me the cool breath and the scent of the great wood that was above us; I seemed to wander in its deepest depths, and there was the sound of trickling water, the scent of the green leaves, and the breath of the summer night. The carriage stopped at last, and I could scarcely distinguish the form of the house as I waited a moment at the pillared porch; and the rest of the evening seemed a dream of strange things bounded by the great silence of the wood and the valley and the river.

===Chinese===
日子过得飞快；我看得出，教授浑身颤动着压抑的兴奋；当我们把古老的庄园宅第抛在身后、启程上路时，他眼中那急切的渴望，我几乎不敢相信。我们正午动身，待到暮色四合，才抵达一个小小的乡间车站。我又倦又奋，穿巷而行的那段车程，恍如一场梦。先是被人遗忘的村庄里荒凉的空街，耳边是格雷格教授的声音，谈着奥古斯都军团（Augustan Legion），谈着金戈的铿锵，谈着随鹰旗而行的全部赫赫威仪；随后是宽阔的大河涨满潮水，最后一抹余晖在黄色的水面上幽幽闪烁，是辽阔的草场、渐白的麦田，是山与水之间蜿蜒于坡上的深巷。末了我们开始攀登，空气渐见清冽；我俯瞰下去，只见纯白的雾沿河勾出一条轮廓，宛如尸衣，一片朦胧而多影的乡野，隆起的冈峦与悬垂的林木尽是想象与奇幻，更远处是隐隐成形的山的轮廓，而天边山头高炉的火光，时而涨成一根通亮的火柱，时而又缩作一点暗红。我们缓缓驶上一条私家车道，随即，头顶上那片大林子清凉的气息与芬芳便扑到我身上；我仿佛游荡在它最深最深处，有涓涓水声，有绿叶的香气，有夏夜的呼吸。马车终于停了；我在立柱门廊前伫立片刻，几乎辨不清宅子的轮廓；那一晚余下的时光恍如一场奇事之梦，四面包拢它的，是树林、山谷与河流的大寂静。

===Original===
The next morning when I awoke and looked out of the bow window of the big old-fashioned bedroom, I saw under a gray sky a country that was still all mystery. The long, lovely valley, with the river winding in and out below, crossed in mid vision by a medieval bridge of vaulted and buttressed stone, the clear presence of the rising ground beyond, and the woods that I had only seen in shadow the night before, seemed tinged with enchantment, and the soft breath of air that sighed in at the opened pane was like no other wind. I looked across the valley, and beyond, hill followed on hill as wave on wave, and here a faint blue pillar of smoke rose still in the morning air from the chimney of an ancient gray farmhouse, there was a rugged height crowned with dark firs, and in the distance I saw the white streak of a road that climbed and vanished into some unimagined country. But the boundary of all was a great wall of mountain, vast in the west, and ending like a fortress with a steep ascent and a domed tumulus clear against the sky.

===Chinese===
第二天早晨我醒来，从那间老式大卧房的凸窗外望去，灰蒙蒙的天空底下，是一片依旧满是神秘的乡野。修长可爱的山谷，河流在下蜿蜒出入，视野中央横着一座中世纪的石桥，有拱有垛；近处冈峦清晰地耸立，昨夜只见阴影的树林，此刻似都染上了魔法；敞开的窗棂间叹进来的那一缕柔风，与世上任何风都不同。我望过山谷，山谷之外，山峦相逐如层浪；这里，一柱淡蓝的炊烟在晨空中袅袅不散，从一座古老的灰色农舍的烟囱里升起；那里，一座嶙峋的高冈戴着一顶黑黢黢的冷杉；更远处，我望见一线白色的道路盘旋而上，消失在某个想象不到的国度里。而这一切的边界，是一堵大山之墙，在西面浩浩荡荡，末端如一座堡垒，一道陡坡直上，一座穹顶的古冢衬着天色，轮廓分明。

===Original===
I saw Professor Gregg walking up and down the terrace path below the windows, and it was evident that he was revelling in the sense of liberty, and the thought that he had, for a while, bidden goodbye to task-work. When I joined him there was exultation in his voice as he pointed out the sweep of valley and the river that wound beneath the lovely hills.

“Yes,” he said, “it is a strangely beautiful country; and to me, at least, it seems full of mystery. You have not forgotten the drawer I showed you, Miss Lally? No; and you have guessed that I have come here not merely for the sake of the children and the fresh air?”

“I think I have guessed as much as that,” I replied; “but you must remember I do not know the mere nature of your investigations; and as for the connection between the search and this wonderful valley, it is past my guessing.”

===Chinese===
我看见格雷格教授正在窗下的露台小径上来回踱步；显然，他正陶然于自由之感，陶然于这样一个念头：他已暂时向差事告了假。我走到他身边时，他指点着绵延的山谷、在可爱的群山之下蜿蜒的河流，语声里满是得意。

“是啊，”他说，“这是个美得奇异的地方；至少在我看来，它满是神秘。我给您看过的那只抽屉，您没有忘吧，拉利小姐？没有；而且您已经猜到，我到这里来，并不单是为了孩子们和新鲜空气？”

“我想我猜到了这一层，”我答道，“但您须记得，您研究的实质，我尚一无所知；至于这番搜求同这条奇妙山谷之间的关联，那是我猜不出的。”

===Original===
He smiled queerly at me. “You must not think I am making a mystery for the sake of mystery,” he said. “I do not speak out because, so far, there is nothing to be spoken, nothing definite I mean, nothing that can be set down in hard black and white, as dull and sure and irreproachable as any blue book. And then I have another reason: many years ago a chance paragraph in a newspaper caught my attention, and focused in an instant the vagrant thoughts and half-formed fancies of many idle and speculative hours into a certain hypothesis. I saw at once that I was treading on a thin crust; my theory was wild and fantastic in the extreme, and I would not for any consideration have written a hint of it for publication. But I thought that in the company of scientific men like myself, men who knew the course of discovery, and were aware that the gas that blazes and flares in the gin-palace was once a wild hypothesis; I thought that with such men as these I might hazard my dream﻿—let us say Atlantis, or the philosopher’s stone, or what you like﻿—without danger of ridicule. I found I was grossly mistaken; my friends looked blankly at me and at one another, and I could see something of pity, and something also of insolent contempt, in the glances they exchanged. One of them called on me next day, and hinted that I must be suffering from overwork and brain exhaustion. ‘In plain terms,’ I said, ‘you think I am going mad. I think not’; and I showed him out with some little appearance of heat. Since that day I vowed that I would never whisper the nature of my theory to any living soul; to no one but yourself have I ever shown the contents of that drawer. After all, I may be following a rainbow; I may have been misled by the play of coincidence; but as I stand here in this mystic hush and silence amidst the woods and wild hills, I am more than ever sure that I am hot on the scent. Come, it is time we went in.”

===Chinese===
他朝我古怪地一笑。“您切莫以为，我是为神秘而故弄玄虚，”他说，“我所以不说破，是因为至今还无话可说——我是说，没有确定的东西，没有能落在冷硬的黑字白纸上的东西，不像蓝皮书那样枯燥、确凿、无可指摘。再者我还有一重缘故：多年以前，报上一段偶然的文字攫住了我的注意，顷刻之间，把许多闲散沉思的时辰里游移的念头与半成的幻想，聚成了一个假设。我当即明白，自己是踩在一层薄壳上；我的理论狂乱荒诞到了极点，无论出什么代价，我也绝不肯写下其中一丝一毫拿去发表。但我原想，在同我一样的科学之士中间——他们知道发现之路的坎坷，也知道此刻在杜松子酒肆（gin-palace）里熊熊烧着的煤气，当年也曾是一个狂放的假设——同这样的人在一起，我或许可以斗胆说出我的梦——就说是亚特兰蒂斯，或是点金石，或是随便什么——而不至于招来嘲弄。我发现我大错特错；我的朋友们面面相觑，茫然望着我，从他们交换的眼色里，我看得出几分怜悯，还有几分无礼的轻蔑。第二天就有一个人登门看我，暗示我必是操劳过度、脑力枯竭了。‘说白了，’我说，‘你们是以为我要疯了。我看未必。’我带着几分愠色把他送了出去。自那日起我立誓，再不向任何活人透露我理论的实质；除了您，我从没给任何人看过那只抽屉里的东西。说到底，我或许只是在追逐一道彩虹；我或许是被巧合的把戏引入了歧途；可如今，站在这树林与荒山之间的一片神秘的静穆里，我比以往任何时候都更确信：气味正热。来吧，该进屋去了。”

===Original===
To me in all this there was something both of wonder and excitement; I knew how in his ordinary work Professor Gregg moved step by step, testing every inch of the way, and never venturing on assertion without proof that was impregnable. Yet I divined more from his glance and the vehemence of his tone than from the spoken word that he had in his every thought the vision of the almost incredible continually with him; and I, who was with some share of imagination no little of a sceptic, offended at a hint of the marvellous, could not help asking myself whether he was cherishing a monomania, and barring out from this one subject all the scientific method of his other life.

===Chinese===
这一切于我，既新奇又令人激动；我知道，格雷格教授平日治学，是寸寸为营、步步验证，没有攻不破的证据，绝不下断语。然而从他的一瞥、从他语调的热切里，我察知的比他的言词更多：那几乎不可置信的幻象，无时无刻不随在他每一个念头里；而我——虽有几分想象力、却也颇为怀疑、闻“奇”即嗤的人——不禁自问：他是否正豢养着一桩偏执的痴迷，是否单单把这一题目，关在了他毕生科学方法的门外。

===Original===
Yet, with this image of mystery haunting my thoughts, I surrendered wholly to the charm of the country. Above the faded house on the hillside began the great forest; a long dark line seen from the opposing hills, stretching above the river for many a mile from north to south, and yielding in the north to even wilder country, barren and savage hills, and ragged common land, a territory all strange and unvisited, and more unknown to Englishmen than the very heart of Africa. The space of a couple of steep fields alone separated the house from the wood, and the children were delighted to follow me up the long alleys of undergrowth, between smooth pleached walls of shining beech, to the highest point in the wood, whence one looked on one side across the river and the rise and fall of the country to the great western mountain wall, and on the other, over the surge and dip of the myriad trees of the forest, over level meadows and the shining yellow sea to the faint coast beyond. I used to sit at this point on the warm sunlit turf which marked the track of the Roman Road, while the two children raced about hunting for the whinberries that grew here and there on the banks. Here beneath the deep blue sky and the great clouds rolling, like olden galleons with sails full-bellied, from the sea to the hills, as I listened to the whispered charm of the great and ancient wood, I lived solely for delight, and only remembered strange things when we would return to the house, and find Professor Gregg either shut up in the little room he had made his study, or else pacing the terrace with the look, patient and enthusiastic, of the determined seeker.

===Chinese===
然而，虽有这神秘的暗影萦绕心头，我还是整个人沉醉在这乡野的魅力里了。山坡上那所褪色的宅子背后，便展开那片大森林；从对面的山上望去，它是一道绵长的黑线，沿着河岸，从南到北逶迤许多英里，到了北面，又让位于更加蛮荒的地带——贫瘠狰狞的荒山，坎坷残破的公地，一片全然陌生、无人造访的疆域，对英国人来说，比非洲的腹地还要未知。宅子与树林之间，只隔两片陡峭的田；孩子们兴高采烈，跟着我走上林下灌木丛中长长的甬道，两旁是闪闪发亮的山毛榉编成的平滑绿墙，一直走到林中的至高处；从那里，一侧望去，越过河流与起起伏伏的原野，直抵西面那堵大山之墙；另一侧望去，越过森林千万棵树的涌起与低伏，越过平坦的草场和明黄的闪亮大海，直达远方隐约的海岸。我惯常坐在那个高点上，坐在标记着罗马古道（Roman Road）轨迹的、暖洋洋的阳光草皮上，看两个孩子跑来跑去，搜寻岸边随处丛生的越橘果。在这里，深蓝的天空底下，大团的云像满帆的古老大帆船，从海上滚滚驶向群山；我听着那伟大而古老的树林低语的魔咒，只为欢愉而活着；只有当我们回到宅子、发现格雷格教授不是关在他辟作书房的小间里，便是带着那种既耐心又热烈的神情——寻索已定的人的神情——在露台上踱步时，才会记起那些怪事来。

===Original===
One morning, some eight or nine days after our arrival, I looked out of my window and saw the whole landscape transmuted before me. The clouds had dipped low and hidden the mountain in the west, and a southern wind was driving the rain in shifting pillars up the valley, and the little brooklet that burst the hill below the house now raged, a red torrent, down to the river. We were perforce obliged to keep snug within doors, and when I had attended to my pupils, I sat down in the morning-room where the ruins of a library still encumbered an old-fashioned bookcase. I had inspected the shelves once or twice, but their contents had failed to attract me; volumes of eighteenth century sermons, an old book on farriery, a collection of *Poems* by “persons of quality,” Prideaux’s *Connection*, and an odd volume of Pope were the boundaries of the library, and there seemed little doubt that everything of interest or value had been removed. Now, however, in desperation, I began to reexamine the musty sheepskin and calf bindings, and found, much to my delight, a fine old quarto printed by the Stephani, containing the three books of Pomponius Mela, *De Situ Orbis*, and other of the ancient geographers. I knew enough of Latin to steer my way through an ordinary sentence, and I soon became absorbed in the odd mixture of fact and fancy; light shining on a little of the space of the world, and beyond mist and shadow and awful forms. Glancing over the clear-printed pages, my attention was caught by the heading of a chapter in Solinus, and I read the words:﻿

===Chinese===
我们到达后约莫八九天的一个早晨，我凭窗望去，只见整片风景在我眼前变了样。云层低压，把西面的山藏了起来；南风驱赶着雨，一柱柱斜斜地送上山谷；宅子下方破山而出的小溪，如今吼成一股红色的急流，直泻入河。我们只得缩在屋里；安顿好我的两个学生，我便在晨间起居室里坐下，那里一座老式书架上，还堆着一座藏书楼的废墟。书架我已检看过一两回，里面的东西引不起我的兴致：几卷十八世纪的布道文集，一本讲马蹄铁术的古书，一部“诸名士”（persons of quality）的《诗选》（*Poems*），普里多（Prideaux）的《新旧约联结史》（*Connection*），还有零星一册蒲柏——这便是那藏书楼的疆界；毫无疑问，一切有趣或有价值的东西早被搬走了。可这会儿，出于无可奈何，我开始重新翻检那些发霉的羊皮装和小牛皮装，而令我大喜过望的是，竟翻出一部斯特法尼（Stephani）印行的精美古四开本，收着庞波尼乌斯·梅拉（Pomponius Mela）的三卷《大地志》（*De Situ Orbis*），还有其他几位古代地理学家的著作。我的拉丁文，够我在寻常句子里摸着走；不多时，我便沉浸到那事实与幻想的古怪混合里去了：光照着世上小小一角，之外便是迷雾、阴影与可怖的形体。翻着那字迹清朗的书页，索利努斯（Solinus）一章的标题攫住了我的注意，我读到了那几个字：——

===Original===
> **Mira de intimis gentibus Libyae, de lapide hexecontalitho.**

“The wonders of the people that inhabit the inner parts of Libya, and of the stone called Sixtystone.”

===Chinese===
> **Mira de intimis gentibus Libyae, de lapide hexecontalitho.**（拉丁文：利比亚腹地之民的奇事，及名为“六十石”之石。）

“利比亚腹地之民的奇事，及名为‘六十石’（Sixtystone）之石。”

===Original===
The odd title attracted me and I read on:﻿

===Chinese===
这古怪的题目吸引了我，我便读了下去：——

===Original===
> “Gens ista avia et secreta habitat, in montibus horrendis fœda mysteria celebrat. De hominibus nihil aliud illi præferunt quam figuram, ab humano ritu prorsus exulant, oderunt deum lucis. Stridunt potius quam loquuntur; vox absona nec sine horrore auditur. Lapide quodam gloriantur, quem Hexecontalithon vocant, dicunt enim hunc lapidem sexaginta notas ostendere. Cujus lapidis nomen secretum ineffabile colunt: quod Ixaxar.”

===Chinese===
> “Gens ista avia et secreta habitat, in montibus horrendis fœda mysteria celebrat. De hominibus nihil aliud illi præferunt quam figuram, ab humano ritu prorsus exulant, oderunt deum lucis. Stridunt potius quam loquuntur; vox absona nec sine horrore auditur. Lapide quodam gloriantur, quem Hexecontalithon vocant, dicunt enim hunc lapidem sexaginta notas ostendere. Cujus lapidis nomen secretum ineffabile colunt: quod Ixaxar.”（拉丁文照录；今译见下段。）

===Original===
“This folk,” I translated to myself, “dwells in remote and secret places, and celebrates foul mysteries on savage hills. Nothing have they in common with men save the face, and the customs of humanity are wholly strange to them; and they hate the sun. They hiss rather than speak; their voices are harsh, and not to be heard without fear. They boast of a certain stone, which they call Sixtystone; for they say that it displays sixty characters. And this stone has a secret unspeakable name; which is Ixaxar.”

===Chinese===
“这族类，”我在心里译道，“居于幽远隐秘之地，在狰狞的山上举行污秽的秘仪。他们与人除面孔而外别无相通；人间的习俗于他们全然隔膜；他们憎恨太阳。他们与其说是言语，毋宁说是嘶鸣；他们的嗓音刺耳，闻之不能不惧。他们夸耀一石，称之为‘六十石’；因为他们说，此石显现六十个字符。而此石有一个秘密的、不可言说的名字：即是伊克萨沙尔（Ixaxar）。”

===Original===
I laughed at the queer inconsequence of all this, and thought it fit for Sinbad the Sailor or other of the supplementary Nights. When I saw Professor Gregg in the course of the day, I told him of my find in the bookcase, and the fantastic rubbish I had been reading. To my surprise, he looked up at me with an expression of great interest.

“That is really very curious,” he said. “I have never thought it worth while to look into the old geographers, and I daresay I have missed a good deal. Ah, that is the passage, is it. It seems a shame to rob you of your entertainment, but I really think I must carry off the book.”

===Chinese===
这一切前言不搭后语的古怪，逗得我发笑；我心想，这配水手辛巴达，或配《一千零一夜》的哪篇补遗还差不多。当天晚些见到格雷格教授时，我把书架上的发现、把我读的那堆奇谈讲给他听。令我意外的是，他抬眼望我，神情大感兴趣。

“这实在非常有意思，”他说，“我从不曾想到值得去翻那些老地理学家，看来我错过了不少东西。啊，就是这一段，是么。剥夺您的消遣，未免可惜；可我真觉得，这本书非得让我抢走了。”

===Original===
The next day the professor called to me to come to the study. I found him sitting at a table in the full light of the window, scrutinizing something very attentively with a magnifying-glass.

“Ah, Miss Lally,” he began, “I want to use your eyes. This glass is pretty good, but not like my old one that I left in town. Would you mind examining the thing yourself, and telling me how many characters are cut on it?”

===Chinese===
第二天，教授唤我到书房去。只见他坐在窗前最亮的地方，正用放大镜极其专注地端详一件东西。

“啊，拉利小姐，”他开口道，“我要借重您的眼睛。这块镜片相当不错，可到底比不上我留在城里的那块老的。可否劳您亲自验看这东西，告诉我上面刻着多少个字符？”

===Original===
He handed me the object in his hand, and I saw that it was the black seal he had shown me in London, and my heart began to beat with the thought that I was presently to know something. I took the seal, and holding it up to the light checked off the grotesque dagger-shaped characters one by one.

“I make sixty-two,” I said at last.

“Sixty-two? Nonsense; it’s impossible. Ah, I see what you have done, you have counted that and that,” and he pointed to two marks which I had certainly taken as letters with the rest.

===Chinese===
他把手里的东西递给我；我认出，那正是他在伦敦给我看过的黑印；想到自己马上就要知道些什么，我的心开始怦怦地跳。我接过印，举向光处，把那一个个狰狞的短剑形字符逐一数过去。

“我数出六十二个。”我末了说。

“六十二个？胡说；不可能。啊，我明白你干了什么了，你把那个和那个也数进去了，”他指着两处刻痕；那两处，我确实同其余的字形一样当成了字母。

===Original===
“Yes, yes,” Professor Gregg went on; “but those are obvious scratches, done accidentally; I saw that at once. Yes, then that’s quite right. Thank you very much, Miss Lally.”

I was going away, rather disappointed at my having been called in merely to count a number of marks on the black seal, when suddenly there flashed into my mind what I had read in the morning.

“But, Professor Gregg,” I cried, breathless, “the seal, the seal. Why, it is the stone Hexecontalithos that Solinus writes of; it is Ixaxar.”

“Yes,” he said, “I suppose it is. Or it maybe a mere coincidence. It never does to be too sure, you know, in these matters. Coincidence killed the professor.”

===Chinese===
“是了，是了，”格雷格教授继续说，“那两个分明是划痕，偶然碰出来的；我一眼就看出来了。是了，那就对了。多谢您，拉利小姐。”

我正要走开，颇为扫兴——唤我来，原来不过是为数一数黑印上的刻痕——忽然，早晨读到的那些字闪进了我脑海。

“可是，格雷格教授，”我气也透不过地喊道，“那印，那印。这不就是索利努斯笔下的‘六十石’（Hexecontalithos）么；这就是伊克萨沙尔。”

“是啊，”他说，“我想是的。要不然，也不过是巧合。这些事情上，您知道，太有把握是万万要不得的。巧合杀死了教授。”

===Original===
I went away puzzled by what I had heard, and as much as ever at a loss to find the ruling clue in this maze of strange evidence. For three days the bad weather lasted, changing from driving rain to a dense mist, fine and dripping, and we seemed to be shut up in a white cloud that veiled all the world away from us. All the while Professor Gregg was darkling in his room, unwilling, it appeared, to dispense confidences or talk of any kind, and I heard him walking to and fro with a quick, impatient step, as if he were in some way wearied of inaction. The fourth morning was fine, and at breakfast the professor said briskly:﻿

“We want some extra help about the house; a boy of fifteen or sixteen, you know. There are a lot of little odd jobs that take up the maids’ time, which a boy could do much better.”

“The girls have not complained to me in any way,” I replied. “Indeed, Anne said there was much less work than in London, owing to there being so little dust.”

“Ah, yes, they are very good girls. But I think we shall do much better with a boy. In fact, that is what has been bothering me for the last two days.”

===Chinese===
