我走开了，满心疑惑，依旧寻不出这堆奇证迷宫里的那条主线索。坏天气持续了三天，从横扫的大雨变成一片浓密、细密而滴沥的雾；我们仿佛被关在一朵白云里，整个世界都被它同我们隔开了。整整这几日，格雷格教授都蛰在他房间里暗自运筹，看来既不愿吐露心腹，也不愿作任何攀谈；我听见他来回疾走，脚步急促而不耐，仿佛被这无所事事磨得倦了。第四天早晨放晴，用早餐时，教授爽利地说：——

“家里要多添个帮手；要个十五六岁的男孩，您知道。有一大堆零零碎碎的活计占着女仆们的时间，换个男孩来干，要顺手得多。”

“两个女仆可从没向我抱怨过什么，”我答道，“说实话，安还说，这边的活儿比伦敦少得多，因为灰尘少。”

“啊，是，她们都是很好的姑娘。不过我看，添个男孩会好得多。说实在的，这两天为这事，我心里一直在犯嘀咕。”

===Original===
“Bothering you?” I said in astonishment, for as a matter of fact the professor never took the slightest interest in the affairs of the house.

“Yes,” he said, “the weather, you know. I really couldn’t go out in that Scotch mist; I don’t know the country very well, and I should have lost my way. But I am going to get the boy this morning.”

“But how do you know there is such a boy as you want anywhere about?”

“Oh, I have no doubt as to that. I may have to walk a mile or two at the most, but I am sure to find just the boy I require.”

===Chinese===
“犯嘀咕？”我惊讶地说；因为实情是，教授对家务从不过问半分。

“是啊，”他说，“您知道，都怪那天气。那样的苏格兰浓雾里，我实在出不了门；这一带我又不熟，保准要迷路。可今天早晨，我就去把这孩子弄来。”

“可您怎么知道，这左近真有您要的这么一个男孩？”

“哦，这层我毫不怀疑。至多走上一两英里，我保管找得到正合我需要的那一个。”

===Original===
I thought the professor was joking, but though his tone was airy enough there was something grim and set about his features that puzzled me. He got his stick, and stood at the door looking meditatively before him, and as I passed through the hall he called to me.

“By the way, Miss Lally, there was one thing I wanted to say to you. I daresay you may have heard that some of these country lads are not over bright; idiotic would be a harsh word to use, and they are usually called ‘naturals,’ or something of the kind. I hope you won’t mind if the boy I am after should turn out not too keen-witted; he will be perfectly harmless, of course, and blacking boots doesn’t need much mental effort.”

===Chinese===
我只当教授在说笑；可他虽然口气轻快，脸上却有一种冷峻而笃定的神情，叫我纳闷。他取了手杖，立在门口，若有所思地望着前方；我穿过门厅时，他叫住了我。

“对了，拉利小姐，有件事我正想同您说。您想必也听说过，这一带的乡下孩子，有些脑筋不大灵光；说‘白痴’未免太重，人们往常管他们叫‘天生的呆子’（naturals）之类。我要找的这孩子，万一也显得不那么伶俐，还望您不要介意；他当然绝无危害，再说，擦擦靴子也用不着多少心思。”

===Original===
With that he was gone, striding up the road that led to the wood; and I remained stupefied, and then for the first time my astonishment was mingled with a sudden note of terror, arising I knew not whence, and all unexplained even to myself, and yet I felt about my heart for an instant something of the chill of death, and that shapeless, formless dread of the unknown that is worse than death itself. I tried to find courage in the sweet air that blew up from the sea, and in the sunlight after rain, but the mystic woods seemed to darken around me; and the vision of the river coiling between the reeds, and the silver gray of the ancient bridge, fashioned in my mind symbols of vague dread, as the mind of a child fashions terror from things harmless and familiar.

===Chinese===
说罢他便去了，大步走上通往树林的那条路；我怔在那里，随后，我的惊愕第一次掺进了一缕突至的恐怖——它不知从何而起，连我自己也无从解释——然而刹那间，我觉得心口掠过一丝死亡的寒意，掠过那种对未知的无形无状的惧怕，那比死亡本身更可怕。我试着从海上吹来的甜美空气里、从雨后的阳光里寻一点勇气；可那神秘的树林仿佛在我四围暗了下去；芦苇间蜿蜒盘绕的河流，那古桥的银灰，在我心里凝成种种朦胧恐惧的象征，一如孩子的心从无害而熟识的事物里造出恐怖。

===Original===
Two hours later Professor Gregg returned. I met him as he came down the road, and asked quietly if he had been able to find a boy.

“Oh, yes,” he answered; “I found one easily enough. His name is Jervase Cradock, and I expect he will make himself very useful. His father has been dead for many years, and the mother, whom I saw, seemed very glad at the prospect of a few shillings extra coming in on Saturday nights. As I expected, he is not too sharp, has fits at times, the mother said; but as he will not be trusted with the china, that doesn’t much matter, does it? And he is not in any way dangerous, you know, merely a little weak.”

“When is he coming?”

“Tomorrow morning at eight o’clock. Anne will show him what he has to do, and how to do it. At first he will go home every night, but perhaps it may ultimately turn out more convenient for him to sleep here, and only go home for Sundays.”

===Chinese===
两个钟头后，格雷格教授回来了。我在他下山的路上迎着他，平静地问他可寻到了男孩。

“哦，寻到了，”他答道，“不费什么事就找到一个。他叫贾维斯·克拉多克（Jervase Cradock），我看他会很得用。他父亲已死去多年；我见了他母亲，听说星期六晚上能多进几个先令，她似乎欢喜得很。不出我所料，这孩子不大伶俐，母亲说他时常犯病；不过反正瓷器不会交到他手里，这也不大要紧，是不是？况且他绝无任何危险，您知道，不过脑子弱点罢了。”

“他什么时候来？”

“明天早晨八点。安会教他该做些什么、怎么去做。起初他每晚回家；往后兴许住在这里更便当些，只星期日回家去。”

===Original===
I found nothing to say to all this. Professor Gregg spoke in a quiet tone of matter-of-fact, as indeed was warranted by the circumstance; and yet I could not quell my sensation of astonishment at the whole affair. I knew that in reality no assistance was wanted in the housework, and the professor’s prediction that the boy he was to engage might prove a little “simple,” followed by so exact a fulfilment, struck me as bizarre in the extreme. The next morning I heard from the housemaid that the boy Cradock had come at eight, and that she had been trying to make him useful. “He doesn’t seem quite all there, I don’t think, miss,” was her comment; and later in the day I saw him helping the old man who worked in the garden. He was a youth of about fourteen, with black hair and black eyes, and an olive skin, and I saw at once from the curious vacancy of his expression that he was mentally weak. He touched his forehead awkwardly as I went by, and I heard him answering the gardener in a queer, harsh voice that caught my attention; it gave me the impression of someone speaking deep below under the earth, and there was a strange sibilance, like the hissing of the phonograph as the pointer travels over the cylinder. I heard that he seemed anxious to do what he could, and was quite docile and obedient, and Morgan the gardener, who knew his mother, assured me he was perfectly harmless. “He’s always been a bit queer,” he said, “and no wonder, after what his mother went through before he was born. I did know his father, Thomas Cradock, well, and a very fine workman he was too, indeed. He got something wrong with his lungs owing to working in the wet woods, and never got over it, and went off quite sudden like. And they do say as how Mrs. Cradock was quite off her head; anyhow, she was found by Mr. Hillyer, Ty Coch, all crouched up on the Gray Hills, over there, crying and weeping like a lost soul. And Jervase he was born about eight months afterwards, and as I was saying, he was a bit queer always; and they do say when he could scarcely walk he would frighten the other children into fits with the noises he would make.”

===Chinese===
这一切教我无言可对。格雷格教授说得平平静静，就事论事，情形也的确当得起这份平静；可我对整桩事情的那种惊愕，怎么也压不下去。我知道，家务上实在无需添什么帮手；教授预言他要雇的男孩兴许有点“呆”，而预言竟如此分毫不爽地应验，在我看来古怪之至。第二天早晨，我从女仆那里听说，克拉多克那孩子八点来了，她一直想法子让他派上用场。“我看他那脑子，好像不大齐全，小姐，”她这么评；当天晚些，我看见他在给园子里做工的老人打下手。他是个十四岁上下的少年，黑头发，黑眼睛，橄榄色的皮肤；他脸上那种异样的茫然，叫我一眼看出他智力孱弱。我走过时，他笨拙地碰了碰额头；随后我听见他答园丁的话，嗓音古怪而刺耳，引了我的注意：它给我的印象，仿佛有谁在地底深处说话，又带着一种奇异的嘶声，像留声机的唱针划过滚筒时的嘶嘶作响。听说他巴不得尽力做事，十分驯顺听话；识得他母亲的园丁摩根（Morgan）向我担保，他绝无危害。“他从来就有点古怪，”他说，“也难怪，他娘在他落地之前遭了那场罪。他爹托马斯·克拉多克（Thomas Cradock）我是熟识的，实实在在是个好匠人。他在湿林子里做活，肺坏了，一直没好利索，说去就去了。人家都说克拉多克太太那阵子简直疯了；反正，是泰科奇（Ty Coch）的希利尔先生，在那边灰山上找见她的，蜷作一团，哭得像个沦落的冤魂。贾维斯就是那以后大约八个月生的；像我方才说的，他从来就有点古怪；人家还说，他还不大 会走路的时候，弄出的那些声响就能把别的孩子吓出病来。”

===Original===
A word in the story had stirred up some remembrance within me, and vaguely curious, I asked the old man where the Gray Hills were.

“Up there,” he said, with the same gesture he had used before; “you go past the Fox and Hounds, and through the forest, by the old ruins. It’s a good five mile from here, and a strange sort of a place. The poorest soil between this and Monmouth, they do say, though it’s good feed for sheep. Yes, it was a sad thing for poor Mrs. Cradock.”

===Chinese===
这故事里有一个词，搅起了我记忆深处的什么；怀着朦胧的好奇，我问老人，灰山在哪里。

“在上头，”他说，做出先前那个手势，“您过了‘狐狸与猎犬’（Fox and Hounds），穿林子走，经过那片老废墟。离这儿足有五英里，是个古怪地方。人家都说，从这儿到蒙茅斯（Monmouth），数那儿的土最贫；不过放羊倒是好牧场。是啊，可怜的克拉多克太太，那是桩伤心事。”

===Original===
The old man turned to his work, and I strolled on down the path between the espaliers, gnarled and gouty with age, thinking of the story I had heard, and groping for the point in it that had some key to my memory. In an instant it came before me; I had seen the phrase “Gray Hills” on the slip of yellowed paper that Professor Gregg had taken from the drawer in his cabinet. Again I was seized with pangs of mingled curiosity and fear; I remembered the strange characters copied from the limestone rock, and then again their identity with the inscription on the age-old seal, and the fantastic fables of the Latin geographer. I saw beyond doubt that, unless coincidence had set all the scene and disposed all these bizarre events with curious art, I was to be a spectator of things far removed from the usual and customary traffic and jostle of life. Professor Gregg I noted day by day. He was hot on his trail, growing lean with eagerness; and in the evenings, when the sun was swimming on the verge of the mountain, he would pace the terrace to and fro with his eyes on the ground, while the mist grew white in the valley, and the stillness of the evening brought far voices near, and the blue smoke rose a straight column from the diamond-shaped chimney of the gray farmhouse, just as I had seen it on the first morning. I have told you I was of sceptical habit; but though I understood little or nothing, I began to dread, vainly proposing to myself the iterated dogmas of science that all life is material, and that in the system of things there is no undiscovered land even beyond the remotest stars, where the supernatural can find a footing. Yet there struck in on this the thought that matter is as really awful and unknown as spirit, that science itself but dallies on the threshold, scarcely gaining more than a glimpse of the wonders of the inner place.

===Chinese===
老人转身做他的活去了；我沿着果树棚架间的小径信步走下去——那些果树上了年纪，疙疙瘩瘩，虬曲如患痛风——一面想着方才听来的故事，一面摸索着故事里那处同我记忆相连的关键。刹那间，它显现在我眼前了：“灰山”这两个字，我曾在格雷格教授从柜中抽屉里取出的那张发黄纸条上见过。我又被好奇心与恐惧交织的阵阵绞痛攫住；我想起从石灰岩上摹下的那些怪字，想起它们与那方太古之印上铭文的如出一辙，想起那位拉丁地理学家的荒唐传说。我看得分明：除非是巧合以奇巧的手笔布下了这一切场景、安排了这全部怪事，否则，我将成为远远超乎日常生活往来熙攘之事的看客。格雷格教授，我日复一日地留意着。他追在踪迹上，气味正热，急得清瘦下来；每到傍晚，太阳泅在山巅的边上时，他便在露台上来回踱步，眼睛盯着地面；山谷里雾气渐白，暮色的静谧把远处的声音送近，蓝烟从灰色农舍那菱形烟囱里笔直地升起一柱——正同我头一个早晨见的一模一样。我已告诉过您，我素来惯于怀疑；可如今，虽然我懂得的微乎其微，我却开始惧怕了——徒劳地搬出科学那些反复申说的教条自劝：凡生命皆属物质；天地万有的秩序里，即便在最远的星辰之外，也没有一寸未发现的土地，可供超自然之物立足。然而，这里又斜刺里插进一个念头：物质之可畏、之不可知，实在不亚于精神；科学自己不过在门槛上盘桓，于内在居所的诸奇，不过窥得一眼罢了。

===Original===
There is one day that stands up from amidst the others as a grim red beacon, betokening evil to come. I was sitting on a bench in the garden, watching the boy Cradock weeding, when I was suddenly alarmed by a harsh and choking sound, like the cry of a wild beast in anguish, and I was unspeakably shocked to see the unfortunate lad standing in full view before me, his whole body quivering and shaking at short intervals as though shocks of electricity were passing through him, and his teeth grinding, and foam gathering on his lips, and his face all swollen and blackened to a hideous mask of humanity. I shrieked with terror, and Professor Gregg came running; and as I pointed to Cradock, the boy with one convulsive shudder fell face forward, and lay on the wet earth, his body writhing like a wounded blindworm, and an inconceivable babble of sounds bursting and rattling and hissing from his lips; he seemed to pour forth an infamous jargon, with words, or what seemed words, that might have belonged to a tongue dead since untold ages, and buried deep beneath Nilotic mud, or in the inmost recesses of the Mexican forest. For a moment the thought passed through my mind, as my ears were still revolted with that infernal clamor, “Surely this is the very speech of hell,” and then I cried out again and again, and ran away shuddering to my inmost soul. I had seen Professor Gregg’s face as he stooped over the wretched boy and raised him, and I was appalled by the glow of exultation that shone on every lineament and feature. As I sat in my room with drawn blinds, and my eyes hidden in my hands, I heard heavy steps beneath, and I was told afterwards that Professor Gregg had carried Cradock to his study, and had locked the door. I heard voices murmur indistinctly, and I trembled to think of what might be passing within a few feet of where I sat; I longed to escape to the woods and sunshine, and yet I dreaded the sights that might confront me on the way. And at last, as I held the handle of the door nervously, I heard Professor Gregg’s voice calling to me with a cheerful ring: “It’s all right now, Miss Lally,” he said. “The poor fellow has got over it, and I have been arranging for him to sleep here after tomorrow. Perhaps I may be able to do something for him.”

===Chinese===
有一天，从其余的日子里直直立起，像一座血红的不祥灯塔，预示着将至的灾祸。我正坐在园中一条长椅上，看着克拉多克那孩子锄草，蓦地被一声嘶哑而噎人的声响惊动，像困兽临绝的哀号；我惊骇莫名地看见，那不幸的少年正整个立在我眼前，全身一阵阵地抽搐震颤，仿佛一股股电流正穿过他，牙齿磨得咯咯作响，白沫在唇边积聚，脸胀得乌青，成了一张人性的可怖假面。我恐怖地尖叫起来；格雷格教授奔了过来；我手指克拉多克，那孩子痉挛着猛一哆嗦，脸朝下栽倒，伏在湿地上，身体像一条受伤的瞎蛇般扭动，唇间迸出一连串难以想象的嘈音，噼啪作响，嘶嘶不绝；他仿佛倾泻着一种邪恶的黑话，那些词——或者说像是词的东西——或许属于一种死了不知多少万年的语言，深埋在尼罗河的淤泥底下，或墨西哥森林最幽深的腹地。那阵地狱般的喧响还煎熬着我耳朵的一刻，一个念头掠过我心头：“这分明就是地狱的言语”；随后我一声又一声地叫喊起来，战栗到灵魂深处地跑开了。我曾瞥见格雷格教授俯身扶起那可怜孩子时的脸；他每一根线条、每一处容貌上燃烧的那份得胜的狂喜，把我吓呆了。我坐在自己房里，窗帘垂下，双眼埋在手里，听见楼下沉重的脚步；后来听说，格雷格教授把克拉多克抱进了书房，锁上了门。我听见人语含混地低低响起，一想到离我坐处不过几英尺的地方可能正经过什么，我便战栗；我渴望逃到树林和阳光里去，却又怕一路上会撞见的景象。末了，当我紧张地握住门柄时，我听见格雷格教授的声音唤我，语气欢快：“都过去了，拉利小姐，”他说，“可怜的家伙缓过来了。我已经安排好，后天起他就睡在这里。说不定，我还能替他做点什么。”

===Original===
“Yes,” he said later, “it was a very painful sight, and I don’t wonder you were alarmed. We may hope that good food will build him up a little, but I am afraid he will never be really cured”; and he affected the dismal and conventional air with which one speaks of hopeless illness, and yet beneath it I detected the delight that leapt up rampant within him, and fought and struggled to find utterance. It was as if one glanced down on the even surface of the sea, clear and immobile, and saw beneath raging depths, and a storm of contending billows. It was indeed to me a torturing and offensive problem that this man, who had so bounteously rescued me from the sharpness of death, and showed himself in all the relations of life full of benevolence and pity and kindly forethought, should so manifestly be for once on the side of the demons, and take a ghastly pleasure in the torments of an afflicted fellow-creature. Apart, I struggled with the horned difficulty, and strove to find the solution, but without the hint of a clue; beset by mystery and contradiction, I saw nothing that might help me, and began to wonder whether, after all, I had not escaped from the white mist of the suburb at too dear a rate. I hinted something of my thought to the professor; I said enough to let him know that I was in the most acute perplexity, but the moment after regretted what I had done, when I saw his face contort with a spasm of pain.

===Chinese===
“是啊，”他后来说，“那情景很不好受，您受惊我不奇怪。我们可以指望，好饭食会把他将养得好一些；只是恐怕他永远治不好了。”他装出谈起不治之症时那种凄惨而俗套的神气；可在这底下，我察觉到那在他心里狂跳着、挣扎着要夺口而出的欢喜。这好比人俯瞰大海平滑的表面，清澈而凝定，底下却看见翻腾的深渊，看见相互搏斗的怒涛掀起的风暴。这于我实在是一道折磨人、也触犯人的难题：这个人，曾如此慷慨地把我从死亡的利刃下救出，在人生的一切关系里都显得满腔仁善、怜悯与周到的慈心，此刻却如此明白地站在了魔鬼一边，在一个受苦同类的痛楚里取得一种可怖的愉悦。背地里，我同这棘手的难题搏斗，竭力寻一个解答，却连一丝线索的影子也没有；神秘与矛盾四面围困，我看不到任何可以助我的东西，便开始纳闷：我当年逃出郊外的白雾，这代价是否未免太昂贵了。我向教授隐隐约约吐露了一点心思；我说了足够多的话，让他知道我正陷于至深的困顿；可话一出口我就后悔了——我看见他的脸因一阵痛苦的痉挛而扭曲。

===Original===
“My dear Miss Lally,” he said, “you surely do not wish to leave us? No, no, you would not do it. You do not know how I rely on you; how confidently I go forward, assured that you are here to watch over my children. You, Miss Lally, are my rearguard; for, let me tell you, that the business in which I am engaged is not wholly devoid of peril. You have not forgotten what I said the first morning here; my lips are shut by an old and firm resolve, till they can open to utter no ingenious hypothesis or vague surmise but irrefragable fact, as certain as a demonstration in mathematics. Think over it, Miss Lally, not for a moment would I endeavor to keep you here against your own instincts, and yet I tell you frankly that I am persuaded that it is here, here amidst the woods, that your duty lies.”

===Chinese===
“亲爱的拉利小姐，”他说，“您总不至于想要离开我们吧？不，不，您不会的。您不知道我怎样倚仗着您；我怎样满怀信心地向前走，因为确知您在这里照看我的孩子们。您，拉利小姐，是我的后军；请听我说，我从事的这桩事业，并非全无危险。我头一个早晨在这里说过的话，您没有忘；我的双唇被一个久远而坚定的决心封着，直到它们张开的那个时辰——那时说出的将不是巧妙的假设或含糊的猜测，而是如数学证明一般确凿的、无可辩驳的事实。好好想想吧，拉利小姐；哪怕一刻，我也绝不愿违着您的天性把您留在这里；可我还是坦白告诉您：我深信，您的职分就在这里，就在这树林之间。”

===Original===
I was touched by the eloquence of his tone, and by the remembrance that the man, after all, had been my salvation, and I gave him my hand on a promise to serve him loyally and without question. A few days later the rector of our church, a little church, gray and severe and quaint, that hovered on the very banks of the river and watched the tides swim and return, came to see us, and Professor Gregg easily persuaded him to stay and share our dinner. Mr. Meyrick was a member of an antique family of squires, whose old manor house stood amongst the hills some seven miles away, and thus rooted in the soil, the rector was a living store of all the old fading customs and lore of the country. His manner, genial with a deal of retired oddity, won on Professor Gregg; and towards the cheese, when a curious Burgundy had begun its incantations, the two men glowed like the wine, and talked of philology with the enthusiasm of a burgess over the peerage. The parson was expounding the pronunciation of the Welsh *ll*, and producing sounds like the gurgle of his native brooks, when Professor Gregg struck in.

===Chinese===
我被他言辞的恳切打动了，又记起这个人毕竟曾是我的救主，便把手伸给他，许诺忠心耿耿、绝不二话地为他效力。几天之后，我们教堂的牧师来访——那是座小小的教堂，灰色的、峻严而古雅，悬踞在河岸之上，守着潮水的涨落来去——格雷格教授轻易便劝得他留下共进晚餐。梅里克先生（Mr. Meyrick）出身于一系古老的乡绅人家，祖上的庄园宅邸坐落在七八英里外的群山之间；如此根植于乡土，这位牧师便是一部活的库藏，藏着这地方一切日渐消褪的旧俗与传闻。他的举止，敦厚之中带着几分退隐的古怪，很讨格雷格教授的欢喜；吃到奶酪的时候，一款难得的勃艮第（Burgundy）已念完了它的咒语，两个人红光满面，谈起了语文学，那份热络，好比一个市民谈论贵族的门第。牧师正讲着威尔士语里 *ll* 的发音，学着他家乡溪水汩汩的声口，格雷格教授插了进来。

===Original===
“By the way,” he said, “that was a very odd word I met with the other day. You know my boy, poor Jervase Cradock. Well, he has got the bad habit of talking to himself, and the day before yesterday I was walking in the garden here and heard him; he was evidently quite unconscious of my presence. A lot of what he said I couldn’t make out, but one word struck me distinctly. It was such an odd sound; half-sibilant, half-guttural, and as quaint as those double *l*’s you have been demonstrating. I do not know whether I can give you an idea of the sound. ‘Ishakshar’ is perhaps as near as I can get; but the *k* ought to be a Greek *chi* or a Spanish *j*. Now what does it mean in Welsh?”

“In Welsh?” said the parson. “There is no such word in Welsh, nor any word remotely resembling it. I know the book-Welsh, as they call it, and the colloquial dialects as well as any man, but there’s no word like that from Anglesea to Usk. Besides, none of the Cradocks speak a word of Welsh; it’s dying out about here.”

“Really. You interest me extremely, Mr. Meyrick. I confess the word didn’t strike me as having the Welsh ring. But I thought it might be some local corruption.”

“No, I never heard such a word, or anything like it. Indeed,” he added, smiling whimsically, “if it belongs to any language, I should say it must be that of the fairies﻿—the *Tylwydd Têg*, as we call them.”

===Chinese===
“对了，”他说，“前两天我碰到一个非常古怪的词。您知道我那个孩子，可怜的贾维斯·克拉多克。嗯，他有个坏毛病，爱自言自语；前天我正在这园子里散步，听见了他；他显然全然不觉我在跟前。他说的一大半我听不懂，可有一个词清清楚楚敲在我耳朵里。那声音真是古怪；半个是嘶音，半个是喉音，同您方才演示的那两个 *l* 一样别致。我不知道能不能让您听出个大概。‘Ishakshar’（伊沙克沙尔）大概是我能凑上的最近的音了；只是那个 *k*，本当是希腊语的 *chi*，或西班牙语的 *j*。它在威尔士语里是什么意思？”

“威尔士语里？”牧师说，“威尔士语里没有这个词，连一个略微像它的词也没有。所谓的书本威尔士语也好，各处的口语土话也好，我同任何人一样熟；可从安格尔西（Anglesea）到厄斯克（Usk），没有这样的词。再说，克拉多克家的人一个威尔士语词也不会讲；这一带它正在绝迹呢。”

“真的么。您让我大感兴趣，梅里克先生。我承认，这个词听在我耳里，本就不带威尔士语的腔调。可我还当它是本地话的什么讹变。”

“不，这样的词我从未听过，连相像的也没有。说真的，”他古怪地笑着补了一句，“它若真属于哪种语言，我只能说，那必是仙女们的语言——我们叫作‘美丽之民’（*Tylwydd Têg*）的。”

===Original===
The talk went on to the discovery of a Roman villa in the neighborhood; and soon after I left the room, and sat down apart to wonder at the drawing together of such strange clues of evidence. As the professor had spoken of the curious word, I had caught the glint of his eye upon me; and though the pronunciation he gave was grotesque in the extreme, I recognized the name of the stone of sixty characters mentioned by Solinus, the black seal shut up in some secret drawer of the study, stamped forever by a vanished race with signs that no man could read, signs that might, for all I knew, be the veils of awful things done long ago, and forgotten before the hills were moulded into form.

===Chinese===
话题又转到左近发掘出一处罗马别墅上去；不久我便离了席，独自坐下，惊叹这般离奇的线索竟一环环收拢到一处。教授说起那个古怪词的时候，我曾捕捉到他瞥向我的眼色；他发的音固然荒腔走板之至，我却认出来了：那是索利努斯提到的六十字符之石的名字——那方黑印，此刻正锁在书房哪只秘密抽屉里，被一个消逝的种族永远钤上了无人能识的印记；那些印记，天知道，或许正是很久很久以前做下的可怕之事的帷幕，在群山尚未成形之前，便已被遗忘了。

===Original===
When, the next morning, I came down, I found Professor Gregg pacing the terrace in his eternal walk.

“Look at that bridge,” he said when he saw me, “observe the quaint and Gothic design, the angles between the arches, and the silvery gray of the stone in the awe of the morning light. I confess it seems to me symbolic; it should illustrate a mystical allegory of the passage from one world to another.”

“Professor Gregg,” I said quietly, “it is time that I knew something of what has happened, and of what is to happen.”

===Chinese===
第二天早晨我下楼来，见格雷格教授又在露台上做他永不止息的踱步。

“看那座桥，”他一见我便说，“看那别致的哥特式样，拱与拱之间的夹角，看晨光的敬畏里石头的银灰。我承认，它在我看来是象征性的；它该为一则神秘的寓言作插图：从一个世界通往另一个世界的渡越。”

“格雷格教授，”我平静地说，“是时候了，我该知道已经发生了什么、还将发生什么。”

===Original===
For the moment he put me off, but I returned again with the same question in the evening, and then Professor Gregg flamed with excitement. “Don’t you understand yet?” he cried. “But I have told you a good deal; yes, and shown you a good deal. You have heard pretty nearly all that I have heard, and seen what I have seen; or at least,” and his voice chilled as he spoke, “enough to make a good deal clear as noonday. The servants told you, I have no doubt, that the wretched boy Cradock had another seizure the night before last; he awoke me with cries in that voice you heard in the garden, and I went to him, and God forbid you should see what I saw that night. But all this is useless; my time here is drawing to a close; I must be back in town in three weeks, as I have a course of lectures to prepare, and need all my books about me. In a very few days it will be all over, and I shall no longer hint, and no longer be liable to ridicule as a madman and a quack. No, I shall speak plainly, and I shall be heard with such emotions as perhaps no other man has ever drawn from the breasts of his fellows.”

===Chinese===
他一时把我岔开了；可到了晚上，我又拿着同一个问题回去，这一回，格雷格教授勃然兴奋起来。“你还不明白么？”他喊道，“可我已经告诉了你不少；是的，也让你看了不少。我所听见的，你差不多全听见了；我所看见的，你也看见了；或者说，至少——”说到这里他的声音冷了下来，“至少已足够让许多事情明白如正午。仆人们想必已告诉你，可怜的克拉多克前天夜里又犯了一回病；他用你在园子里听见的那种嗓音把我哭醒，我到他那里去了——但愿上帝保佑你，永远不要看见我那晚看见的东西。可说这些全无用处；我在此地的日子将尽了；三星期内我必须回城，我有一门课要备，身边离不开我的书。用不了几天，一切便见分晓；我将不再隐隐约约，也不再担着疯子和骗子的骂名招人耻笑。不，我要说得明明白白，而我将被倾听——带着那样的激动，或许还从没有一个凡人从同类胸中唤起过。”

===Original===
He paused, and seemed to grow radiant with the joy of great and wonderful discovery.

“But all that is for the future, the near future certainly, but still the future,” he went on at length. “There is something to be done yet; you will remember my telling you that my researches were not altogether devoid of peril? Yes, there is a certain amount of danger to be faced; I did not know how much when I spoke on the subject before, and to a certain extent I am still in the dark. But it will be a strange adventure, the last of all, the last demonstration in the chain.”

===Chinese===
他顿了顿，仿佛因伟大而奇妙的发现之乐而容光焕发。

“但那都是将来的事，无疑是不远的将来，但终归是将来，”过了半晌他又说，“眼下还有一件事要办；你可记得我对你说过，我的研究并非全无危险？是的，有那么几分危险须得面对；先前说起这话题时，我还不知深浅几何；到今日，某种程度上我仍在暗处。但这将是一场奇遇，最后的一场，链条上的最后一环实证。”

===Original===
He was walking up and down the room as he spoke, and I could hear in his voice the contending tones of exultation and despondence, or perhaps I should say awe, the awe of a man who goes forth on unknown waters, and I thought of his allusion to Columbus on the night he had laid his book before me. The evening was a little chilly, and a fire of logs had been lighted in the study where we were, and the remittent flame and the glow on the walls reminded me of the old days. I was sitting silent in an armchair by the fire, wondering over all I had heard, and still vainly speculating as to the secret springs concealed from me under all the phantasmagoria I had witnessed, when I became suddenly aware of a sensation that change of some sort had been at work in the room, and that there was something unfamiliar in its aspect. For some time I looked about me, trying in vain to localize the alteration that I knew had been made; the table by the window, the chairs, the faded settee were all as I had known them. Suddenly, as a sought-for recollection flashes into the mind, I knew what was amiss. I was facing the professor’s desk, which stood on the other side of the fire, and above the desk was a grimy looking bust of Pitt, that I had never seen there before. And then I remembered the true position of this work of art; in the furthest corner by the door was an old cupboard, projecting into the room, and on the top of the cupboard, fifteen feet from the floor, the bust had been, and there no doubt it had delayed, accumulating dirt since the early years of the century.

===Chinese===
他说话时在屋里来回踱着；我听得出他语声里得胜与沮丧交战的声音——或者该说敬畏，一个驶向未知水域之人的敬畏；我想起他把书放到我面前那晚对哥伦布的援引。晚间的天气微微有些寒意，我们所在的书房里已生起一炉柴火；那忽明忽暗的火焰和墙上的红光，使我想起旧日的岁月。我无言地坐在炉边的扶手椅里，想着听到的一切，仍徒然揣测着那隐在这一切幻影之下、避我而不见的秘密泉源；蓦地，我觉出一种感觉：屋里起了某种变化，它的气象里有了什么陌生的东西。有一阵子我环顾四周，想找出我知道必已做下的那处改动，却徒劳无功；窗边的桌子、椅子、褪色的长榻，都同我素来识得的一样。忽然，如一个寻而不得的回忆蓦地闪进心里，我知道毛病出在哪里了。我正对着教授的书桌，书桌立在炉火另一侧；书桌上方，是一尊灰扑扑的皮特（Pitt）半身像——我从未在那里见过它。跟着我想起了这件艺术品原先的位置：靠门最远的角落里立着一口旧橱柜，凸进屋内；橱柜顶上，离地十五英尺，那半身像原是搁在那里的；毫无疑问，它正是在那里消磨着岁月，自本世纪初年起积攒着灰尘。

===Original===
I was utterly amazed, and sat silent still, in a confusion of thought. There was, so far as I knew, no such thing as a stepladder in the house, for I had asked for one to make some alterations in the curtains of my room; and a tall man standing on a chair would have found it impossible to take down the bust. It had been placed not on the edge of the cupboard, but far back against the wall; and Professor Gregg was, if anything, under the average height.

“How on earth did you manage to get down Pitt?” I said at last.

The professor looked curiously at me, and seemed to hesitate a little.

“They must have found you a stepladder, or perhaps the gardener brought in a short ladder from outside.”

“No, I have had no ladder of any kind. Now, Miss Lally,” he went on with an awkward simulation of jest, “there is a little puzzle for you; a problem in the manner of the inimitable Holmes; there are the facts, plain and patent; summon your acuteness to the solution of the puzzle. For Heaven’s sake,” he cried with a breaking voice, “say no more about it. I tell you, I never touched the thing,” and he went out of the room with horror manifest on his face, and his hand shook and jarred the door behind him.

===Chinese===
我惊愕之至，仍坐在那里一言不发，思绪纷乱。据我所知，这宅子里根本没有折梯这样一件东西，因为我要改一改房里的窗帘，曾问人讨过一架；而一个高个子男人站在椅子上，也不可能取下那尊半身像。它搁的位置，不是橱柜的边沿，而是深深靠里、贴着墙；至于格雷格教授，他的个子倘若有什么可说，那便是低于中等。

“您到底是怎么把皮特搬下来的？”我终于说。

教授古怪地看了看我，似有片刻的踌躇。

“他们准是给您寻了一架折梯来，要不就是园丁从外头搬了张短梯进来。”

“不，我从未有过任何梯子。好了，拉利小姐，”他强作戏语，接着说，“这儿有个小小的谜给您；一道无与伦比的福尔摩斯（Holmes）式的题目；事实都在这儿，明明白白；请调动您的敏锐，来解这道谜吧。看在老天爷的分上，”他嗓音都劈了，喊道，“别再提它了。我告诉你，我从没碰过那东西。”他走出屋去，脸上明明白白写着恐怖；他的手抖着，带得身后的门一阵震响。

===Original===
I looked round the room in vague surprise, not at all realizing what had happened, making vain and idle surmises by way of explanation, and wondering at the stirring of black waters by an idle word, and the trivial change of an ornament. “This is some petty business, some whim on which I have jarred,” I reflected; “the professor is perhaps scrupulous and superstitious over trifles, and my question may have outraged unacknowledged fears, as though one killed a spider or spilled the salt before the very eyes of a practical Scotchwoman.” I was immersed in these fond suspicions, and began to plume myself a little on my immunity from such empty fears, when the truth fell heavily as lead upon my heart, and I recognized with cold terror that some awful influence had been at work. The bust was simply inaccessible; without a ladder no one could have touched it.

===Chinese===
我茫然惊讶地环顾这间屋子，全然没有意识到发生了什么，徒然做着种种无聊的猜测权当解释，纳闷一句闲话怎会搅动黑水，一件摆设的小小挪移又算得了什么。“这是桩区区小事，是我碰痛了人家什么雅好，”我这样想，“教授或许在小事上拘谨而迷信，我的问话许是冒犯了他不曾自认的恐惧，就像当着一个讲实际的苏格兰妇人的眼，掐死一只蜘蛛，或是撒了一把盐。”我正浸在这些痴想里，还颇为自己不为这等空洞恐惧所染而沾沾自喜，真相却如铅块般重重落在我心上；我带着冰冷的恐怖认了出来：某种可怕的影响力已经动过手了。那半身像根本无从企及；没有梯子，谁也碰不着它。

===Original===
I went out to the kitchen and spoke as quietly as I could to the housemaid.

“Who moved that bust from the top of the cupboard, Anne?” I said to her. “Professor Gregg says he has not touched it. Did you find an old stepladder in one of the outhouses?”

The girl looked at me blankly.

===Chinese===
我出去到厨房，尽量心平气和地问那女仆。

“安，橱柜顶上那尊半身像，是谁搬下来的？”我对她说，“格雷格教授说他没碰过。你是不是在哪间外屋里找着一架旧折梯？”

姑娘茫然地望着我。

===Original===
“I never touched it,” she said. “I found it where it is now the other morning when I dusted the room. I remember now, it, was Wednesday morning, because it was the morning after Cradock was taken bad in the night. My room is next to his, you know, miss,” the girl went on piteously; “and it was awful to hear how he cried and called out names that I couldn’t understand. It made me feel all afraid, and then master came, and I heard him speak, and he took down Cradock to the study and gave him something.”

“And you found that bust moved the next morning?”

===Chinese===
“我从没碰过它，”她说，“那天早晨我掸灰，它就在现在这个位置上了。我想起来了，那是星期三早晨，因为那是克拉多克夜里发病的第二天。小姐，我的房间就在他隔壁，”姑娘可怜巴巴地说下去，“听他那样哭喊、喊出那些我听不懂的名字，真是吓死人。我吓得全身发毛；后来老爷来了，我听见他说话，把克拉多克抱下楼去了书房，还喂了他什么。”

“第二天早晨，你就发现半身像挪了位置？”

===Original===
“Yes, miss, there was a queer sort of a smell in the study when I came down and opened the windows; a bad smell it was, and I wondered what it could be. Do you know, miss, I went a long time ago to the Zoo in London with my cousin Thomas Barker, one afternoon that I had off, when I was at Mrs. Prince’s in Stanhope Gate, and we went into the snake-house to see the snakes, and it was just the same sort of a smell, very sick it made me feel, I remember, and I got Barker to take me out. And it was just the same kind of a smell in the study, as I was saying, and I was wondering what it could be from, when I see that bust with Pitt cut in it standing on the master’s desk, and I thought to myself, now who has done that, and how have they done it? And when I came to dust the things, I looked at the bust, and I saw a great mark on it where the dust was gone, for I don’t think it can have been touched with a duster for years and years, and it wasn’t like fingermarks, but a large patch like, broad and spread out. So I passed my hand over it, without thinking what I was doing, and where that patch was it was all sticky and slimy, as if a snail had crawled over it. Very strange, isn’t it, miss? and I wonder who can have done it, and how that mess was made.”

===Chinese===
“是的，小姐。我下楼打开书房窗户的时候，里面有一股怪味儿；味儿很不好，我纳闷那能是什么。您可不知道，小姐，好些年前，我有一个下午放假，那时我在斯坦霍普门（Stanhope Gate）普林斯太太（Mrs. Prince）家帮工，跟我表哥托马斯·巴克（Thomas Barker）去伦敦动物园（the Zoo），我们进蛇馆看蛇，就是那么一股味儿；我记得那味儿熏得我直想吐，我求巴克带我出去了。我正说着呢，书房里就是那么一股味儿；我正纳闷它是打哪儿来的，一抬眼，就瞧见那尊刻着皮特的半身像立在老爷的书桌上，我心里就琢磨：这是谁干的？又是怎么干成的？等到我来掸灰，我看了看那像，见上头有一大块印子，那儿的灰没了；我想那东西怕是许多许多年没让掸子碰过了。那印子不像指印，倒像一大片，宽宽的、摊开的。我也没多想就伸手往那儿一摸——小姐，那块地方黏糊糊、滑腻腻的，就像有蜗牛从上面爬过。真是邪门，是不是，小姐？我纳闷到底是谁干的，那摊烂污又是怎么弄出来的。”

===Original===
The well-meant gabble of the servant touched me to the quick. I lay down upon my bed, and bit my lip that I should not cry out loud in the sharp anguish of my terror and bewilderment. Indeed, I was almost mad with dread; I believe that if it had been daylight I should have fled hot foot, forgetting all courage and all the debt of gratitude that was due to Professor Gregg, not caring whether my fate were that I must starve slowly so long as I might escape from the net of blind and panic fear that every day seemed to draw a little closer round me. If I knew, I thought, if I knew what there were to dread, I could guard against it; but here, in this lonely house, shut in on all sides by the olden woods and the vaulted hills, terror seems to spring inconsequent from every covert, and the flesh is aghast at the half-heard murmurs of horrible things. All in vain I strove to summon scepticism to my aid, and endeavored by cool common-sense to buttress my belief in a world of natural order, for the air that blew in at the open window was a mystic breath, and in the darkness I felt the silence go heavy and sorrowful as a mass of requiem, and I conjured images of strange shapes gathering fast amidst the reeds, beside the wash of the river.

===Chinese===
这女仆一片好心的絮叨，句句扎在我心口上。我躺到床上，咬着嘴唇，才没有在恐惧与惶乱的剧痛里放声喊出来。说实话，我几乎被吓疯了；我相信，倘若那是白天，我会没命地逃走，什么勇气、什么欠格雷格教授的恩义，全都顾不得；哪怕命里注定要慢慢饿死，我也不管，只要能逃出这张盲目恐慌的天罗地网——它仿佛一天天朝我收拢得愈来愈紧。我若知道，我想，我若知道怕的是什么，我还能设防；可在这所孤宅里，四面被古老的树林与穹隆的山冈围困，恐怖仿佛从每一处隐蔽里没头没脑地窜出来，血肉之躯被那半听不真的、可怕之物的低语惊得发僵。我徒劳地想把怀疑主义召来救驾，想用冷静的常识撑住我对自然秩序之世界的信念；可敞开的窗口吹进来的风是一缕神秘的呼吸，黑暗里我觉得那沉默沉甸甸、悲切切，像一台安魂弥撒；我眼前幻出种种奇形，在芦苇丛间、在河水的拍溅旁，飞快地聚拢来。

===Original===
In the morning, from the moment that I set foot in the breakfast-room I felt that the unknown plot was drawing to a crisis; the professor’s face was firm and set, and he seemed hardly to hear our voices when we spoke.

“I am going out for rather a long walk,” he said, when the meal was over. “You mustn’t be expecting me, now, or thinking anything has happened if I don’t turn up to dinner. I have been getting stupid lately, and I dare say a miniature walking tour will do me good. Perhaps I may even spend the night in some little inn, if I find any place that looks clean and comfortable.”

===Chinese===
到了早晨，一脚踏进早餐室，我便觉得那桩未知的阴谋正逼近关头；教授的脸绷紧而定固，我们说话时，他仿佛几乎听不见。

“我要出去走一趟相当远的路，”饭毕，他说，“您可别等我，也别以为我赶不上晚饭就是出了什么事。近来我人发木了，我想，来一回小小的徒步旅行，于我有益。倘若寻着个看上去干净舒服的地方，说不定我就在哪家小客栈里过夜了。”

===Original===
I heard this, and knew by my experience of Professor Gregg’s manner that it was no ordinary business or pleasure that impelled him. I knew not, nor even remotely guessed, where he was bound, nor had I the vaguest notion of his errand, but all the fear of the night before returned; and as he stood, smiling, on the terrace, ready to set out, I implored him to stay, and to forget all his dreams of the undiscovered continent.

“No, no, Miss Lally,” he replied, still smiling, “it’s too late now. *Vestigia nulla retrorsum*, you know, is the device of all true explorers, though I hope it won’t be literally true in my case. But, indeed, you are wrong to alarm yourself so; I look upon my little expedition as quite commonplace; no more exciting than a day with the geological hammers. There is a risk, of course, but so there is on the commonest excursion. I can afford to be jaunty; I am doing nothing so hazardous as ’Arry does a hundred times over in the course of every Bank Holiday. Well, then, you must look more cheerfully; and so goodbye till tomorrow at latest.”

===Chinese===
我听着，凭我对格雷格教授脾性的了解，我知道驱使他出行的，绝非寻常的事务或消遣。他要去哪里，我不知道，连远远的猜测也没有；他此行的目的，我更无半点头绪；可昨夜的全部恐惧都回来了。他微笑着立在露台上，即将动身；我恳求他留下，恳求他把那未发现大陆的一切梦都忘掉。

“不，不，拉利小姐，”他仍旧笑着答道，“如今太迟了。*Vestigia nulla retrorsum*（拉丁文：决不原路折返），您知道，这是一切真正探险家的箴言；不过在我这里，但愿它不至于字字应验。说真的，您这样自惊自吓是不对的；我把这趟小小的远征看得平平常常；还不及带着地质锤去敲敲打打一天来得刺激。风险自然有，可最寻常的出游哪样没有风险？我尽可以轻松些；我干的这点事，还比不上‘阿利’（’Arry）们在每个银行假日（Bank Holiday）里干上一百回的呢。好了，您得高兴些才是；那么再会了，至迟明天见。”

===Original===
He walked briskly up the road, and I saw him open the gate that marks the entrance of the wood, and then he vanished in the gloom of the trees.

All the day passed heavily with a strange darkness in the air, and again I felt as if imprisoned amidst the ancient woods, shut in an olden land of mystery and dread, and as if all was long ago and forgotten by the living outside. I hoped and dreaded, and when the dinner-hour came, I waited expecting to hear the professor’s step in the hall, and his voice exulting at I knew not what triumph. I composed my face to welcome him gladly, but the night descended dark, and he did not come.

===Chinese===
他脚步轻快地上了路；我看见他推开标志着树林入口的那道门，随后便隐没在树影的幽暗里了。

整整一天，天色沉沉地挨过去，空气里有一种奇异的晦暗；我又觉得自己像被囚在古老的树林中间，关在一片神秘与惊惶的太古之地，仿佛一切早已成为往昔，早被外间的生者遗忘。我盼着，又怕着；到了晚饭时分，我守着，等着听教授的脚步声在大厅里响起，等着听他为何种我不知情的凯旋扬声欢呼。我把面容整备成欢喜迎他的模样；可夜色黑沉沉地落下来，他没有回来。

===Original===
In the morning when the maid knocked at my door, I called out to her, and asked if her master had returned; and when she replied that his bedroom stood open and empty, I felt the cold clasp of despair. Still, I fancied he might have discovered genial company, and would return for luncheon, or perhaps in the afternoon, and I took the children for a walk in the forest, and tried my best to play and laugh with them, and to shut out the thoughts of mystery and veiled terror. Hour after hour I waited, and my thoughts grew darker; again the night came and found me watching, and at last, as I was making much ado to finish my dinner, I heard steps outside and the sound of a man’s voice.

The maid came in and looked oddly at me.

“Please, miss,” she began, “Mr. Morgan the gardener wants to speak to you for a minute, if you didn’t mind.”

“Show him in, please,” I answered, and I set my lips tight.

The old man came slowly into the room, and the servant shut the door behind him.

“Sit down, Mr. Morgan,” I said; “what is it that you want to say to me?”

===Chinese===
早晨女仆来敲我的门，我朝她喊，问老爷回来了没有；她答说，他的卧室敞着，空着，我只觉绝望冰冷地扼住了我。尽管如此，我还痴想他或许遇到了投契的旅伴，午饭时、或下午总会回来；我领着孩子们到林中散步，竭力同他们玩耍说笑，好把那些神秘与掩面恐怖的念头关在门外。一个钟头又一个钟头，我等着，念头愈等愈暗；夜又一次降临，见我仍在守望；末了，我正强打精神对付着吃完晚饭，忽听得外面响起脚步声和一个男人的说话声。

女仆走进来，神色古怪地望着我。

“对不起，小姐，”她开口道，“园丁摩根先生想同您说句话，不知您方不方便。”

“请他进来吧。”我答道，双唇紧紧抿住。

老人缓缓走进屋来，女仆随手掩上了门。

“请坐，摩根先生，”我说，“您想同我说什么？”

===Original===
“Well, miss, Mr. Gregg he gave me something for you yesterday morning, just before he went off; and he told me particular not to hand it up before eight o’clock this evening exactly, if so be as he wasn’t back again home before, and if he should come home before I was just to return it to him in his own hands. So, you see, as Mr. Gregg isn’t here yet, I suppose I’d better give you the parcel directly.”

He pulled out something from his pocket, and gave it to me, half rising. I took it silently, and seeing that Morgan seemed doubtful as to what he was to do next, I thanked him and bade him good night, and he went out. I was left alone in the room with the parcel in my hand﻿—a paper parcel neatly sealed and directed to me, with the instructions Morgan had quoted all written in the professor’s large loose hand. I broke the seals with a choking at my heart, and found an envelope inside, addressed also, but open, and I took the letter out.

===Chinese===
“是这样的，小姐，格雷格先生昨天早晨临走之前，交给我一样给您的物件；还特别嘱咐我，非得等到今晚八点整才能递上来，倘若他先前没回来；要是他回来了呢，我就得原封不动、亲手还给他。您瞧，格雷格先生到现在还没回来，我想，这包裹我不如这就交给您的好。”

他从口袋里掏出一件东西，半起身递给我。我默默接过；见摩根似乎拿不准接下来该怎么办，我谢了他，道了晚安，他便出去了。屋里只剩我一个人，手里托着那包裹——一封纸包，封得整整齐齐，写着我的名字，摩根方才转述的那番嘱咐，全用教授那手松大的字写在上面。我拆开封缄，心头一阵哽咽；里面是一只信封，也写着名字，却没封口；我抽出信来。

===Original===
> “My dear Miss Lally,” it began, “To quote the old logic manual, the case of your reading this note is a case of my having made a blunder of some sort, and, I am afraid, a blunder that turns these lines into a farewell. It is practically certain that neither you nor anyone else will ever see me again. I have made my will with provision for this eventuality, and I hope you will consent to accept the small remembrance addressed to you, and my sincere thanks for the way in which you joined your fortunes to mine. The fate which has come upon me is desperate and terrible beyond the remotest dreams of man; but this fate you have a right to know﻿—if you please. If you look in the left-hand drawer of my dressing-table, you will find the key of the escritoire, properly labelled. In the well of the escritoire is a large envelope sealed and addressed to your name. I advise you to throw it forthwith into the fire; you will sleep better of nights if you do so. But if you must know the history of what has happened, it is all written down for you to read.”

===Chinese===
> “亲爱的拉利小姐，”信是这样开头的，“援引旧逻辑教科书上的话：您读到这封短简的情境，便是我在什么事上失了手的情境；而且恐怕，这一失手，要使这几行字成为诀别。几乎可以断定，您和任何别人，都永远见不到我了。我已立下遗嘱，为此等不测作了安排；谨望您肯收下那份写明赠与您的薄礼，并请接受我至诚的谢忱，谢您把自己的命运同我的连在一起。落到我头上的这个命，其绝望与可怕，远超世人最迢远的梦；但这命运，您有权知道——如果您愿意的话。您若看我梳妆台的左手抽屉，会寻到写字柜的钥匙，标签贴得妥妥当当。写字柜的柜井里，有一只封好、写着您名字的大信封。我劝您把它即刻投进火里；这样做了，您夜里会睡得安稳些。可您若一定要知道已发生之事的原委，那一切都已写下，供您阅读。”

===Original===
The signature was firmly written below, and again I turned the page and read out the words one by one, aghast and white to the lips, my hands cold as ice, and sickness choking me. The dead silence of the room, and the thought of the dark woods and hills closing me in on every side, oppressed me, helpless and without capacity, and not knowing where to turn for counsel. At last I resolved that though knowledge should haunt my whole life and all the days to come, I must know the meaning of the strange terrors that had so long tormented me, rising gray, dim, and awful, like the shadows in the wood at dusk. I carefully carried out Professor Gregg’s directions, and not without reluctance broke the seal of the envelope, and spread out his manuscript before me. That manuscript I always carry with me, and I see that I cannot deny your unspoken request to read it. This, then, was what I read that night, sitting at the desk, with a shaded lamp beside me.

===Chinese===
签名坚实地写在末尾。我又把那一页纸翻过来，一个词一个词地读下去，惊骇得嘴唇全无血色，双手冰凉，恶心哽在喉头。屋里死一般的寂静，四面合围的黑林与黑山的念头压着我，我无助，无力，不知该向何处讨主意。末了我立下决心：哪怕这知识要纠缠我的一生、纠缠此后所有的日子，我也必须知道，那长久折磨着我的怪怖究竟是什么意思——它们灰暗、朦胧而可怖地升起，如黄昏林中的阴影。我一板一眼地照格雷格教授的指示做了，并非没有犹豫地拆开信封的火漆，把手稿在我面前展开。那份手稿，我至今随身携带；我看出，您那一句未曾出口的“愿闻其详”，我是无法拒绝的了。那一夜，我坐在书桌前，身边一盏罩灯，我读到的，便是下面这些。

---

===Original===
The young lady who called herself Miss Lally then proceeded to recite:﻿

===Chinese===
这位自称拉利小姐的年轻姑娘，随即开始诵读：——

===Original===
> It is many years since the first glimmer of the theory which is now almost, if not quite, reduced to fact dawned first on my mind. A somewhat extensive course of miscellaneous and obsolete reading had done a good deal to prepare the way, and, later, when I became somewhat of a specialist and immersed myself in the studies known as ethnological, I was now and then startled by facts that would not square with orthodox scientific opinion, and by discoveries that seemed to hint at something still hidden for all our research. More particularly I became convinced that much of the folklore of the world is but an exaggerated account of events that really happened, and I was especially drawn to consider the stories of the fairies, the good folk of the Celtic races. Here I thought I could detect the fringe of embroidery and exaggeration, the fantastic guise, the little people dressed in green and gold sporting in the flowers, and I thought I saw a distinct analogy between the name given to this race (supposed to be imaginary) and the description of their appearance and manners. Just as our remote ancestors called the dreaded beings “fair” and “good” precisely because they dreaded them, so they had dressed them up in charming forms, knowing the truth to be the very reverse. Literature, too, had gone early to work, and had lent a powerful hand in the transformation, so that the playful elves of Shakespeare are already far removed from the true original, and the real horror is disguised in a form of prankish mischief. But in the older tales, the stories that used to make men cross themselves as they sat round the burning logs, we tread a different stage; I saw a widely opposed spirit in certain histories of children and of men and women who vanished strangely from the earth. They would be seen by a peasant in the fields walking towards some green and rounded hillock, and seen no more on earth; and there are stories of mothers who have left a child quietly sleeping with the cottage door rudely barred with a piece of wood, and have returned, not to find the plump and rosy little Saxon, but a thin and wizened creature, with sallow skin and black piercing eyes, the child of another race. Then, again, there were myths darker still; the dread of witch and wizard, the lurid evil of the Sabbath, and the hint of demons who mingled with the daughters of men. And just as we have turned the terrible “fair folk” into a company of benignant, if freakish, elves, so we have hidden from us the black foulness of the witch and her companions under a popular diablerie of old women and broomsticks and a comic cat with tail on end. So the Greeks called the hideous furies benevolent ladies, and thus the northern nations have followed their example. I pursued my investigations, stealing odd hours from other and more imperative labors, and I asked myself the question: Supposing these traditions to be true, who were the demons who are reported to have attended the Sabbaths? I need not say that I laid aside what I may call the supernatural hypothesis of the middle ages, and came to the conclusion that fairies and devils were of one and the same race and origin; invention, no doubt, and the Gothic fancy of old days had done much in the way of exaggeration and distortion; yet I firmly believed that beneath all this imagery there was a black background of truth. As for some of the alleged wonders, I hesitated. While I should be very loth to receive any one specific instance of modern spiritualism as containing even a grain of the genuine, yet I was not wholly prepared to deny that human flesh may now and then, once perhaps in ten million cases, be the veil of powers which seem magical to us; powers which, so far from proceeding from the heights and leading men thither, are in reality survivals from the depths of being. The amoeba and the snail have powers which we do not possess; and I thought it possible that the theory of reversion might explain many things which seem wholly inexplicable. Thus stood my position; I saw good reason to believe that much of the tradition, a vast deal of the earliest and uncorrupted tradition of the so-called fairies, represented solid fact, and I thought that the purely supernatural element in these traditions was to be accounted for on the hypothesis that a race which had fallen out of the grand march of evolution might have retained, as a survival, certain powers which would be to us wholly miraculous. Such was my theory as it stood conceived in my mind; and working with this in view, I seemed to gather confirmation from every side, from the spoils of a tumulus or a barrow, from a local paper reporting an antiquarian meeting in the country, and from general literature of all kinds. Amongst other instances, I remember being struck by the phrase “articulate-speaking men” in Homer, as if the writer knew or had heard of men whose speech was so rude that it could hardly be termed articulate; and on my hypothesis of a race who had lagged far behind the rest, I could easily conceive that such a folk would speak a jargon but little removed from the inarticulate noises of brute-beasts.

===Chinese===
> 我的这条理论——如今几乎、若非全然，已经化作了事实——最初在我心头投下一线微光，已是多年以前的事了。一段相当驳杂的、涉猎废旧之学的阅读，为它铺好了路；后来，当我略略成了专门家、沉浸在所谓民族学的研究里时，又不时被一些事实惊动——它们不肯就范于正统的科学成见；被一些发现惊动——它们仿佛暗示，尽管我们有这许多考索，仍有某种藏而未露的东西。我尤其被引去琢磨小仙（fairies）的故事，克尔特诸族所谓“好百姓”（the good folk）的故事。在这里，我以为自己辨认出了缘饰与夸大的边穗，那奇幻的伪装：小小人身着绿衣金裳，在花间嬉戏；我还以为，自己在这一种族（世人只当是虚构）的名号，与他们形貌行止的描述之间，看出了一种确凿的类比。正如我们远祖正因为惧怕那些可畏之物，才恰恰称他们为“美丽”（fair）与“善良”（good），他们同样把他们装扮成千娇百媚的形象，心里却深知真相恰是其反面。文学也早早地插了手，为这场变形助了一臂大力；于是莎士比亚笔下顽皮的精灵，距离真正的原型已属遥远，真正的恐怖，被装进了一个恶作剧的滑稽外形之下。可是在更古老的传说里，在那些昔日教人围坐炭火、听得连连画十字的故事里，我们踏上了另一个层面；在某些孩童与男女离奇地从世上消失的记载里，我看出一种截然相异的精神。农人会看见他们在田里朝某座青翠浑圆的土丘走去，从此在世上再无踪影；又有这样的故事：母亲把熟睡的婴孩留在家里，用一根木棍把茅屋的门草草闩上，回来时寻到的，已不是那白胖红润的撒克逊小儿，而是一个干瘦枯皱的造物，灰黄的皮肤，黑而锐利的眼睛，另一个种族的孩子。再者，还有更黑暗的神话：对女巫与男巫的恐惧，巫魔夜会（Sabbath）那阴惨的邪恶，以及恶魔与人类之女交合的暗示。正如我们已把可怖的“美丽种族”改造成一群纵然促狭、倒也和善的精灵，我们也把女巫及其同伙的黑秽，藏进了一套老太婆、扫帚柄、尾巴直竖的滑稽猫儿的通俗鬼把戏里。希腊人把狰狞的复仇女神唤作仁慈的女士，北方诸族亦步其后尘。我窃取零星的时辰于别样更要紧的劳作之外，推进着我的考察，并向自己发问：倘若这些传说属实，那么据称出没于巫魔会的，究竟是些什么妖魔？无须说，我撇开了那可称为中世纪超自然论的假设，得出结论：小仙与魔鬼同属一族、同出一源；无疑，虚构与旧日哥特式的幻想，在夸饰与扭曲上出了大力；然而我坚信，这一切意象底下，横着一层黑暗的真相底子。至于若干所谓奇观，我尚踌躇。现代招魂术的任何一个具体案例，我纵然极不愿承认其含有一粒真金；可若说人的血肉之躯，间或——譬如千万例中之一——或为某种于我们貌似魔法的权能之帷幕，我却也不至于全盘否认；这等权能，非但不自高处而来、引人上升，实则倒是生命深处的孑遗。变形虫与蜗牛，自有我们所不具备的权能；我以为，返祖的理论，或可解释许多看似全然无解之事。我的立场便是如此：我有充分的理由相信，传说的一大部分，那所谓小仙的最早、未经窜改的传说的大部分，代表着坚实的事实；而传说中纯粹超自然的成分，则可以这样解释：一个从进化大进军中掉队的种族，作为孑遗，保留了某些于我们全然是奇迹的权能。这便是我心中构想的那个理论；怀着这一目标工作，我仿佛从四面八方都采到了印证：从古冢或坟丘的出土物，从报道乡间古物学会集会的地方报纸，从各色一般的书刊。诸例之中，我记得曾为荷马那句“言语清晰的人们”所触动，仿佛作者知道、或听说过某种人，其言语粗拙得几乎称不上清晰；依我这掉队种族的假设，我不难设想，这样一个族类说的，必是一种黑话，同野兽那不成人声的嘈叫相去无几。

===Original===
> Thus I stood, satisfied that my conjecture was at all events not far removed from fact, when a chance paragraph in a small country print one day arrested my attention. It was a short account of what was to all appearance the usual sordid tragedy of the village; a young girl unaccountably missing, and evil rumor blatant and busy with her reputation. Yet I could read between the lines that all this scandal was purely hypothetical, and in all probability invented to account for what was in any other manner unaccountable. A flight to London or Liverpool, or an undiscovered body lying with a weight about its neck in the foul depths of a woodland pool, or perhaps murder﻿—such were the theories of the wretched girl’s neighbors. But as I idly scanned the paragraph, a flash of thought passed through me with the violence of an electric shock: What if the obscure and horrible race of the hills still survived, still remained haunting wild places, and barren hills, and now and then repeating the evil of Gothic legend, unchanged and unchangeable as the Turanian Shelta, or the Basques of Spain. I have said that the thought came with violence; and indeed I drew in my breath sharply, and clung with both hands to my elbow-chair, in a strange confusion of horror and elation. It was as if one of my confrères of physical science, roaming in a quiet English wood, had been suddenly stricken aghast by the presence of the slimy and loathsome terror of the ichthyosaurus, the original of the stories of the awful worms killed by valorous knights, or had seen the sun darkened by the pterodactyl, the dragon of tradition. Yet as a resolute explorer of knowledge, the thought of such a discovery threw me into a passion of joy, and I cut out the slip from the paper, and put it in a drawer in my old bureau, resolved that it should be but the first piece in a collection of the strangest significance. I sat long that evening dreaming of the conclusions I should establish, nor did cooler reflection at first dash my confidence. Yet as I began to put the case fairly, I saw that I might be building on an unstable foundation; the facts might possibly be in accordance with local opinion; and I regarded the affair with a mood of some reserve. Yet I resolved to remain perched on the lookout, and I hugged to myself the thought that I alone was watching and wakeful, while the great crowd of thinkers and searchers stood heedless and indifferent, perhaps letting the most prerogative facts pass by unnoticed.

===Chinese===
> 我正站在这般境地，自谓猜想纵不中的亦不远矣，不料一份乡间小报上一段偶然的文字，一日攫住了我的注意。那是一篇短讯，讲的看来无非村野惯常的那类污秽悲剧：一个年轻姑娘无缘无故失踪，恶声嚣嚣，闹得沸沸扬扬。可我读出了字里行间：这满城风雨纯属臆测，十之八九是编出来解释那无从解释之事的。逃往伦敦或利物浦，或是一具无人发现的尸身，颈上坠着重物，沉在林中池塘的污深处，再不然便是谋杀——这便是那可怜姑娘的邻居们的高论。可当我懒懒扫过那段文字，一道思想的闪电携着电击般的暴力穿过我：倘若那隐秘而可怖的山中之族依然存世，依然出没于荒野与瘠山之间，时时重演哥特传说的罪恶，一成不变亦不可变易，一如图兰人的谢尔塔语（Turanian Shelta），或西班牙的巴斯克人（Basques），那又如何？我说过，这念头来得凶猛；的确，我猛地抽了一口气，双手死死扒住扶手椅，在一阵惊骇与狂喜交织的迷乱里。这就仿佛我的一位自然科学同侪，在一座安静的英国树林里游逛，骤然被鱼龙（ichthyosaurus）——勇士骑士们斩杀的可怖巨蟒，其故事的原型——那黏滑可憎的恐怖吓得魂飞魄散，或是亲眼见太阳被翼手龙（pterodactyl）——传说里的巨龙——遮黑。然而，作为知识的一名果决的探险者，这样一桩发现的前景，反倒把我掷进一阵狂喜；我把那段文字从报上剪下，放进旧写字柜的一只抽屉，决意让它只做一批收藏的第一件——一批意义最不寻常的收藏。那晚我久坐梦想着我将确立的种种结论，而较为冷静的反省起初也不曾挫我的信心。可待我开始公允地掂量此案，便明白自己或许正筑基于一方不稳的地基；事实或许竟与乡里的舆论相合；我便怀着几分保留看待这件事了。然而我决意守在瞭望位上，并且暗自得意地想着：唯有我一人醒着守望，而那一大群思想者与搜寻者兀自漫不经心、漠然置之，或许正任凭那些最堪先录的事实，无人察觉地从旁溜过。

===Original===
> Several years elapsed before I was enabled to add to the contents of the drawer; and the second find was in reality not a valuable one, for it was a mere repetition of the first, with only the variation of another and distant locality. Yet I gained something; for in the second case, as in the first, the tragedy took place in a desolate and lonely country, and so far my theory seemed justified. But the third piece was to me far more decisive. Again, amongst outland hills, far even from a main road of traffic, an old man was found done to death, and the instrument of execution was left beside him. Here, indeed, there was rumor and conjecture, for the deadly tool was a primitive stone axe, bound by gut to the wooden handle, and surmises the most extravagant and improbable were indulged in. Yet, as I thought with a kind of glee, the wildest conjectures went far astray; and I took the pains to enter into correspondence with the local doctor, who was called at the inquest. He, a man of some acuteness, was dumbfounded. “It will not do to speak of these things in country places,” he wrote to me; “but, frankly, Professor Gregg, there is some hideous mystery here. I have obtained possession of the stone axe, and have been so curious as to test its powers. I took it into the back-garden of my house one Sunday afternoon when my family and the servants were all out, and there, sheltered by the poplar hedges, I made my experiments. I found the thing utterly unmanageable. Whether there is some peculiar balance, some nice adjustment of weights, which require incessant practice, or whether an effectual blow can be struck only by a certain trick of the muscles, I do not know; but I assure you that I went into the house with but a sorry opinion of my athletic capacities. It was like an inexperienced man trying ‘putting the hammer’; the force exerted seemed to return on oneself, and I found myself hurled backwards with violence, while the axe fell harmless to the ground. On another occasion I tried the experiment with a clever woodman of the place; but this man, who had handled his axe for forty years, could do nothing with the stone implement, and missed every stroke most ludicrously. In short, if it were not so supremely absurd, I should say that for four thousand years no one on earth could have struck an effective blow with the tool that undoubtedly was used to murder the old man.” This, as may be imagined, was to me rare news; and afterwards, when I heard the whole story, and learned that the unfortunate old man had babbled tales of what might be seen at night on a certain wild hillside, hinting at unheard-of wonders, and that he had been found cold one morning on the very hill in question, my exultation was extreme, for I felt I was leaving conjecture far behind me. But the next step was of still greater importance. I had possessed for many years an extraordinary stone seal﻿—a piece of dull black stone, two inches long from the handle to the stamp, and the stamping end a rough hexagon an inch and a quarter in diameter. Altogether, it presented the appearance of an enlarged tobacco-stopper of an old-fashioned make. It had been sent to me by an agent in the East, who informed me that it had been found near the site of the ancient Babylon. But the characters engraved on the seal were to me an intolerable puzzle. Somewhat of the cuneiform pattern, there were yet striking differences, which I detected at the first glance, and all efforts to read the inscription on the hypothesis that the rules for deciphering the arrow-headed writing would apply proved futile. A riddle such as this stung my pride, and at odd moments I would take the Black Seal out of the cabinet, and scrutinize it with so much idle perseverance that every letter was familiar to my mind, and I could have drawn the inscription from memory without the slightest error. Judge then of my surprise, when I one day received from a correspondent in the west of England a letter and an enclosure that positively left me thunderstruck. I saw carefully traced on a large piece of paper the very characters of the Black Seal, without alteration of any kind, and above the inscription my friend had written: Inscription found on a limestone rock on the Gray Hills, Monmouthshire. Done in some red earth and quite recent. I turned to the letter. My friend wrote: “I send you the enclosed inscription with all due reserve. A shepherd who passed by the stone a week ago swears that there was then no mark of any kind. The characters, as I have noted, are formed by drawing some red earth over the stone, and are of an average height of one inch. They look to me like a kind of cuneiform character, a good deal altered, but this of course is impossible. It may be either a hoax or more probably some scribble of the gypsies, who are plentiful enough in this wild country. They have, as you are aware, many hieroglyphics which they use in communicating with one another. I happened to visit the stone in question two days ago in connection with a rather painful incident which has occurred here.”

===Chinese===
> 数年过去，我才有余力为那抽屉添藏；第二件发现其实算不得宝贵，因为它不过是第一件的重演，只换了另一个遥远的地点。但我仍有收获：第二案同第一案一样，惨剧都发生在一处荒僻孤绝之乡，至此，我的理论似乎得了佐证。而第三件，于我则决定性远甚。又一次，在穷乡僻壤的乱山之间，离一条通衢大路也还远得很，一个老人被发现惨遭毒手，行刑的器械就丢在他身旁。这一回自然流言四起、猜测纷纭，因为那夺命的凶器是一柄原始的石斧，以肠线缚在木柄上；于是种种最荒诞、最不着边际的臆想竞相出笼。然而——我带着几分幸灾乐祸想——最狂放的猜想也远远脱了靶；我不惮烦劳，同验尸时被传的那位当地医生通了信。他是个颇有头脑的人，却惊得瞠目结舌。“这些事情，在乡间地方是不好明说的，”他写信给我，“可是坦白说，格雷格教授，这里头有一桩骇人的哑谜。那柄石斧已归我所有，我还一时好奇，试了试它的斤两。一个星期日的下午，家里人和仆役都出去了，我把它拿到我家后园，在白杨树篱的遮蔽下做了我的实验。我发现这东西全然不听使唤。是不是里面有什么特异的配重、什么精微的衡准，须得不停地练习；还是说，有效的一击，非得某种肌肉上的诀窍不可——我不知道；可我向您担保，我进屋时，对我自己的运动本领只落得一声长叹。那就像一个生手去掷链球（putting the hammer）；使出的力气仿佛全回到自己身上，我被狠狠地掀了回去，那斧子却无害地落在地上。另一次，我同本地一个机灵的樵夫一同试验；此人使了四十年斧子，对着那件石器却一筹莫展，每一斧都劈空，滑稽之至。总而言之，倘若事情不是荒谬到极点，我就只好说：四千年来，地上没有一个凡人，能用那件毫无疑问是用来杀死老人的家伙，击出有效的一击。”——可想而知，这于我是稀世的喜讯；其后，待我听全了始末，得知那不幸的老人生前曾絮絮叨叨，讲某个荒山坡上夜里可以看见什么，暗示着闻所未闻的奇观，而他偏就在那座山上，一天早晨被人发现死僵了，我的得意更是到了极处，因为我觉得自己已把猜想远远甩在身后。而下一步，其重要犹有过之。多年以来，我一直藏着一方异样的石印——一段暗黑的石头，从柄端到印面长两英寸，钤印的一端是个粗糙的六角形，直径一英寸又四分之一。通体看去，它像一枚放大了的老式压烟器。它是一位东方的经纪人寄给我的，来函说，它是在古巴比伦城址附近出土的。可印上镌的字符，于我是一道无法容忍的谜。字形略带楔形文字的风范，差异却也触目，我一眼便看了出来；一切想按破译箭头形文字的通则来解读这铭文的尝试，全归徒劳。这样的哑谜刺痛我的自尊；闲暇的时刻，我便把黑印从柜里取出来，以那样无所事事的坚韧端详它，直到每一个字母都烂熟于心，能凭记忆一笔不误地把铭文画出来。那么请设想我的惊愕：一天，英格兰西部的一位通信人来信，随函附来一件东西，当真把我劈得目瞪口呆。一大张纸上，端端正正摹着黑印上那批字符，分毫不改；铭文上方，我朋友写道：蒙茅斯郡灰山石灰岩上所见铭文。以某种红土画成，为时甚近。我翻到信上。朋友写道：“兹将铭文摹本奉上，姑妄存之。一星期前路过那块石头的牧羊人赌咒说，当时上面没有任何记号。如我所注，字符系以某种红土画于石上，平均高约一英寸。依我看，它们像一种楔形文字，只是大大变了样；当然，这是不可能的。这或是一场恶作剧，或者更可能是吉卜赛人的什么涂鸦——在这片荒乡野地，他们多的是。您知道，他们自有种种象形符号，用来彼此通信。前日我因本地发生的一桩颇为痛心的事件，恰巧去看过那块石头。”

===Original===
> As may be supposed, I wrote immediately to my friend, thanking him for the copy of the inscription, and asking him in a casual manner, the history of the incident he mentioned. To be brief, I heard that a woman named Cradock, who had lost her husband a day before, had set out to communicate the sad news to a cousin who lived some five miles away. She took a shortcut which led by the Gray Hills. Mrs. Cradock, who was then quite a young woman, never arrived at her relative’s house. Late that night a farmer who had lost a couple of sheep, supposed to have wandered from the flock, was walking over the Gray Hills, with a lantern and his dog. His attention was attracted by a noise, which he described as a kind of wailing, mournful and pitiable to hear; and, guided by the sound, he found the unfortunate Mrs. Cradock crouched on the ground by the limestone rock, swaying her body to and fro, and lamenting and crying in so heartrending a manner that the farmer was, as he says, at first obliged to stop his ears, or he would have run away. The woman allowed herself to be taken home, and a neighbor came to see to her necessities. All the night she never ceased her crying, mixing her lament with words of some unintelligible jargon, and when the doctor arrived he pronounced her insane. She lay on her bed for a week, now wailing, as people said, like one lost and damned for eternity, and now sunk in a heavy coma; it was thought that grief at the loss of her husband had unsettled her mind, and the medical man did not at one time expect her to live. I need not say that I was deeply interested in this story, and I made my friend write to me at intervals with all the particulars of the case. I heard then that in the course of six weeks the woman gradually recovered the use of her faculties and some months later she gave birth to a son, christened Jervase, who unhappily proved to be of weak intellect. Such were the facts known to the village; but to me while I whitened at the suggested thought of the hideous enormities that had doubtless been committed, all this was nothing short of conviction, and I incautiously hazarded a hint of something like the truth to some scientific friends. The moment the words had left my lips I bitterly regretted having spoken, and thus given away the great secret of my life, but with a good deal of relief mixed with indignation, I found my fears altogether misplaced, for my friends ridiculed me to my face, and I was regarded as a madman; and beneath a natural anger I chuckled to myself, feeling as secure amidst these blockheads, as if I had confided what I knew to the desert sands.

===Chinese===
> 可以想见，我立即复信，谢过他惠寄铭文摹本，又以闲闲的口气，问起他提到的那桩事件的始末。简而言之，我听说有位姓克拉多克的妇人，丈夫死去才一日，动身去给一位住在五英里外的表亲报丧。她抄了一条经由灰山的近路。克拉多克太太那时还是个十分年轻的妇人，始终没有走到亲戚家。当夜深时分，一个农夫丢了两只羊，疑是离了群，提着灯笼、带着狗在灰山上寻。一片声响引他侧目——他说那像一种哀号，凄楚难闻；循声寻去，他发现不幸的克拉多克太太蜷伏在石灰岩旁，身子前后摇晃，哭得那样肝肠寸断，农夫说，他起初只好捂住耳朵，不然早逃了。妇人由人送回家去，一个邻人来照应她。整夜她哭声不绝，哀号里夹着无人能懂的黑话词句；医生到时，断为精神失常。她在床上躺了一星期，时而哀号——人们说——像被弃绝而永堕之人，时而沉入沉重的昏迷；众人都说是丧夫之恸乱了她的神，医生一度以为她性命不保。无须说，我对这故事何等关心；我请我朋友随时把案中种种细节函告。后来听说，六个星期后她渐渐恢复了神智；数月之后产下一子，取名贾维斯，不幸智识孱弱。村人所知，尽于此矣；可于我——一念及那多半已经犯下的可憎罪行，我便周身发白——这一切不啻是定谳；我一时不慎，向几位科学界的朋友漏了半句近乎真相的口风。话一出口我便痛悔莫及，就此把我此生的大秘密送了人；可随即，释然与愤懑交并，我发现我的忧惧全然落空：朋友们当面把我嘲笑了个够，我被当成了疯子；在一层自然的恼怒底下，我暗自好笑，置身这班蠢物之间，安稳得很，就像把我知道的一切，托付给了漠漠黄沙。

===Original===
> But now, knowing so much, I resolved I would know all, and I concentrated my efforts on the task of deciphering the inscription on the Black Seal. For many years I made this puzzle the sole object of my leisure moments; for the greater portion of my time was, of course, devoted to other duties, and it was only now and then that I could snatch a week of clear research. If I were to tell the full history of this curious investigation, this statement would be wearisome in the extreme, for it would contain simply the account of long and tedious failure. By what I knew already of ancient scripts I was well-equipped for the chase, as I always termed it to myself. I had correspondents amongst all the scientific men in Europe, and, indeed, in the world, and I could not believe that in these days any character, however ancient and however perplexed, could long resist the searchlight I should bring to bear upon it. Yet, in point of fact, it was fully fourteen years before I succeeded. With every year my professional duties increased, and my leisure became smaller. This no doubt retarded me a good deal; and yet, when I look back on those years I am astonished at the vast scope of my investigation of the Black Seal. I made my bureau a centre, and from all the world and from all the ages I gathered transcripts of ancient writing. Nothing, I resolved, should pass me unawares, and the faintest hint should be welcomed and followed up. But as one covert after another was tried and proved empty of result, I began in the course of years to despair, and to wonder whether the Black Seal were the sole relic of some race that had vanished from the world and left no other trace of its existence﻿—had perished, in fine, as Atlantis is said to have done, in some great cataclysm, its secrets perhaps drowned beneath the ocean or moulded into the heart of the hills. The thought chilled my warmth a little, and though I still persevered, it was no longer with the same certainty of faith. A chance came to the rescue. I was staying in a considerable town in the north of England, and took the opportunity of going over the very creditable museum that had for some time been established in the place. The curator was one of my correspondents; and, as we were looking through one of the mineral cases, my attention was struck by a specimen, a piece of black stone some four inches square, the appearance of which reminded me in a measure of the Black Seal. I took it up carelessly, and was turning it over in my hand, when I saw, to my astonishment, that the under side was inscribed. I said, quietly enough, to my friend the curator that the specimen interested me, and that I should be much obliged if he would allow me to take it with me to my hotel for a couple of days. He, of course, made no objection, and I hurried to my rooms, and found that my first glance had not deceived me. There were two inscriptions; one in the regular cuneiform character, another in the character of the Black Seal, and I realized that my task was accomplished. I made an exact copy of the two inscriptions; and when I got to my London study, and had the Seal before me, I was able seriously to grapple with the great problem. The interpreting inscription on the museum specimen, though in itself curious enough, did not bear on my quest, but the transliteration made me master of the secret of the Black Seal. Conjecture, of course, had to enter into my calculations; there was here and there uncertainty about a particular ideograph, and one sign recurring again and again on the Seal baffled me for many successive nights. But at last the secret stood open before me in plain English, and I read the key of the awful transmutation of the hills. The last word was hardly written, when with fingers all trembling and unsteady I tore the scrap of paper into the minutest fragments, and saw them flame and blacken in the red hollow of the fire, and then I crushed the gray films that remained into finest powder. Never since then have I written those words; never will I write the phrases which tell me how man can be reduced to the slime from which he came, and be forced to put on the flesh of the reptile and the snake. There was now but one thing remaining. I knew; but I desired to see, and I was after some time able to take a house in the neighborhood of the Gray Hills, and not far from the cottage where Mrs. Cradock and her son Jervase resided. I need not go into a full and detailed account of the apparently inexplicable events which have occurred here, where I am writing this. I knew that I should find in Jervase Cradock something of the blood of the “Little People,” and I found later that he had more than once encountered his kinsmen in lonely places in that lonely land. When I was summoned one day to the garden, and found him in a seizure speaking or hissing the ghastly jargon of the Black Seal, I am afraid that exultation prevailed over pity. I heard bursting from his lips the secrets of the underworld, and the word of dread, “Ishakshar,” the signification of which I must be excused from giving.

===Chinese===
> 然而如今，既已知道这么多，我决意知道全部；我把全副力气集中在破解黑印记文这一桩任务上。多年以来，这哑谜便是我闲暇时刻唯一的目标；我的大部分时间，自然尽于别样的职守，不过偶尔方能偷得一星期干净的研究工夫。倘要把这项奇特考察的全史一一叙来，这份陈述未免沉闷之至，因为它无非是一部旷日持久、屡战屡败的流水账。凭我对古文字的所知，我于这场追猎——我私下总这样称呼它——原是装备精良。整个欧洲、乃至整个世界的科学之士中，我皆有通信之人；我不能相信，在这年头，任何文字，任它多古、多纠缠，能长久挡得住我将投射过去的那道探照灯。然而，事实上，足足十四年过去，我方才成功。年复一年，我的本职负担愈重，闲暇愈少。这无疑大大耽搁了我；可如今回望那些年，我仍旧惊诧于自己对黑印考察的规模之阔。我以我的写字柜为中心，从全世界、从一切世代，搜罗古文字的摹本。我决意：任何东西不得从我眼皮底下溜过，最微弱的一点提示也须欢迎、须追索。可当一个又一个藏身之所试过、试一个空一个，几年下来，我开始绝望，开始疑心黑印莫非是某个已从世上消逝的种族仅存的遗物——此外再无踪迹；一句话，如同传闻里亚特兰蒂斯那样，毁于某场大灾变，其秘密或沉于海底，或已揉进群山的心脏。这念头稍挫了我的热焰；我仍旧坚持，却再没有当初那种信仰的笃定了。一个偶然救了场。我客居在英格兰北部一座颇有规模的城市，趁便去逛那里开办有年、颇堪一观的地方博物馆。馆长是我的一位通信人；我们正看一个矿物陈列柜，一件标本引住了我的目光——一块黑石头，四英寸见方，样子有几分叫我想起黑印。我漫不经心把它拿起来，在手里翻着，蓦地不胜惊愕地看见：它的底面刻有铭文。我相当平静地对馆长朋友说，这件标本使我很感兴趣，可否容我带回旅馆两三日。他自然一口应允；我急急赶回寓所，证明我第一眼没有看错。那是两篇铭文：一篇是规规矩矩的楔形文字，另一篇正是黑印的字符；我明白，我的任务成了。我把两篇铭文精确地摹了下来；回到伦敦的书房，把黑印摆在眼前，我便能郑重其事地同这道大难题肉搏了。博物馆标本上那篇起诠释作用的铭文，本身虽也古怪，却与我的追求无涉；可是那转写本，使我做了黑印之秘的主人。推想，自然免不了要进我的推算；这一处那一处的表意字符，尚有存疑；印上一个反复出现的符号，接连许多夜晚令我束手。可末了，那秘密终于用明白的英语敞立在我面前，我读出了群山那可怖变形的钥匙。最后一个词刚落笔，我便用颤抖不稳的手指把那片纸撕成最细的碎屑，看它们在火炉的红膛里烧焰、焦黑，再把剩下的灰膜碾成齑粉。自那以后，我再不曾写下那些字；我也永不会写下那些短语——它们告诉我，人如何能被还原为他所从来的黏泥，被迫换上爬虫与蛇的皮肉。如今只剩一件事了。我已经知道；可我还要亲见。于是我得以在灰山左近租下一所宅子，离克拉多克太太和她的儿子贾维斯居住的村舍不远。我在此间——我写下这些的地方——所经历的种种看似无从解释之事，无须在此详陈。我早知道，我会在贾维斯·克拉多克身上寻到几分“小小人”（the Little People）的血；后来又发现，在那片孤寂之地，他不只一次在僻静处同他的亲族相遇。那一天我被唤到园中，见他发病，说着——嘶着——黑印那骇人的黑话；我怕的是，得胜之情压倒了怜悯。我听见从他唇间迸出的地底世界的诸般秘密，听见那个可怖之词，“伊沙克沙尔”（Ishakshar）；其含义如何，请恕我不宣。

===Original===
> But there is one incident I cannot pass over unnoticed. In the waste hollow of the night I awoke at the sound of those hissing syllables I knew so well; and on going to the wretched boy’s room, I found him convulsed and foaming at the mouth, struggling on the bed as if he strove to escape the grasp of writhing demons. I took him down to my room and lit the lamp, while he lay twisting on the floor, calling on the power within his flesh to leave him. I saw his body swell and become distended as a bladder, while the face blackened before my eyes; and then at the crisis I did what was necessary according to the directions on the Seal, and putting all scruple on one side, I became a man of science, observant of what was passing. Yet the sight I had to witness was horrible, almost beyond the power of human conception and the most fearful fantasy; something pushed out from the body there on the floor, and stretched forth, a slimy wavering tentacle, across the room, and grasped the bust upon the cupboard, and laid it down on my desk.

===Chinese===
> 但有一事，我不能轻轻放过。在夜半荒寂的深处，我在那阵我熟极了的嘶嘶音节里惊醒；走到那可怜孩子的房中，见他痉挛着，口吐白沫，在床上挣扎，仿佛在同攫住他的一群扭动的恶魔搏斗。我把他抱到我的房里，点起灯；他躺在地板上扭动，哀求他血肉之中的那个“它”离开他。我看见他的身子膨胀鼓胀，像一只膀胱，那张脸在我眼前黑下去；随后到了危象的关头，我依照黑印上的指示做了该做之事，把一切顾忌撇在一旁，做一个科学之人，静观其变。然而我不得不目睹的景象，是可怖的，几乎超乎人类想象与最可怖的幻想的能力：有什么东西从地板上那具躯体里推了出来，探出，伸出，一条黏滑摇曳的触腕，横过房间，攫住橱柜顶上的半身像，把它搁到了我的书桌上。

===Original===
> When it was over, and I was left to walk up and down all the rest of the night, white and shuddering, with sweat pouring from my flesh, I vainly tried to reason with myself; I said, truly enough, that I had seen nothing really supernatural, that a snail pushing out his horns and drawing them in was but an instance on a smaller scale of what I had witnessed; and yet horror broke through all such reasonings and left me shattered and loathing myself for the share I had taken in the night’s work.

===Chinese===
> 一切过去之后，我独自来回踱步，挨完那晚余下的全部时辰，面色惨白，浑身战抖，汗水从皮肉里涌出来；我徒然想同自己讲理；我说——说得也很对——我见到的并非真正超自然之物；蜗牛伸出触角又缩回去，不过是我所见之事的一个缩微样本；可恐怖仍旧冲破了这一切道理，留下一个支离破碎的我，为我今夜这桩勾当里出的那份力，厌恶着我自己。

===Original===
> There is little more to be said. I am going now to the final trial and encounter; for I have determined that there shall be nothing wanting, and I shall meet the “Little People” face to face. I shall have the Black Seal and the knowledge of its secrets to help me, and if I unhappily do not return from my journey, there is no need to conjure up here a picture of the awfulness of my fate.

===Chinese===
> 可说的不多了。此刻我就要赴最后的试炼与遭遇；我已决意事事做到万全，我将与“小小人”当面对面。我将带着黑印与它的秘密之学为助；倘若我不幸不得归程，也无须在此描画我那命运的可怖图景了。

===Original===
Pausing a little at the end of Professor Gregg’s statement, Miss Lally continued her tale in the following words:﻿

===Chinese===
拉利小姐在格雷格教授自述的末尾略停了停，随后用下面的话继续讲她的故事：——

===Original===
Such was the almost incredible story that the professor had left behind him. When I had finished reading it, it was late at night, but the next morning I took Morgan with me, and we proceeded to search the Gray Hills for some trace of the lost professor. I will not weary you with a description of the savage desolation of that tract of country, a tract of utterest loneliness, of bare green hills dotted over with gray limestone boulders, worn by the ravage of time into fantastic semblances of men and beasts. Finally, after many hours of weary searching, we found what I told you﻿—the watch and chain, the purse, and the ring﻿—wrapped in a piece of coarse parchment. When Morgan cut the gut that bound the parcel together, and I saw the professor’s property, I burst into tears, but the sight of the dreaded characters of the Black Seal repeated on the parchment froze me to silent horror, and I think I understood for the first time the awful fate that had come upon my late employer.

===Chinese===
教授留下的，便是这样一篇近乎难以置信的故事。我读罢，夜已深了；第二天早晨，我带着摩根动身，往灰山去寻失踪教授的踪迹。那一带乡野荒凉蛮荒的景象，我不再絮絮叨叨地描摹，免得您生厌：一片彻底孤绝之地，光秃秃的青山，四散缀着灰色的石灰岩巨砾，被岁月的剥蚀磨成人与兽的奇形幻状。寻了几个钟头、疲惫不堪之后，我们终于找到了我方才所说的那些——怀表与表链、钱包、戒指——裹在一块粗羊皮纸里。摩根割断扎住包裹的肠线，我看见教授的遗物，泪水夺眶而出；可羊皮纸上重现的黑印字符，那叫人心惊的字符，把我冻成一片无声的恐怖；我想，我头一次彻悟了我那位故主遭逢的是怎样可怖的命运。

===Original===
I have only to add that Professor Gregg’s lawyer treated my account of what had happened as a fairy tale, and refused even to glance at the documents I laid before him. It was he who was responsible for the statement that appeared in the public press, to the effect that Professor Gregg had been drowned, and that his body must have been swept into the open sea.

Miss Lally stopped speaking and looked at Mr. Phillipps, with a glance of some enquiry. He, for his part, was sunken in a deep revery of thought; and when he looked up and saw the bustle of the evening gathering in the square, men and women hurrying to partake of dinner, and crowds already besetting the music-halls, all the hum and press of actual life seemed unreal and visionary, a dream in the morning after an awakening.

===Chinese===
我只须再补一句：格雷格教授的律师把我讲的这一切当作童话，连我摊在他面前的文件也拒绝瞥上一眼。后来见诸报端的那个说法——说格雷格教授溺了水，遗体想必已被冲进外海——正是他一手炮制的。

拉利小姐住了口，带着几分探询望着菲利普斯先生。他呢，正沉在深深的出神里；待到他抬起头来，看见晚间的人潮在广场上聚拢，男男女女赶着去用晚餐，人群已然围住了各家音乐厅——现实生活的这一切喧嚣与拥挤，竟显得虚而不实，如梦如幻，像一场大梦醒后的清晨，回想昨宵梦境。

===Original===
“I thank you,” he said at last, “for your most interesting story, interesting to me, because I feel fully convinced of its exact truth.”

“Sir,” said the lady, with some energy of indignation, “you grieve and offend me. Do you think I should waste my time and yours by concocting fictions on a bench in Leicester Square?”

===Chinese===
“我谢谢您，”他终于说，“谢谢您这个最有趣的故事；于我之所以有趣，因为我完全确信它字字属实。”

“先生，”女士说，语气里带着几分愤慨，“您叫我伤心，也叫我不快。您以为，我会坐在莱斯特广场的长椅上，编些小说来糟蹋我的时间、也糟蹋您的时间么？”

===Original===
“Pardon me, Miss Lally, you have a little misunderstood me. Before you began I knew that whatever you told would be told in good faith, but your experiences have a far higher value than that of bona fides. The most extraordinary circumstances in your account are in perfect harmony with the very latest scientific theories. Professor Lodge would, I am sure, value a communication from you extremely; I was charmed from the first by his daring hypothesis in explanation of the wonders of Spiritualism (so called), but your narrative puts the whole matter out of the range of mere hypothesis.”

===Chinese===
“请原谅，拉利小姐，您稍稍误会了我。您开口之前，我已知道您所述说的一切必出于至诚；可您的经历，其价值远不止于诚意（bona fides）二字。您叙述中最不寻常的种种情形，同最晚近的科学理论完美合辙。我敢说，洛奇教授（Professor Lodge）定会把您的来信视若至宝；他用以解释（所谓的）招魂术诸奇的那个大胆假设，我从一开始便为之倾倒；而您的记叙，更使全案超出了单纯假设的范围。”

===Original===
“Alas, sir, all this will not help me. You forget, I have lost my brother under the most startling and dreadful circumstances. Again, I ask you, did you not see him as you came here? His black whiskers, his spectacles, his timid glance to right and left; think, do not these particulars recall his face to your memory?”

“I am sorry to say I have never seen anyone of the kind,” said Phillipps, who had forgotten all about the missing brother. “But let me ask you a few questions. Did you notice whether Professor Gregg﻿—”

“Pardon me, sir, I have stayed too long. My employers will be expecting me. I thank you for your sympathy. Goodbye.”

===Chinese===
“唉，先生，这些于我全都无用。您忘了，我是在何等骇人、何等可怕的境况之下失去我的兄弟的。我再问您一遍：您来这里的路上，当真没有见到他么？他的黑髭鬓，他的眼镜，他左右怯生生的一瞥；想想，难道这些细节都不能唤回您记忆中他的脸么？”

“很遗憾，我从没见过这样的人，”菲利普斯说——那失踪的兄弟，他早忘得一干二净了。“不过请容我问您几个问题。您可曾注意到，格雷格教授——”

“请恕我，先生，我耽搁得太久了。我的东家该等着急了。多谢您的同情。再见。”

===Original===
Before Mr. Phillipps had recovered from his amazement at this abrupt departure, Miss Lally had disappeared from his gaze, passing into the crowd that now thronged the approaches to the Empire. He walked home in a pensive frame of mind, and drank too much tea. At ten o’clock he had made his third brew, and had sketched out the outlines of a little work to be called *Protoplasmic Reversion*.

===Chinese===
菲利普斯先生还没从这突如其来的告辞里回过神来，拉利小姐已从他的视野里消失了，没进此刻涌向帝国剧院（the Empire）入口处的人潮里。他一路心事重重地走回家，茶喝得太多了。十点钟，他冲下第三壶，并且拟出了一部小书的提纲，书名就定作《原生质逆转》（*Protoplasmic Reversion*）。

## Incident of the Private Bar / 雅座酒吧事件

===Original===
Mr. Dyson often meditated at odd moments over the singular tale he had listened to at the Café de la Touraine. In the first place he cherished a profound conviction that the words of truth were scattered with a too niggardly and sparing hand over the agreeable history of Mr. Smith and the Black Gulf Canyon; and, secondly, there was the undeniable fact of the profound agitation of the narrator, and his gestures on the pavement, too violent to be simulated. The idea of a man going about London haunted by the fear of meeting a young man with spectacles struck Dyson as supremely ridiculous; he searched his memory for some precedent in romance, but without success; he paid visits at odd times to the little café, hoping to find Mr. Wilkins there; and he kept a sharp watch on the great generation of the spectacled men without much doubt that he would remember the face of the individual whom he had seen dart out of the Aerated Bread Shop. All his peregrinations and researches, however, seemed to lead to nothing of value, and Dyson needed all his warm conviction of his innate detective powers and his strong scent for mystery to sustain him in his endeavors. In fact, he had two affairs on hand; and every day, as he passed through streets crowded or deserted, and lurked in the obscure districts, and watched at corners, he was more than surprised to find that the affair of the gold coin persistently avoided him; while the ingenious Wilkins, and the young man with spectacles whom he dreaded, seemed to have vanished from the pavements.

===Chinese===
戴森时常在偷闲的片刻里，细细回味他在都兰咖啡馆（Café de la Touraine）里听来的那段离奇故事。其一，他怀着一种深深的定见：在那段关于史密斯先生与黑渊峡的动听历险里，真话撒得未免太吝啬、太俭省；其二，叙述者那阵深巨的激动，连同他在人行道上那副激烈得绝非做作得出的手势，又是无可否认的事实。一个人在伦敦四处行走，心头却萦绕着对一个戴眼镜的年轻人的惧怕——这个念头在戴森看来简直荒唐透顶；他搜遍记忆，想从传奇小说里寻出一个先例，却一无所获；他不时到那家小咖啡馆去走走，指望在那里撞见威尔金斯先生；他还对满城那一支蔚为大观的戴眼镜族类严加监视，并不怎么怀疑自己认得出那个曾从他眼前飞奔出充气面包店的家伙的面孔。然而他这许多踏勘与查访，似乎全然引不出一点有价值的东西；戴森只得靠他那份对天生侦探才干的火热自信、靠他那副嗅探神秘的浓烈嗅觉，来支撑这番营生。事实上，他手头原有两桩案子；日复一日，他穿行于人稠或人稀的街巷，潜身于晦暗的地段，守候在街角，却愕然发现那桩金币案始终避他而行；至于乖巧的威尔金斯，和他惧怕的那个戴眼镜的年轻人，竟仿佛从人行道上凭空消失了。

===Original===
He was pondering these problems one evening in a house of call in the Strand, and the obstinacy with which the persons he so ardently desired to meet hung back gave the modest tankard before him an additional touch of bitter. As it happened, he was alone in his compartment, and, without thinking, he uttered aloud the burden of his meditations. “How bizarre it all is!” he said, “a man walking the pavement with the dread of a timid-looking young man with spectacles continually hovering before his eyes. And there was some tremendous feeling at work, I could swear to that.” Quick as thought, before he had finished the sentence, a head popped round the barrier, and was withdrawn again; and while Dyson was wondering what this could mean, the door of the compartment was swung open, and a smooth, clean-shaven, and smiling gentleman entered.

===Chinese===
一天傍晚，他正在斯特兰德街上一家熟识的酒馆里思量这些问题；那些他如此热切盼着一会的人物偏又执拗地避而不出，倒给他面前那一大杯素朴的麦酒平添了一丝苦味。事有凑巧，他那间隔间里只有他一人，他不假思索，便把沉思的腹稿念叨出了声。“这一切多么离奇！”他说，“一个人在人行道上走着，一个怯生生、戴眼镜的年轻人的影子却不住悬在他眼前。况且那底下有一种惊涛骇浪般的情感在翻涌，这个我可以起誓。”话音未落，一颗脑袋从隔板后头探出来，随即又缩了回去；戴森正自纳罕这是什么意思，隔间的门已被推开，一位面皮光洁、唇上刮得干干净净、满面笑容的绅士走了进来。

===Original===
“You will excuse me, sir,” he said politely, “for intruding on your thoughts, but you made a remark a minute ago.”

“I did,” said Dyson; “I have been puzzling over a foolish matter, and I thought aloud. As you heard what I said, and seem interested, perhaps you may be able to relieve my perplexity?”

===Chinese===
“请恕我冒昧，先生，”他客气地说，“打断了您的思绪；不过方才您说了一句话。”

“我是说了，”戴森道，“我正为一桩无聊事犯糊涂，便自言自语出了声。您既然听见了我的话，似乎又颇有兴致，或许您能解我的困惑？”

===Original===
“Indeed. I scarcely know; it is an odd coincidence. One has to be cautious. I suppose, sir, that you would have no repulsion in assisting the ends of justice.”

“Justice,” replied Dyson, “is a term of such wide meaning, that I too feel doubtful about giving an answer. But this place is not altogether fit for such a discussion; perhaps you would come to my rooms?”
