我们一同走去那家铺子。老塞斯认得哈伯登医生，有问必答。

“这药，您照我的方子送到莱斯特先生那里，我想有些星期了，”医生说着，递给老人一张铅笔写的纸片。

===Original===
The chemist put on his great spectacles with trembling uncertainty, and held up the paper with a shaking hand.

“Oh, yes,” he said, “I have very little of it left; it is rather an uncommon drug, and I have had it in stock some time. I must get in some more, if Mr. Leicester goes on with it.”

===Chinese===
药剂师颤颤巍巍、犹疑不定地戴上他那副大眼镜，抖着一只手举起纸片。

“哦，是的，”他说，“剩下的不多了；这药相当少见，我存货也有些日子了。要是莱斯特先生继续用，我就得再进些。”

===Original===
“Kindly let me have a look at the stuff,” said Haberden; and the chemist gave him a glass bottle. He took out the stopper and smelt the contents, and looked strangely at the old man.

“Where did you get this?” he said, “and what is it? For one thing, Mr. Sayce, it is not what I prescribed. Yes, yes, I see the label is right enough, but I tell you this is not the drug.”

===Chinese===
“请让我看看那东西，”哈伯登说；药剂师递给他一只玻璃瓶。他拔出瓶塞，嗅了嗅里面的东西，古怪地看了老人一眼。

“您这是从哪儿弄来的？”他说，“这又是什么？有一点，塞斯先生：这不是我开的药。是的，是的，我看标签没错；可我告诉您，这不是那味药。”

===Original===
“I have had it a long time,” said the old man, in feeble terror. “I got it from Burbage’s in the usual way. It is not prescribed often, and I have had it on the shelf for some years. You see there is very little left.”

“You had better give it to me,” said Haberden. “I am afraid something wrong has happened.”

We went out of the shop in silence, the doctor carrying the bottle neatly wrapped in paper under his arm.

===Chinese===
“我存了很久了，”老人说，声音里透着衰弱的惊恐，“是照常例从伯比奇（Burbage）那儿进的。这药不常有人开，我在架上搁了几年了。您瞧，剩得不多了。”

“您最好把它交给我，”哈伯登说，“只怕出了岔子。”

我们默默走出铺子，医生把那只纸裹得整整齐齐的瓶子挟在臂下。

===Original===
“Dr. Haberden,” I said when we had walked a little way﻿—“Dr. Haberden.”

“Yes,” he said, looking at me gloomily enough.

“I should like you to tell me what my brother has been taking twice a day for the last month or so.”

“Frankly, Miss Leicester, I don’t know. We will speak of this when we get to my house.”

We walked on quickly without another word till we reached Dr. Haberden’s. He asked me to sit down, and began pacing up and down the room, his face clouded over, as I could see, with no common fears.

===Chinese===
“哈伯登医生，”走出一段路后我开口——“哈伯登医生。”

“嗯，”他说，望着我的神色阴沉已极。

“我想请您告诉我，近一个月来，我哥哥一天两次服下的究竟是什么。”

“坦白说，莱斯特小姐，我不知道。到了我家再谈吧。”

我们一言不发，快步走到哈伯登医生家。他请我坐下，便在房里踱起步来；我看得出，他脸上笼着的绝非寻常的忧惧。

===Original===
“Well,” he said at length, “this is all very strange; it is only natural that you should feel alarmed, and I must confess that my mind is far from easy. We will put aside, if you please, what you told me last night and this morning, but the fact remains that for the last few weeks Mr. Leicester has been impregnating his system with a drug which is completely unknown to me. I tell you, it is not what I ordered; and what that stuff in the bottle really is remains to be seen.”

===Chinese===
“好吧，”他终于开口，“这一切太蹊跷了；您感到惊慌是人之常情，我也得承认，我心里远不踏实。您昨夜和今晨对我讲的那些，我们暂且搁下不提；可事实俱在：几个星期以来，莱斯特先生的身体里，一直在浸入一种我全然不识的药物。我告诉您，那不是我开的药；瓶里那东西究竟是什么，还有待查明。”

===Original===
He undid the wrapper, and cautiously tilted a few grains of the white powder on to a piece of paper, and peered curiously at it.

“Yes,” he said, “it is like the sulphate of quinine, as you say; it is flaky. But smell it.”

===Chinese===
他解开纸包，小心翼翼地倾出几粒白色粉末在一张纸上，好奇地端详。

“不错，”他说，“正如您所说，它像硫酸奎宁；是鳞片状的。可是您闻闻。”

===Original===
He held the bottle to me, and I bent over it. It was a strange sickly smell, vaporous and overpowering, like some strong anaesthetic.

“I shall have it analyzed,” said Haberden. “I have a friend who has devoted his whole life to chemistry as a science. Then we shall have something to go upon. No, no, say no more about that other matter; I cannot listen to that, and take my advice and think no more about it yourself.”

===Chinese===
他把瓶子递到我面前，我俯身去嗅。那是一种古怪的、令人作呕的气味，蒸腾而窒人，像某种烈性的麻醉剂。

“我要找人化验，”哈伯登说，“我有一位朋友，把毕生献给了化学这门科学。到那时我们才有凭据。不，不，另一桩事别再提了；我听不下去；听我一句劝，您自己也别再想它。”

===Original===
That evening my brother did not go out as usual after dinner.

“I have had my fling,” he said with a queer laugh; “and I must go back to my old ways. A little law will be quite a relaxation after so sharp a dose of pleasure,” and he grinned to himself, and soon after went up to his room. His hand was still all bandaged.

===Chinese===
那天晚上，哥哥没有照常在饭后出门。

“我放荡也放荡够了，”他发出一声怪笑，“该回到老路上去了。这么一剂猛烈的快乐服过之后，读点法律倒正是一种松弛。”他自顾自咧嘴一笑，不多时便上楼回房去了。他的手依旧缠着绷带。

===Original===
Dr. Haberden called a few days later.

“I have no special news to give you,” he said. “Chambers is out of town, so I know no more about that stuff than you do. But I should like to see Mr. Leicester if he is in.”

“He is in his room,” I said; “I will tell him you are here.”

“No, no, I will go up to him; we will have a little quiet talk together. I dare say that we have made a good deal of fuss about very little; for, after all, whatever the white powder may be, it seems to have done him good.”

===Chinese===
几天后，哈伯登医生来访。

“我没有什么特别的消息奉告，”他说，“钱伯斯博士（Dr. Chambers）出城了，那东西我也知道得不比您多。不过，要是莱斯特先生在家，我想见见他。”

“他在自己房里，”我说，“我去告诉他您来了。”

“不必，不必，我自己上去；我们要好好静静谈一谈。我敢说，我们是无事忙了一场；毕竟，不管那白粉是什么，它似乎倒治好了他。”

===Original===
The doctor went upstairs, and standing in the hall I heard his knock, and the opening and shutting of the door; and then I waited in the silent house for an hour, and the stillness grew more and more intense as the hands of the clock crept round. Then there sounded from above the noise of a door shut sharply, and the doctor was coming down the stairs. His footsteps crossed the hall, and there was a pause at the door. I drew a long sick breath with difficulty, and saw my face white in a little mirror, and he came in and stood at the door. There was an unutterable horror shining in his eyes; he steadied himself by holding the back of a chair with one hand, and his lower lip trembled like a horse’s, and he gulped and stammered unintelligible sounds before he spoke.

===Chinese===
医生上了楼；我立在门厅里，听见他的叩门声，听见门开门关；随后我在这幢寂静的房子里等了一个钟头，钟针缓缓爬行，那死寂也愈凝愈重。随后，楼上传来一扇门砰然关死的声响，医生下楼来了。他的脚步穿过门厅，在门口停了一停。我艰难地抽了一口病弱的长气，从一面小镜子里看见自己惨白的脸；他走进来，立在门边。一种难以言传的恐怖在他眼中灼灼发亮；他一只手抓住椅背撑住自己，下唇像马唇一样颤动，开口之前，喉头滚动作声，嗫嚅出不成字句的声响。

===Original===
“I have seen that man,” he began in a dry whisper. “I have been sitting in his presence for the last hour. My God! and I am alive and in my senses! I, who have dealt with death all my life, and have dabbled with the melting ruins of the earthly tabernacle. But not this! Oh, not this,” and he covered his face with his hands as if to shut out the sight of something before him.

===Chinese===
“我见过那个人了，”他用干哑的耳语开了口。“这一个钟头，我一直坐在他面前。我的上帝！而我居然还活着，神志还清醒！我这个一辈子与死亡打交道、亲手摆弄过尘世皮囊的熔解残骸的人。可从没有见过这个！哦，从没有这个，”他双手掩面，仿佛要挡住眼前什么东西的景象。

===Original===
“Do not send for me again, Miss Leicester,” he said with more composure. “I can do nothing in this house. Goodbye.”

As I watched him totter down the steps and along the pavement towards his house, it seemed to me that he had aged by ten years since the morning.

===Chinese===
“不要再打发人找我了，莱斯特小姐，”他镇定了些，说道，“在这幢房子里，我无能为力。再会。”

我望着他蹒跚下阶、沿人行道向自己家走去，只觉得从早晨到现在，他苍老了十岁。

===Original===
My brother remained in his room. He called out to me in a voice I hardly recognized, that he was very busy, and would like his meals brought to his door and left there, and I gave the order to the servants. From that day it seemed as if the arbitrary conception we call time had been annihilated for me. I lived in an ever present sense of horror, going through the routine of the house mechanically, and only speaking a few necessary words to the servants. Now and then I went out and paced the streets for an hour or two and came home again; but whether I were without or within, my spirit delayed before the closed door of the upper room, and, shuddering, waited for it to open. I have said that I scarcely reckoned time, but I suppose it must have been a fortnight after Dr. Haberden’s visit that I came home from my stroll a little refreshed and lightened. The air was sweet and pleasant, and the hazy form of green leaves, floating cloud-like in the square, and the smell of blossoms, had charmed my senses, and I felt happier and walked more briskly. As I delayed a moment at the verge of the pavement, waiting for a van to pass by before crossing over to the house, I happened to look up at the windows, and instantly there was the rush and swirl of deep cold waters in my ears, and my heart leapt up, and fell down, down as into a deep hollow, and I was amazed with a dread and terror without form or shape. I stretched out a hand blindly through folds of thick darkness, from the black and shadowy valley, and held myself from falling, while the stones beneath my feet rocked and swayed and tilted, and the sense of solid things seemed to sink away from under me. I had glanced up at the window of my brother’s study, and at that moment the blind was drawn aside, and something that had life stared out into the world. Nay, I cannot say I saw a face or any human likeness; a living thing, two eyes of burning flame glared at me, and they were in the midst of something as formless as my fear, the symbol and presence of all evil and all hideous corruption. I stood shuddering and quaking as with the grip of ague, sick with unspeakable agonies of fear and loathing, and for five minutes I could not summon force or motion to my limbs. When I was within the door, I ran up the stairs to my brother’s room, and knocked.

===Chinese===
哥哥守在他的房里。他用一种我几乎认不出的嗓音朝我喊，说他很忙，想让人把饭送到门口放下；我便这样吩咐了仆人。从那一天起，那被我们称作时间的专横概念，于我仿佛已被消灭。我活在一种无时不在的恐怖感里，机械地打点家中的例行事务，同仆人只说几句必要的话。我间或出门，在街上踱一两个钟头再回家来；可无论身在门外还是门内，我的魂灵都滞留在楼上那扇紧闭的房门前，战栗着，等它开启。我说过，我几乎不再计算时日；但想来必是哈伯登医生来过后约莫两星期，一天我散步回来，精神稍得舒展，心头稍得轻快。空气甘美宜人，绿叶朦朦的形影云一般浮在广场上空，花气袭人，迷醉了我的感官；我觉得快活了些，脚步也轻快了些。我在人行道边上略停了停，等一辆货车驶过再回家里；就在那时，我偶然抬头朝窗户望去——刹那间，耳中轰响起深冷水流的奔涌与漩卷，我的心猛地一跳，随即跌下去，跌下去，跌进一个深穴；我坠入了一种无形无状、无从名状的惊怖。我盲目地穿过一层层浓黑，从那黑沉沉、影幢幢的谷底伸出手去，撑住自己不倒；脚下的石路摇晃、倾侧、翻腾，坚实之物的感觉仿佛从我脚底沉陷下去。原来我抬眼望去的是哥哥书房的窗；就在那一瞬，窗帘被拉向一旁，一样有生命的东西，朝外面的世界瞪视出来。不，我不能说我看见了一张脸，或任何人的形貌；一个活物，两只燃烧的眼睛灼灼地瞪着我；它们嵌在一团与我的恐惧一般无形的东西中间，那是一切邪恶、一切可憎腐朽的象征与现身。我站在那里，抖得像被疟疾攫住，怀着无可言说的恐惧与憎恶的剧痛，五分钟里，竟唤不出一丝力气到四肢上。进了家门，我奔上楼去，敲哥哥的房门。

===Original===
“Francis, Francis,” I cried, “for heaven’s sake answer me. What is the horrible thing in your room? Cast it out, Francis, cast it from you!”

I heard a noise as of feet shuffling slowly and awkwardly, and a choking, gurgling sound, as if someone was struggling to find utterance, and then the noise of a voice, broken and stifled, and words that I could scarcely understand.

“There is nothing here,” the voice said, “Pray do not disturb me. I am not very well today.”

===Chinese===
“弗朗西斯，弗朗西斯，”我喊道，“看在老天爷份上，答应我。你房里那可怕的东西是什么？把它赶出去，弗朗西斯，把它从你身上赶出去！”

我听见一阵缓慢而笨拙的拖步声，一种哽咽咕噜的声响，仿佛有谁挣扎着想出声；随后是一个嗓音，破碎而窒塞，吐出几句我几乎听不懂的话。

“这里什么也没有，”那嗓音说，“请勿搅扰我。我今天不大舒服。”

===Original===
I turned away, horrified and yet helpless. I could do nothing, and I wondered why Francis had lied to me, for I had seen the appearance beyond the glass too plainly to be deceived, though it was but the sight of a moment. And I sat still, conscious that there had been something else, something I had seen in the first flash of terror before those burning eyes had looked at me. Suddenly I remembered; as I lifted my face the blind was being drawn back, and I had had an instant’s glance of the thing that was moving it, and in my recollection I knew that a hideous image was engraved forever on my brain. It was not a hand: there were no fingers that held the blind, but a black stump pushed it aside; the mouldering outline and the clumsy movement as of a beast’s paw had glowed into my senses before the darkling waves of terror had overwhelmed me as I went down quick into the pit. My mind was aghast at the thought of this, and of the awful presence that dwelt with my brother in his room; I went to his door and cried to him again, but no answer came. That night one of the servants came up to me and told me in a whisper that for three days food had been regularly placed at the door and left untouched; the maid had knocked, but had received no answer; she had heard the noise of shuffling feet that I had noticed. Day after day went by, and still my brother’s meals were brought to his door and left untouched; and though I knocked and called again and again, I could get no answer. The servants began to talk to me; it appeared they were as alarmed as I. The cook said that when my brother first shut himself up in his room, she used to hear him come out at night and go about the house; and once, she said, the hall door had opened and closed again, but for several nights she had heard no sound. The climax came at last. It was in the dusk of the evening, and I was sitting in the darkening dreary room when a terrible shriek jarred and rang harshly out of the silence, and I heard a frightened scurry of feet dashing down the stairs. I waited, and the servant maid staggered into the room and faced me, white and trembling.

===Chinese===
我转身走开，惊怖又无助。我无能为力；我不明白弗朗西斯为什么对我撒谎，因为玻璃后头的那张面影我看得再清楚不过，绝不会认错——尽管那不过一瞬。我呆坐着，意识到还有一样别的东西，一样在那双燃烧的眼睛瞪视我之前、在恐怖初闪的刹那被我看进眼里去的东西。蓦地我想起来了：我抬脸时，窗帘正被拉开，那拉动窗帘的东西，我瞥见了刹那的一眼；在记忆里我知道，一幅可憎的图景已永远刻上了我的脑髓。那不是一只手：握着窗帘的不是手指，而是一截黑色的残端把它拨开；那朽坏崩解的轮廓，那野兽脚爪般笨拙的动作，早在恐怖的暗潮将我吞没、把我活生生卷下深坑之前，已灼灼烙进了我的感官。想到这个，想到与我哥哥同住一室的那可怕的存在，我的心魂战栗；我走到他门前，又向他呼喊，却无人应答。当夜，一个仆人上楼来，低声告诉我：三天来，饭食照常放在门口，原封未动；女仆敲过门，无人应；她听见过我注意过的那种拖步声。日复一日过去，哥哥的饭食依旧送到门口，依旧原封不动；我敲了又敲、喊了又喊，始终讨不到一句回应。仆人们开始来同我絮叨；原来他们同我一样惊惶。厨娘说，哥哥起初闭门不出时，她夜里常听见他出来，在屋里走动；有一回，她说，临街的大门开了一下又关上；可近几夜，什么声息也没有了。终于，那顶点的一刻来了。那是一个傍晚，天色向暝，我正坐在渐渐暗下来的凄清屋子里，一声可怕的尖叫猛然刺破寂静，刺耳地回荡；我又听见一阵惊惶的脚步声冲下楼来。我等着；那女仆跌跌撞撞冲进屋来，面对着我，面无人色，浑身发抖。

===Original===
“O Miss Helen,” she whispered. “Oh, for the Lord’s sake, Miss Helen, what has happened? Look at my hand, miss; look at that hand!” I drew her to the window, and saw there was a black wet stain upon her hand.

“I do not understand you,” I said. “Will you explain to me?”

===Chinese===
“哦，海伦小姐，”她悄声道，“哦，看在主的份上，海伦小姐，出了什么事呀？瞧我的手，小姐；瞧这只手！”我把她拉到窗前，看见她手上有一块黑色的湿痕。

“我不明白你的意思，”我说，“你能讲清楚些么？”

===Original===
“I was doing your room just now,” she began. “I was turning down the bedclothes, and all of a sudden there was something fell upon my hand wet, and I looked up, and the ceiling was black and dripping on me.”

I looked hard at her, and bit my lip. “Come with me,” I said. “Bring your candle with you.”

===Chinese===
“我刚才在收拾您的房间，”她说，“我正掀开床罩，忽然有什么东西湿漉漉地落在我手上；我抬头一看，天花板黑了，正往我身上滴。”

我死死盯着她，咬住嘴唇。“跟我来，”我说，“把蜡烛带上。”

===Original===
The room I slept in was beneath my brother’s, and as I went in I felt I was trembling. I looked up at the ceiling, and saw a patch, all black and wet and a dew of black drops upon it, and a pool of horrible liquor soaking into the white bedclothes.

===Chinese===
我睡的房间就在哥哥房下；走进去时，我觉出自己在发抖。我抬头望天花板，看见一块斑，乌黑、湿濡，凝着一层黑露珠似的液滴；一摊骇人的汁水，正渗进白色的床褥里。

===Original===
I ran upstairs and knocked loudly.

“O Francis, Francis, my dear brother,” I cried, “what has happened to you?”

And I listened. There was a sound of choking, and a noise like water bubbling and regurgitating, but nothing else, and I called louder, but no answer came.

===Chinese===
我奔上楼去，大声敲门。

“哦，弗朗西斯，弗朗西斯，我亲爱的哥哥，”我喊道，“你出了什么事呀？”

我侧耳去听。里面有哽咽之声，有水泡般汩汩翻涌的声响，再无其他；我喊得更响，仍无人应。

===Original===
In spite of what Dr. Haberden had said, I went to him, and with tears streaming down my cheeks, I told him of all that had happened, and he listened to me with a face set hard and grim.

“For your father’s sake,” he said at last, “I will go with you, though I can do nothing.”

===Chinese===
不顾哈伯登医生说过的话，我还是去找了他；我泪流满面，把发生的一切告诉了他；他听着，脸色冷硬如铁。

“看在令尊的份上，”他终于说，“我随您走一趟，尽管我无能为力。”

===Original===
We went out together; the streets were dark and silent, and heavy with heat and a drought of many weeks. I saw the doctor’s face white under the gas-lamps, and when we reached the house his hand was shaking. We did not hesitate, but went upstairs directly. I held the lamp, and he called out in a loud, determined voice:﻿—

===Chinese===
我们一同出门；街道又黑又静，闷着数周干旱的燥热。煤气灯下，我看见医生脸色惨白；到得家门，他的手在抖。我们没有迟疑，径直上楼。我举着灯，他朗声唤道，语气斩钉截铁：——

===Original===
“Mr. Leicester, do you hear me? I insist on seeing you. Answer me at once.”

There was no answer, but we both heard that choking noise I have mentioned.

“Mr. Leicester, I am waiting for you. Open the door this instant, or I shall break it down.” And he called a third time in a voice that rang and echoed from the walls.

“Mr. Leicester! For the last time I order you to open the door.”

“Ah!” he said, after a pause of heavy silence, “we are wasting time here. Will you be so kind as to get me a poker, or something of the kind?”

===Chinese===
“莱斯特先生，您听见我了吗？我坚持要见您。立刻回答我。”

没有回答；可我们两人都听见了我说过的那种哽咽声。

“莱斯特先生，我在等您。即刻开门，否则我就把门砸开。”他又第三次高喊，嗓音撞在墙上，嗡然回响。

“莱斯特先生！我最后一次命令您开门。”

“啊！”在一阵沉重的静默之后，他说，“我们在这里白耗工夫了。可否劳驾给我找一根拨火棍，或这一类的东西？”

===Original===
I ran into a little room at the back where odd articles were kept, and found a heavy adze-like tool that I thought might serve the doctor’s purpose.

“Very good,” he said, “that will do, I dare say. I give you notice, Mr. Leicester,” he cried loudly at the keyhole, “that I am now about to break into your room.”

===Chinese===
我跑进后头一间堆放杂物的小屋，寻出一件锛子似的重家伙，心想或许合医生的用。

“很好，”他说，“我看这个行。莱斯特先生，我知会您，”他冲着锁孔高声喊道，“我现在要破门而入了。”

===Original===
Then I heard the wrench of the adze, and the woodwork split and cracked under it, and with a loud crash the door suddenly burst open; and for a moment we started back aghast at a fearful screaming cry, no human voice, but as the roar of a monster, that burst forth inarticulate and struck at us out of the darkness.

===Chinese===
接着我听见锛子撬绞的声响，木作在它下面迸裂、爆响；轰隆一声，房门豁然洞开；刹那间，一声凄厉的尖叫迫得我们倒退几步，骇然失色——那不是人的嗓音，倒像巨兽的咆哮，不成言语地迸发出来，从黑暗里朝我们劈头扑到。

===Original===
“Hold the lamp,” said the doctor, and we went in and glanced quickly round the room. “There it is,” said Dr. Haberden, drawing a quick breath; “look, in that corner.”

I looked, and a pang of horror seized my heart as with a white-hot iron. There upon the floor was a dark and putrid mass, seething with corruption and hideous rottenness, neither liquid nor solid, but melting and changing before our eyes, and bubbling with unctuous oily bubbles like boiling pitch. And out of the midst of it shone two burning points like eyes, and I saw a writhing and stirring as of limbs, and something moved and lifted up that might have been an arm. The doctor took a step forward, and raised the iron bar and struck at the burning points, and drove in the weapon, and struck again and again in a fury of loathing. At last the thing was quiet.

===Chinese===
“举好灯，”医生说；我们走进屋去，疾速环视一圈。“在那儿，”哈伯登医生急促地抽了口气，“看，那个角落。”

我望去，一阵恐怖像白热的烙铁般攫住了我的心。地板上伏着一团乌黑腐臭的东西，翻腾着溃烂与可憎的朽坏，非液非固，在我们眼前熔变流形，鼓着油亮的泡，像沸腾的沥青。那东西正中灼灼亮着两点火，宛如双眼；我又看见四肢似的蠕动与挣扎，一样什么东西抬起又落下，或许是条手臂。医生抢上一步，举起铁器，朝那两点燃烧的火击下，将武器捣进去，又在憎恶的狂怒里一击、再击。终于，那东西安静了。

---

===Original===
A week or two later, when I had to some extent recovered from the terrible shock, Dr. Haberden came to see me.

“I have sold my practice,” he began, “and tomorrow I am sailing on a long voyage. I do not know whether I shall ever return to England; in all probability I shall buy a little land in California, and settle there for the remainder of my life. I have brought you this packet, which you may open and read when you feel able to do so. It contains the report of Dr. Chambers on what I submitted to him. Goodbye, Miss Leicester, goodbye.”

===Chinese===
一两星期后，我总算从那可怕的震荡里缓过来几分，哈伯登医生来看我。

“我把诊所盘出去了，”他开口道，“明天我就登上一趟远航。我不知道此生还会不会回英国；十有八九，我会在加利福尼亚买一小块地，在那里了却余生。我给您带来这个小包，等您觉得自己受得了的时候再拆读。里面是钱伯斯博士对我送去之物的鉴定报告。再会，莱斯特小姐，再会。”

===Original===
When he was gone, I opened the envelope; I could not wait, and proceeded to read the papers within. Here is the manuscript; and if you will allow me, I will read you the astounding story it contains.

===Chinese===
他走后，我拆开了信封；我等不及了，随即读起里面的文件。原稿就在这里；诸位若容许，我便把它所载的那惊人故事读给诸位听。

===Original===
> “My dear Haberden,” the letter began, “I have delayed inexcusably in answering your questions as to the white substance you sent me. To tell you the truth, I have hesitated for some time as to what course I should adopt, for there is a bigotry and an orthodox standard in physical science as in theology, and I knew that if I told you the truth I should offend rooted prejudices which I once held dear myself. However, I have determined to be plain with you, and first I must enter into a short personal explanation.
> “You have known me, Haberden, for many years as a scientific man; you and I have often talked of our profession together, and discussed the hopeless gulf that opens before the feet of those who think to attain to truth by any means whatsoever, except the beaten way of experiment and observation, in the sphere of material things. I remember the scorn with which you have spoken to me of men of science who have dabbled a little in the unseen, and have timidly hinted that perhaps the senses are not, after all, the eternal, impenetrable bounds of all knowledge, the everlasting walls beyond which no human being has ever passed. We have laughed together heartily, and I think justly, at the ‘occult’ follies of the day, disguised under various names﻿—the mesmerisms, spiritualisms, materializations, theosophies, all the rabble rant of imposture, with their machinery of poor tricks and feeble conjuring, the true back-parlor magic of shabby London streets. Yet, in spite of what I have said, I must confess to you that I am no materialist, taking the word of course in its usual signification. It is now many years since I have convinced myself, convinced myself a sceptic remember, that the old iron-bound theory is utterly and entirely false. Perhaps this confession will not wound you so sharply as it would have done twenty years ago; for I think you cannot have failed to notice that for some time hypotheses have been advanced by men of pure science which are nothing less than transcendental, and I suspect that most modern chemists and biologists of repute would not hesitate to subscribe the dictum of the old Schoolman, *Omnia exeunt in mysterium*, which means, I take it, that every branch of human knowledge if traced up to its source and final principles vanishes into mystery. I need not trouble you now with a detailed account of the painful steps which led me to my conclusions; a few simple experiments suggested a doubt as to my then standpoint, and a train of thought that rose from circumstances comparatively trifling brought me far. My old conception of the universe has been swept away, and I stand in a world that seems as strange and awful to me as the endless waves of the ocean seen for the first time, shining, from a Peak in Darien. Now I know that the walls of sense that seemed so impenetrable, that seemed to loom up above the heavens and to be founded below the depths, and to shut us in forevermore, are no such everlasting impassable barriers as we fancied, but thinnest and most airy veils that melt away before the seeker, and dissolve as the early mist of the morning about the brooks. I know that you never adopted the extreme materialistic position: you did not go about trying to prove a universal negative, for your logical sense withheld you from that crowning absurdity; yet I am sure that you will find all that I am saying strange and repellent to your habits of thought. Yet, Haberden, what I tell you is the truth, nay, to adopt our common language, the sole and scientific truth, verified by experience; and the universe is verily more splendid and more awful than we used to dream. The whole universe, my friend, is a tremendous sacrament; a mystic, ineffable force and energy, veiled by an outward form of matter; and man, and the sun and the other stars, and the flower of the grass, and the crystal in the test-tube, are each and every one as spiritual, as material, and subject to an inner working.”

===Chinese===
> “亲爱的哈伯登：”信是这样开头的，“关于您送来的那种白色物质，我迟迟未复，实在情无可原。实话对您说，有一阵子我犹豫着不知采取怎样的态度；因为自然科学也同神学一样，有其门户之见与正统标准；我知道，一旦把真相告诉您，我触犯的将是那些我自己也曾奉若至宝的积习成见。然而我已决意向您直言不讳；首先，我得做一段简短的自陈。
> “哈伯登，多年以来您知道我是一个科学界的人；你我常常一同谈论我们的本行，讨论在物质世界的领域里，凡想凭实验与观察这条熟路之外的任何途径达到真理的人，脚下会张开怎样一道无望的深渊。我记得您曾以怎样的轻蔑对我谈起那些科学家——他们对不可见之域略尝辄止，又怯生生地暗示：感官或许终究不是一切知识的永恒而不可穿透的边界，不是人类从来不曾有谁逾越过的万古之墙。我们也曾一同开怀大笑——我想也笑得有理——笑当今种种假各种名目招摇的‘神秘学’愚蠢：什么催眠术、招魂术、显形术、通神学，这一大堆招摇撞骗的群氓鼓噪，连同它们那套蹩脚戏法与拙劣魔术的机关——伦敦寒酸街巷里货真价实的后客厅戏法。然而，尽管我说了这些，我必须向您承认：我并不是一个唯物主义者——当然，这词取它通常的意义。许多年前我就已确信——请您记住，我是以一个怀疑论者的身份确信的——那套铁箍紧锁的旧理论，彻头彻尾是假的。也许这番自白，不至于像二十年前那样刺伤您；因为我想您不至于没有留意：一段时间以来，纯粹的科学家们提出的一些假说，简直可以说是超验的；我疑心当今大多数有声望的化学家与生物学家，都会毫不迟疑地赞同那位老经院哲学家的格言：‘万事皆归于奥秘’（*Omnia exeunt in mysterium*）——依我的理解，它的意思是：人类知识的每一个分支，若追溯到底、穷究到最终原理，无不消融于奥秘之中。此刻我不必用一纸细账来烦扰您，细数引我走到今日结论的那些痛苦台阶；几个简单的实验叫我对当时的立足点起了疑，一串发端于细枝末节的思路又把我带出了很远。我对宇宙的旧有概念已被一扫而空；我站在一个世界里，它于我如此陌生、如此可畏，就像初次从达里恩（Darien）的山巅，望见阳光下粼粼闪烁的无尽海浪。如今我知道，感官之墙看来那般坚不可穿，看来上耸于诸天、下奠于深渊，把我们永远圈禁在内，其实绝非我们想象的那般万古难越的屏障，而是最薄、最轻灵的帷幕，在求索者面前悄然消融，如清晨萦绕溪上的薄雾一般散去。我知道您从来不曾采取极端唯物论的立场：您不曾汲汲于证明一个全称的否定命题，您的逻辑感使您避开了那登峰造极的荒谬；然而我确信，我此刻说的一切，在您的思想习惯听来仍会陌生而扞格。可是哈伯登，我告诉您的是真相；不，借用我们共同的语言说，是唯一的、科学的真相，是经过经验验证的；而宇宙实在比我们旧日所梦想的更辉煌，也更可畏。整个宇宙，我的朋友，是一场庄严浩大的圣礼；一股神秘而不可言说的力与能，罩着一层物质的外形；而人，太阳与众星，草间的花，试管中的结晶，每一个都同样地属灵，同样地属物，同受一种内在运作的支配。”

===Original===
> “You will perhaps wonder, Haberden, whence all this tends; but I think a little thought will make it clear. You will understand that from such a standpoint the whole view of things is changed, and what we thought incredible and absurd may be possible enough. In short, we must look at legend and belief with other eyes, and be prepared to accept tales that had become mere fables. Indeed, this is no such great demand. After all, modern science will concede as much, in a hypocritical manner. You must not, it is true, believe in witchcraft, but you may credit hypnotism; ghosts are out of date, but there is a good deal to be said for the theory of telepathy. Give a superstition a Greek name, and believe in it, should almost be a proverb.
> “So much for my personal explanation. You sent me, Haberden, a phial, stoppered and sealed, containing a small quantity of a flaky white powder, obtained from a chemist who has been dispensing it to one of your patients. I am not surprised to hear that this powder refused to yield any results to your analysis. It is a substance which was known to a few many hundred years ago, but which I never expected to have submitted to me from the shop of a modern apothecary. There seems no reason to doubt the truth of the man’s tale; he no doubt got, as he says, the rather uncommon salt you prescribed from the wholesale chemist’s; and it has probably remained on his shelf for twenty years, or perhaps longer. Here what we call chance and coincidence begins to work; during all these years the salt in the bottle was exposed to certain recurring variations of temperature, variations probably ranging from 40° to 80°. And, as it happens, such changes, recurring year after year at irregular intervals, and with varying degrees of intensity and duration, have constituted a process, and a process so complicated and so delicate, that I question whether modern scientific apparatus directed with the utmost precision could produce the same result. The white powder you sent me is something very different from the drug you prescribed; it is the powder from which the wine of the Sabbath, the *Vinum Sabbati* was prepared. No doubt you have read of the Witches’ Sabbath, and have laughed at the tales which terrified our ancestors; the black cats, and the broomsticks, and dooms pronounced against some old woman’s cow. Since I have known the truth I have often reflected that it is on the whole a happy thing that such burlesque as this is believed, for it serves to conceal much that it is better should not be known generally. However, if you care to read the appendix to Payne Knight’s monograph, you will find that the true Sabbath was something very different, though the writer has very nicely refrained from printing all he knew. The secrets of the true Sabbath were the secrets of remote times surviving into the Middle Ages, secrets of an evil science which existed long before Aryan man entered Europe. Men and women, seduced from their homes on specious pretences, were met by beings well qualified to assume, as they did assume, the part of devils, and taken by their guides to some desolate and lonely place, known to the initiate by long tradition and unknown to all else. Perhaps it was a cave in some bare and windswept hill; perhaps some inmost recess of a great forest, and there the Sabbath was held. There, in the blackest hour of night, the *Vinum Sabbati* was prepared, and this evil graal was poured forth and offered to the neophytes, and they partook of an infernal sacrament; *sumentes calicem principis inferorum*, as an old author well expresses it. And suddenly, each one that had drunk found himself attended by a companion, a shape of glamour and unearthly allurement, beckoning him apart to share in joys more exquisite, more piercing than the thrill of any dream, to the consummation of the marriage of the Sabbath. It is hard to write of such things as these, and chiefly because that shape that allured with loveliness was no hallucination, but, awful as it is to express, the man himself. By the power of that Sabbath wine, a few grains of white powder thrown into a glass of water, the house of life was riven asunder, and the human trinity dissolved, and the worm which never dies, that which lies sleeping within us all, was made tangible and an external thing, and clothed with a garment of flesh. And then in the hour of midnight, the primal fall was repeated and represented, and the awful thing veiled in the mythos of the Tree in the Garden was done anew. Such was the *nuptiae Sabbati*.”

===Chinese===
> “哈伯登，您或许要问，这一切何所趋向；可我想，稍一思索便能明白。您会理解，从这样一个立足点看去，万物的整个图景都变了，我们从前认为不可思议、荒诞不经的，或许大有可能。简而言之，我们必须换一双眼睛去看传说与信仰，预备接受那些早已沦为寓言的故事。其实，这要求并不为过。毕竟，现代科学原也肯承认这一层，只是态度虚伪。诚然，巫术您是不可信的，催眠术却尽可信；鬼魂已经过时，心灵感应的学说却大有可说。‘给一种迷信起个希腊名字，然后信奉它’——这几乎该成为一句谚语了。
> “我的自陈，到此为止。哈伯登，您寄给我的是一只小药瓶，加塞封蜡，里面盛着少量鳞片状的白粉，取自一位一直在为您的病人配药的药剂师。听说这粉末不肯在您的分析面前显出任何结果，我并不惊讶。这是一种几百年前只有少数人知晓的物质，我却从不指望有朝一日会从一家现代药房的柜台上被送到我这里来。那人的说法，看来没有理由怀疑；毫无疑问，正如他所言，他照您开的方子，从批发药商那里购进了那种相当少见的盐；它在他架上，多半一搁就是二十年，或许更久。我们所谓机缘与巧合的东西，便在这里开始运作了：这些年来，瓶中的盐经受着某种周而复始的温度变化，变化幅度大约在 40° 到 80° 之间。而恰巧，这种年复一年、间隔不定、强度与久暂参差的变化，便构成了一道工序——一道如此繁复、如此精微的工序，我怀疑现代的科学仪器纵然以最高的精密度操纵，能否重演同样的结果。您寄给我的这白粉，与您开出的那味药，已是判然两物；它正是用以调制巫魔会之酒——即安息日酒（*Vinum Sabbati*）——的粉末。您想必读到过巫魔会（Witches’ Sabbath）的记载，也笑过那些吓坏我们祖先的传闻：什么黑猫啦，扫帚啦，什么咒死某位老太太的母牛的判词啦。自知道真相以来，我时常想：人们信的偏是这类滑稽戏，总体上倒是一件幸事；因为它遮掩了许多最好不为世人所共知的东西。不过，您若有兴致读一读佩恩·奈特（Payne Knight）那部专著的附录，便会发现真正的巫魔会与那截然不同——尽管那位作者极有分寸，把他知道的一切都按下未印。真正的巫魔会的秘密，是邃古时代残存到中世纪的秘密，是一门邪恶科学的秘密，其存在远在雅利安人进入欧洲之前。男女们被巧言令色诱出家门，便有生灵迎上来——他们委实有充分的资格扮演、也当真扮演了魔鬼的角色——再由引路人带往某处荒僻孤绝之地，那地方入会者凭悠远传统知之，此外无人知晓。那或许是某座童山濯濯、风声猎猎的山上的洞穴；或许是某片大森林最幽深的腹地；巫魔会便在那里举行。就在那里，在夜色最黑的时辰，安息日酒调制而成；这只邪恶的圣杯斟满倾出，敬给新入会的人们；他们领受了一场地狱的圣礼——如一位古时作家所言：‘他们接过地狱之君的杯盏’（*sumentes calicem principis inferorum*）。转瞬间，每一个饮过酒的人，都发觉身边伴着一个同伴，一个魅影般、带着非世间诱惑的身形，招呼他走开去，共享比任何梦境的震颤更精妙、更彻骨的欢愉，直到成就巫魔会的婚配。这样的事，很难落笔；尤其因为，那个以美好惑人的身形并非幻觉，而是——说出口便觉可怕——那男人自己。凭那巫魔会之酒的力量——几粒白粉，投入一杯清水——生命之屋被劈成两半，人的三位一体就此涣解；那条永不死灭的蛆虫，那蛰伏于我们众人之内的事物，被铸成了可触可见的外物，裹上一件肉身的衣裳。随后，在午夜时分，原初的堕落被重演、被搬演，那裹在伊甸园中树木的神话里的可怕之事，被重新做了一遍。这便是巫魔会的婚配（*nuptiae Sabbati*）。”

===Original===
> “I prefer to say no more; you, Haberden, know as well as I do that the most trivial laws of life are not to be broken with impunity; and for so terrible an act as this, in which the very inmost place of the temple was broken open and defiled, a terrible vengeance followed. What began with corruption ended also with corruption.”

===Chinese===
> “我宁愿不再多说；哈伯登，您同我一样清楚：生命最轻微的法则，也不容违背而不受惩罚；而这样一桩可怕的行为——圣殿最内中的至圣所被破门而入、遭玷污——随之而来的，便是可怕的复仇。始于腐朽者，亦终于腐朽。”

===Original===
Underneath is the following in Dr. Haberden’s writing:﻿—

===Chinese===
下面是哈伯登医生亲笔写的一段话：——

===Original===
> “The whole of the above is unfortunately strictly and entirely true. Your brother confessed all to me on that morning when I saw him in his room. My attention was first attracted to the bandaged hand, and I forced him to show it me. What I saw made me, a medical man of many years standing, grow sick with loathing; and the story I was forced to listen to was infinitely more frightful than I could have believed possible. It has tempted me to doubt the Eternal Goodness which can permit nature to offer such hideous possibilities; and if you had not with your own eyes seen the end, I should have said to you﻿—disbelieve it all. I have not, I think, many more weeks to live, but you are young, and may forget all this.
> “Joseph Haberden, M.D.”

===Chinese===
> “上文所述，不幸字字属实，绝无一分虚妄。您哥哥在那早晨、我在他房中见他时，已向我招认了一切。最先惹我注意的是那只缠着绷带的手，我强迫他拿给我看。我所见的景象，令我这个行医多年之人，也憎恶得几欲作呕；而那番我不得不听完的自述，其可怖又远出我所能相信的极限。它诱使我疑心那永恒之善——它竟容许自然拿出如此可憎的可能性；若非您亲眼见过结局，我本要对您说——这一切，都不要信。我想，我活不了几个星期了；而您还年轻，或许能把这一切忘却。
> “医学博士约瑟夫·哈伯登（Joseph Haberden, M.D.）”

===Original===
In the course of two or three months I heard that Dr. Haberden had died at sea, shortly after the ship left England.

Miss Leicester ceased speaking, and looked pathetically at Dyson, who could not refrain from exhibiting some symptoms of uneasiness.

===Chinese===
两三个月后，我听说哈伯登医生死在了海上——船离开英国后不久。

莱斯特小姐止住话头，凄楚地望着戴森；戴森则不由得露出几分不安的神色。

===Original===
He stuttered out some broken phrases expressive of his deep interest in her extraordinary history, and then said with a better grace﻿—

“But, pardon me, Miss Leicester, I understood you were in some difficulty. You were kind enough to ask me to assist you in some way.”

===Chinese===
他结结巴巴地说了几句不成片段的话，表示他对她这段离奇身世的深切关注，随后才较为得体地说——

“不过，恕我冒昧，莱斯特小姐，我记得您正身处某种危难。承您好意，要我略效绵薄。”

===Original===
“Ah,” she said, “I had forgotten that. My own present trouble seems of such little consequence in comparison with what I have told you. But as you are so good to me, I will go on. You will scarcely believe it, but I found that certain persons suspected, or rather pretended to suspect that I had murdered my brother. These persons were relatives of mine, and their motives were extremely sordid ones; but I actually found myself subject to the shameful indignity of being watched. Yes, sir, my steps were dogged when I went abroad, and at home I found myself exposed to constant if artful observation. With my high spirit this was more than I could brook, and I resolved to set my wits to work and elude the persons who were shadowing me. I was so fortunate as to succeed. I assumed this disguise, and for some time have lain snug and unsuspected. But of late I have reason to believe that the pursuer is on my track; unless I am greatly deceived, I saw yesterday the detective who is charged with the odious duty of observing my movements. You, sir, are watchful and keen-sighted; tell me, did you see anyone lurking about this evening?”

===Chinese===
“啊，”她说，“我倒把那桩事忘了。比起我方才讲给您听的，我自己眼下的麻烦实在微不足道。可您既待我这样好，我就接着说吧。您几乎不会相信：我发现某些人起了疑——或者不如说，装作疑心——是我害死了自己的哥哥。这些人是我的亲戚，动机卑鄙龌龊至极；而我竟当真受了盯梢这等无耻的屈辱。是的，先生，我一出门就有人尾随，在家里也时时处在窥伺之下——虽则那窥伺做得巧妙。以我的傲骨，这如何忍得下；我便决计动动脑筋，甩掉那些盯我的影子。我侥幸成功了。我改了这身装束，安然藏了一些时日，无人起疑。可近来我有理由相信，追捕的人又摸到了我的踪迹；除非我大错特错，昨天我已看见了那个奉命盯我行踪的探子——那差事多么可憎。您，先生，眼观六路、目光锐利；告诉我，今晚您可看见什么人在附近逡巡？”

===Original===
“I hardly think so,” said Dyson, “but perhaps you would give me some description of the detective in question.”

“Certainly; he is a youngish man, dark, with dark whiskers. He has adopted spectacles of large size in the hope of disguising himself effectually, but he cannot disguise his uneasy manner, and the quick, nervous glances he casts to right and left.”

===Chinese===
“我看没有，”戴森说，“不过，或许您可以给我描述描述那位探子的模样。”

“当然；他是个模样年轻的男人，黑头发，黑黑的连鬓胡子。他戴上一副大眼镜，指望把自己伪装得严实些；可他掩不住那副坐立不安的做派，掩不住那左顾右盼、神经质的敏捷目光。”

===Original===
This piece of description was the last straw for the unhappy Dyson, who was foaming with impatience to get out of the house, and would gladly have sworn eighteenth century oaths if propriety had not frowned on such a course.

===Chinese===
这一番描述，成了不幸的戴森所不堪承受的最后一根稻草：他心急如焚，恨不能立刻脱身出屋；若非体统不许，他真乐意赌一番十八世纪式的毒咒。

===Original===
“Excuse me, Miss Leicester,” he said with cold politeness, “I cannot assist you.”

“Ah!” she said sadly, “I have offended you in some way. Tell me what I have done, and I will ask you to forgive me.”

===Chinese===
“告辞了，莱斯特小姐，”他冷冷地客套道，“我帮不了您。”

“啊！”她哀然道，“我总有哪里得罪了您。告诉我我做错了什么，我会求您原谅。”

===Original===
“You are mistaken,” said Dyson, grabbing his hat, but speaking with some difficulty; “you have done nothing. But, as I say, I cannot help you. Perhaps,” he added, with some tinge of sarcasm, “my friend Russell might be of service.”

“Thank you,” she replied; “I will try him,” and the lady went off into a shriek of laughter, which filled up Mr. Dyson’s cup of scandal and confusion.

===Chinese===
“您误会了，”戴森说着，一把抓起帽子，话却说得颇为艰难，“您什么也没有做。可是，如我所说，我帮不了您。或许，”他添了一句，带着几分讥诮，“我那位朋友拉塞尔倒能效劳。”

“谢谢，”她答道，“我会去找他。”女士随即爆出一串尖利的狂笑，把戴森先生的惊骇与狼狈之杯，斟得满满当当。

===Original===
He left the house shortly afterwards, and had the peculiar delight of a five-mile walk, through streets which slowly changed from black to gray, and from gray to shining passages of glory for the sun to brighten. Here and there he met or overtook strayed revellers, but he reflected that no one could have spent the night in a more futile fashion than himself; and when he reached his home he had made resolves for reformation. He decided that he would abjure all Milesian and Arabian methods of entertainment, and subscribe to Mudie’s for a regular supply of mild and innocuous romance.

===Chinese===
不多时他便离开了那幢房子，独享徒步五英里的殊乐：一路长街，从黑慢慢转灰，又从灰转成太阳照亮的一道道璀璨的通衢。沿途他遇见或赶上几个迷途的夜游寻欢客，可他思量着：这一夜，谁也不曾把光阴打发得比他更徒劳无益了。到家时，他已痛下改过自新的决心。他决定，从此戒绝一切爱尔兰式（Milesian）与阿拉伯式的娱情之术，转而订阅穆迪流通图书馆（Mudie’s），按时取用温和无害的传奇故事。

## Strange Occurrence in Clerkenwell / 克勒肯维尔怪事

===Original===
Mr. Dyson had inhabited for some years a couple of rooms in a moderately quiet street in Bloomsbury, where, as he somewhat pompously expressed it, he held his finger on the pulse of life without being deafened with the thousand rumors of the main arteries of London. It was to him a source of peculiar, if esoteric gratification, that from the adjacent corner of Tottenham Court Road a hundred lines of omnibuses went to the four quarters of the town; he would dilate on the facilities for visiting Dalston, and dwell on the admirable line that knew extremest Ealing and the streets beyond Whitechapel. His rooms, which had been originally “furnished apartments,” he had gradually purged of their more peccant parts; and though one would not find here the glowing splendors of his old chambers in the street off the Strand, there was something of severe grace about the appointments which did credit to his taste. The rugs were old, and of the true faded beauty; the etchings, nearly all of them proofs printed by the artist, made a good show with broad white margins and black frames, and there was no spurious black oak. Indeed, there was but little furniture of any kind: a plain and honest table, square and sturdy, stood in one corner; a seventeenth century settle fronted the hearth; and two wooden elbow-chairs, and a bookshelf of the Empire made up the equipment, with an exception worthy of note. For Dyson cared for none of these things. His place was at his own bureau, a quaint old piece of lacquered-work at which he would sit for hour after hour, with his back to the room, engaged in the desperate pursuit of literature, or, as he termed his profession, the chase of the phrase. The neat array of pigeonholes and drawers teemed and overflowed with manuscript and notebooks, the experiments and efforts of many years; and the inner well, a vast and cavernous receptacle, was stuffed with accumulated ideas. Dyson was a craftsman who loved all the detail and the technique of his work intensely; and if, as has been hinted, he deluded himself a little with the name of artist, yet his amusements were eminently harmless, and, so far as can be ascertained, he (or the publishers) had chosen the good part of not tiring the world with printed matter.

===Chinese===
戴森先生几年来一直住在布鲁姆斯伯里一条相当安静的街上的两间屋里；用他那略嫌浮夸的说法，他在这条街上把手指按在生命的脉搏上，却不至被伦敦几条大动脉里的千百种喧嚣震聋了耳朵。托特纳姆宫路近旁的那个街角，有百路公共马车分赴全城四方——这是他一种不足为外人道、却又别有滋味的得意；他会滔滔不绝地夸说去达尔斯顿（Dalston）何等便当，会津津乐道那条可敬的线路：它认得最远处的伊灵（Ealing），也认得白教堂（Whitechapel）以远的街巷。这两间屋原先是“备有家具出租”的公寓，他已渐次涤除了其中为害最甚的部分；虽说在这里找不到他斯特兰德街旁旧居那种灼灼的华彩，陈设之间却自有一种严峻的雅致，足为他的品味张目。地毯是旧的，旧得恰到好处，是真正的褪残之美；壁上的蚀刻版画几乎全是艺术家亲手印制的样张，衬着宽白的画缘、乌黑的框子，颇为可观；屋里不见一件假冒的黑橡木。的确，家什本就寥寥：一张方正敦实的素朴桌子立在墙角，一张十七世纪的高背长椅正对壁炉，两把木扶手椅、一驾帝政式的书橱，便算凑齐了陈设——只是有一样例外，值得一提。因为这些物件，戴森一件也不放在心上。他的宝座在自己那张写字台前——一件古怪的旧髹漆家具；他在台前一坐便是钟点连着钟点，背向着房间，从事那殊死备至的文学追逐，或者照他对本行的尊称：字句的狩猎。那一列齐整的文件格与抽屉，塞得满满当当又漫溢而出，全是稿纸与笔记簿，是多年试验与努力的积存；台腹那口深井——一个洞窟般空阔的容身处——填满了层积的念头。戴森是个匠人，酷爱自己行当里的一切细节与技艺；倘若如前文所暗示，他以艺术家之名多少自欺了一点，那么他的消遣也全然无害；而且就迄今所能查考的而言，他（或者说是出版商们）拣了那上好的一份：不以印刷品劳乏世人。

===Original===
Here, then, Dyson would shut himself up with his fancies, experimenting with words, and striving, as his friend the recluse of Bayswater strove, with the almost invincible problem of style, but always with a fine confidence, extremely different from the chronic depression of the realist. He had been almost continuously at work on some scheme that struck him as well-nigh magical in its possibilities since the night of his adventure with the ingenious tenant of the first floor in Abingdon Grove; and as he laid down the pen with a glow of triumph, he reflected that he had not viewed the streets for five days in succession. With all the enthusiasm of his accomplished labor still working in his brain, he put away his papers, and went out, pacing the pavement at first in that rare mood of exultation which finds in every stone upon the way the possibilities of a masterpiece. It was growing late, and the autumn evening was drawing to a close amidst veils of haze and mist, and in the stilled air the voices, and the roaring traffic, and incessant feet seemed, to Dyson like the noise upon the stage when all the house is silent. In the square, the leaves rippled down as quick as summer rain, and the street beyond was beginning to flare with the lights in the butcher’s shops and the vivid illumination of the greengrocer. It was a Saturday night, and the swarming populations of the slums were turning out in force; the battered women in rusty black had begun to paw the lumps of cagmag, and others gloated over unwholesome cabbages, and there was a brisk demand for four-ale. Dyson passed through these night-fires with some relief; he loved to meditate, but his thoughts were not as De Quincey’s after his dose; he cared not two straws whether onions were dear or cheap, and would not have exulted if meat had fallen to twopence a pound. Absorbed in the wilderness of the tale he had been writing, weighing nicely the points of plot and construction, relishing the recollection of this and that happy phrase, and dreading failure here and there, he left the rush and the whistle of the gas-flares behind him, and began to touch upon pavements more deserted.

===Chinese===
就在这里，戴森把自己同自己的玄想关在一处，拿字词做试验，同他那位贝斯沃特的隐士朋友一样，与风格这个几乎不可攻克的难题搏斗；只是他始终怀着一份精良的自信，与现实派作家那种经年累月的消沉判然不同。自从阿宾登格罗夫二楼那位机巧房客的一夜奇遇之后，他几乎不曾间断地伏案于一个构想——那构想种种可能，在他看来近乎魔法；此刻他带着得胜的红光搁下笔，才发觉自己已接连五天不曾见过街道。大功告成的热忱犹在脑中奔涌，他收起稿纸出了门；起初走在人行道上，他正怀着那种难得的欢腾心境，觉得沿路每一块石头里都蕴藏着一部杰作的可能。天色渐晚，秋日的黄昏正在雾霭与轻纱般的迷蒙里收束；在寂静下去的空气中，人语、轰响的车马与不绝的脚步，在戴森听来，恰似满场肃静时舞台上的声响。广场上，落叶如夏日骤雨般簌簌而下；广场外的街上，肉铺的灯火与菜肆鲜明辉煌的照映，开始灼灼放光。那是星期六之夜，贫民窟里蜂拥的人丁正倾巢而出；衣衫破敝、一身锈黑的女人已开始翻弄一块块劣肉，另一些人对着病蔫蔫的卷心菜贪婪咂摸，四便士麦酒也买者踊跃。戴森穿过这一片片夜火，颇觉松了口气；他喜爱玄思冥想，可他的思路不同于德·昆西（De Quincey）服下那一剂之后的思路；洋葱是贵是贱，他毫不在意；纵然肉价跌到两便士一磅，他也不会额手称快。他一心沉在自己所写那篇故事的荒野里，细细掂量情节与结构的关窍，回味这一句那一句妙语的余甘，又为这里那里的失手暗自忧心；就这样，他把煤气灯焰的奔涌与呼啸抛在身后，脚下渐渐踏上更荒凉的人行道。

===Original===
He had turned, without taking note, to the northward, and was passing through an ancient fallen street, where now notices of floors and offices to let hung out, but still about it there was the grace and the stiffness of the Age of Wigs; a broad roadway, a broad pavement, and on each side a grave line of houses with long and narrow windows flush with the walls, all of mellowed brickwork. Dyson walked with quick steps, as he resolved that short work must be made of a certain episode; but he was in that happy humor of invention, and another chapter rose in the inner chamber of his brain, and he dwelt on the circumstances he was to write down with curious pleasure. It was charming to have the quiet streets to walk in, and in his thought he made a whole district the cabinet of his studies, and vowed he would come again. Heedless of his course, he struck off to the east again, and soon found himself involved in a squalid network of gray two-storied houses, and then in the waste void and elements of brickwork, the passages and unmade roads behind great factory walls, encumbered with the refuse of the neighborhood, forlorn, ill-lighted, and desperate. A brief turn, and there rose before him the unexpected, a hill suddenly lifted from the level ground, its steep ascent marked by the lighted lamps, and eager as an explorer Dyson found his way to the place, wondering where his crooked paths had brought him. Here all was again decorous, but hideous in the extreme. The builder, someone lost in the deep gloom of the early twenties, had conceived the idea of twin villas in gray brick, shaped in a manner to recall the outlines of the Parthenon, each with its classic form broadly marked with raised bands of stucco. The name of the street was all strange, and for a further surprise, the top of the hill was crowned with an irregular plot of grass and fading trees, called a square, and here again the Parthenon-motive had persisted. Beyond the streets were curious, wild in their irregularities, here a row of sordid, dingy dwellings, dirty and disreputable in appearance, and there, without warning, stood a house genteel and prim with wire blinds and brazen knocker, as clean and trim as if it had been the doctor’s house in some benighted little country town. These surprises and discoveries began to exhaust Dyson, and he hailed with delight the blazing windows of a public-house, and went in with the intention of testing the beverage provided for the dwellers in this region, as remote as Libya and Pamphylia and the parts about Mesopotamia. The babble of voices from within warned him that he was about to assist at the true parliament of the London workman, and he looked about him for that more retired entrance called private. When he had settled himself on an exiguous bench, and had ordered some beer, he began to listen to the jangling talk in the public bar beyond; it was a senseless argument, alternately furious and maudlin, with appeals to Bill and Tom, and medieval survivals of speech, words that Chaucer wrote belched out with zeal and relish, and the din of pots jerked down and coppers rapped smartly on the zinc counter made a thorough bass for it all. Dyson was calmly smoking his pipe between the sips of beer, when an indefinite looking figure slid rather than walked into the compartment. The man started violently when he saw Dyson placidly sitting in the corner, and glanced keenly about him. He seemed to be on wires, controlled by some electric machine, for he almost bolted out of the door when the barman asked with what he could serve him, and his hand shivered as he took the glass. Dyson inspected him with a little curiosity; he was muffled up almost to the lips, and a soft felt hat was drawn down over his eyes; he looked as if he shrank from every glance, and a more raucous voice suddenly uplifted in the public bar seemed to find in him a sympathy that made him shake and quiver like a jelly. It was pitiable to see anyone so thrilled with nervousness, and Dyson was about to address some trivial remark of casual inquiry to the man, when another person came into the compartment, and, laying a hand on his arm, muttered something in an undertone, and vanished as he came. But Dyson had recognized him as the smooth-tongued and smooth-shaven Burton, who had displayed so sumptuous a gift in lying; and yet he thought little of it, for his whole faculty of observation was absorbed in the lamentable and yet grotesque spectacle before him. At the first touch of the hand on his arm, the unfortunate man had wheeled round as if spun on a pivot, and shrank back with a low, piteous cry, as if some dumb beast were caught in the toils. The blood fled away from the wretch’s face, and the skin became gray as if a shadow of death had passed in the air and fallen on it, and Dyson caught a choking whisper﻿—

===Chinese===
他不知不觉折向北行，正走过一条古老而衰落的街：如今楼上悬着“楼层写字间招租”的告帖，但它周身仍留着假发时代那种端庄与板滞——路面开阔，人行道开阔，两侧是一色凝重的宅子，长而窄的窗子与墙面齐平，砖色尽是温润的旧陶之色。戴森快步走着，心里打定主意，某一处情节必得快刀斩断；可他正处在文思泉涌的好兴致里，脑之内室中又升起了一章，他怀着奇异的快感，预先咂摸着将要落笔的种种情事。清静的街道任他独自行走，何等惬意；在想象中，他把整整一个街区化作了供他研寻的书斋，还立誓要再来。他不辨路径，又折向东去，不久便陷进一片灰色两层楼房的卑陋罗网，继而陷进砖石土方的荒滩：大厂高墙背后的小径与未曾铺砌的土路，堆满左近倾倒的垃圾，荒凉，昏暗，绝望。一个急转弯，眼前陡然拔起意想不到的景象——平地上凭空升起一座小山，陡坡由点亮的街灯一盏盏标出；戴森像探险家一般兴奋，寻到那地方，纳罕自己这一路曲曲折折，究竟被引到了哪里。这里的一切重归端庄，却丑陋到了极点。营造这片房子的匠人——某个湮没在一八二〇年代初叶沉沉暗影里的人物——起了灰砖双子别墅的构想，形制略拟帕特农神庙的轮廓，古典的体貌用一条条凸起的灰泥宽带大笔勾出。街名透着全然的陌生；更出人意料的是，山顶竟顶着一方不规整的草地与几株凋败的树，号称广场，而帕特农的母题在这里依旧阴魂不散。再往外，街巷古怪，错落无章：这里是一排卑污黯淡的住屋，外观又脏又破；那里毫无征兆地立着一所体面拘谨的宅子，窗挂铁纱帘，门装黄铜环，干净齐整得仿佛穷乡僻壤小城里的医生宅邸。这接连的意外与发现开始叫戴森精疲力竭，他欢欣地望见一家酒馆灼亮的橱窗，便走了进去，有意品验一下此地为土著们备下的饮料——这地方之僻远，不亚于利比亚、潘菲利亚（Pamphylia）与美索不达米亚一带。屋里嘈杂的人语提醒他，他即将列席伦敦工人真正的议会；他四下张望，寻找那处更僻静的入口——所谓的“雅座”。他在一条窄小的板凳上坐定，点了啤酒，便听起那边大堂里叮当作响的谈话：那是一场毫无意思的争论，忽而暴怒，忽而伤感，动辄搬出比尔与汤姆来作证；中世纪的语言孑遗、乔叟笔下才有的字眼，被人满腔热忱、津津有味地喷吐出来；酒罐掼落、铜币在锌皮柜台上敲得脆响，这一切的喧声替这场争论打着通奏低音。戴森正啜着啤酒的间隙安闲地抽着烟斗，一个面目模糊的身影与其说是走进、不如说是溜进了这间隔间。那人一眼看见戴森安然坐在角落，猛然一惊，目光锐利地四下一扫。他仿佛上了线，受某台电机操纵：酒保问他要用些什么时，他险些夺门而逃；接杯子时，手抖个不停。戴森带着些许好奇打量他：那人围裹得几乎只露嘴唇，一顶软毡帽压到眼上；他仿佛畏避每一道目光，大堂里一声愈加刺耳的嗓音蓦地扬起，竟似在他身上引起共鸣，叫他像一汪果冻似的抖颤不已。眼见一个人紧张到这般地步，实在可怜；戴森正要向那人搭一句寻常探问的闲话，又进来一个人，一手搭上他的臂膀，低声咕哝了一句什么，便如来时一般倏忽而去。可戴森认出了他：正是那个油嘴滑舌、脸刮得溜光的伯顿——此人撒谎的天才，先前曾展示得那样阔气；不过戴森也未多想，因为他全部的观察力，都被眼前这既可悲又怪诞的景象吸干了。那只手初一搭上臂膀，那不幸的人便像装了转轴似的霍然回身，一声低低的哀鸣退缩回去，仿佛落网的哑畜。血色从这可怜人的脸上逃尽，皮肤转成灰色，仿佛一道死亡的暗影掠过空中，正落在那张脸上；戴森听见一声哽咽的低语——

===Original===
“Mr. Davies! For God’s sake, have pity on me, Mr. Davies. On my oath, I say﻿—” and his voice sank to silence as he heard the message, and strove in vain to bite his lip; and summon up to his aid some tinge of manhood. He stood there a moment, wavering as the leaves of an aspen, and then he was gone out into the street, as Dyson thought silently, with his doom upon his head. He had not been gone a minute when it suddenly flashed into Dyson’s mind that he knew the man; it was undoubtedly the young man with spectacles for whom so many ingenious persons were searching; the spectacles indeed were missing, but the pale face, the dark whiskers, and the timid glances were enough to identify him. Dyson saw at once that by a succession of hazards he had unawares hit upon the scent of some desperate conspiracy, wavering as the track of a loathsome snake in and out of the highways and byways of the London cosmos; the truth was instantly pictured before him, and he divined that all unconscious and unheeding he had been privileged to see the shadows of hidden forms, chasing and hurrying, and grasping and vanishing across the bright curtain of common life, soundless and silent, or only babbling fables and pretences. For him in an instant the jargoning of voices, the garish splendor, and all the vulgar tumult of the public-house became part of magic; for here before his eyes a scene in this grim mystery play had been enacted, and he had seen human flesh grow gray with a palsy of fear; the very hell of cowardice and terror had gaped wide within an arm’s breadth. In the midst of these reflections, the barman came up and stared at him as if to hint that he had exhausted his right to take his ease, and Dyson bought another lease of the seat by an order for more beer. As he pondered the brief glimpse of tragedy, he recollected that with his first start of haunted fear the young man with whiskers had drawn his hand swiftly from his great coat pocket, and that he had heard something fall to the ground; and pretending to have dropped his pipe, Dyson began to grope in the corner, searching with his fingers. He touched some thing, and drew it gently to him, and with one brief glance, as he put it quietly in his pocket, he saw it was a little old-fashioned note book, bound in faded green morocco.

===Chinese===
“戴维斯先生！看在上帝份上，可怜可怜我吧，戴维斯先生。我起誓，我要说——”话音渐低，至于无声：他听完了那句口信，想咬住嘴唇，想唤起一点男子汉的气色来撑住自己，却终归徒然。他呆立片刻，像白杨的叶子一般瑟瑟摇颤，随即出门到了街上；戴森默默想道：他是头顶着劫数走的。他去后不到一分钟，戴森脑中电光一闪——他认得这个人：那正是许多机巧之士苦寻不获的戴眼镜的年轻人；眼镜固然不见了，可那苍白的脸、黑黑的连鬓胡、怯生生的目光，足以验明正身。戴森当即明白：凭着一连串的偶然，他无意间撞上了一桩亡命阴谋的嗅迹，它像一条可憎的蛇的爬痕，在伦敦这个乾坤的大街小巷间蜿蜒出没；真相霎时在他眼前成画，他恍然悟到：自己全然不觉之间，竟有此殊缘，看见了隐匿之形的影子——在寻常生活那明亮的帷幕上追逐、奔忙、攫夺、隐没，无声无息，或者只絮叨些寓言与托词。刹那间，对他而言，酒馆里嘈杂的人语、刺目的光耀与一切俗世的喧嚣，都成了魔法的一部分；因为就在他眼前，这出阴森神秘剧的一幕已然搬演过了，他亲眼看见人的血肉因恐惧的瘫症而转为死灰；怯懦与恐怖的真地狱，就在一臂之遥处豁然大张。正思量间，酒保走过来盯着他，仿佛暗示：他安坐的权利已经用尽。戴森再点一份啤酒，为这座位续了一回租。他细细回味方才那一瞥的惨剧，记起那留连鬓胡的年轻人在魂飞魄散的头一惊里，曾把那只手飞快地抽出大衣口袋，他还听见有什么东西落了地；于是戴森装作烟斗失手，俯身在墙角摸索，用手指探找。他触到一物，轻轻拢向自己，趁从容纳入衣袋之际扫了一眼：那是一本小小的旧式记事簿，装帧是褪色的绿摩洛哥革。

===Original===
He drank down his beer at a gulp, and left the place, overjoyed at his fortunate discovery, and busy with conjecture as to the possible importance of the find. By turns he dreaded to find perhaps mere blank leaves, or the labored follies of a betting-book, but the faded morocco cover seemed to promise better things, and hint at mysteries. He piloted himself with no little difficulty out of the sour and squalid quarter he had entered with a light heart, and emerging at Gray’s Inn Road, struck off down Guilford Street, and hastened home, only anxious for a lighted candle and solitude.

Dyson sat down at his bureau, and placed the little book before him; it was an effort to open the leaves and dare disappointment. But in desperation at last he laid his finger between the pages at haphazard, and rejoiced to see a compact range of writing with a margin, and as it chanced, three words caught his glance, and stood out apart from the mass. Dyson read:

===Chinese===
他把啤酒一饮而尽，离开了那地方，为这场走运的发现喜不自胜，脑子里忙着揣度这桩意外所得可能的分量。他一时怕翻开的不过是空白纸页，一时又怕是赌注簿上吃力写下的种种蠢话；可那褪色的摩洛哥革封皮，似乎许诺着更好的东西，暗示着隐秘。他费了好一番手脚，才把自己从那片当初怀着轻快心情走进去的酸腐卑陋的地段里领出来；出到格雷律师学院路（Gray’s Inn Road），他折入吉尔福德街（Guilford Street），急急赶回家去，一心只盼一支点亮的蜡烛与一室清静。

戴森在写字台前坐下，把那本小簿子摆在面前；要翻开纸页、直面失望，须得费一番气力。但绝望之下，他终于把手指随手插进书页之间，一眼望见密密匝匝的一栏字迹，还留着页边，不由得心中一喜；也是碰巧，有三个词跳入他的眼帘，从字阵中凸现出来。戴森读到：

===Original===
> The Gold Tiberius,

===Chinese===
> 提比略金币，

===Original===
and his face flushed with fortune and the lust of the hunter.

He turned at once to the first leaf of the pocketbook, and proceeded to read with rapt interest the

===Chinese===
财运与猎人的欲念，叫他满面生光。

他当即翻到记事簿的第一页，全神贯注地读起那篇——

### History of the Young Man with Spectacles / 戴眼镜的年轻人史
===Original===
From the filthy and obscure lodging, situated, I verily believe, in one of the foulest slums of Clerkenwell, I indite this history of a life which, daily threatened, cannot last for very much longer. Every day, nay, every hour, I know too well my enemies are drawing their nets closer about me; even now, I am condemned to be a close prisoner in my squalid room, and I know that when I go out I shall go to my destruction. This history, if it chance to fall into good hands, may, perhaps, be of service in warning young men of the dangers and pitfalls that most surely must accompany any deviation from the ways of rectitude.

===Chinese===
从这污秽湫隘的寓所——它坐落在克勒肯维尔（Clerkenwell）数一数二肮脏的贫民窟里，这一点我深信不疑——我写下这部生平记事；这段性命日日受着威胁，撑不了多久了。每一天，不，每一个小时，我都太清楚：敌人正在把网向我收拢；即便此刻，我也被判作这间鄙陋斗室的禁闭囚徒，而我知道，一旦出门，便是赴死。这部记事倘若碰巧落入好人手中，或许还能有所用处：警告年轻人，但凡偏离正道一步，必然随之而来的，是怎样的危险与陷阱。

===Original===
My name is Joseph Walters. When I came of age I found myself in possession of a small but sufficient income, and I determined that I would devote my life to scholarship. I do not mean the scholarship of these days; I had no intention of associating myself with men whose lives are spent in the unspeakably degrading occupation of “editing” classics, befouling the fair margins of the fairest books with idle and superfluous annotation, and doing their utmost to give a lasting disgust of all that is beautiful. An abbey church turned to the base use of a stable or a bakehouse is a sorry sight; but more pitiable still is a masterpiece spluttered over with the commentator’s pen, and his hideous mark “cf.”

===Chinese===
我名叫约瑟夫·沃尔特斯（Joseph Walters）。成年之时，我发现自己拥有一笔不大却足够生活的进项，便决意将一生奉献给学问。我说的是古之学问，不是当今的这一种；我无意与那班人为伍——他们把生命耗在“校注”古典这桩说不出的下贱营生里，用无聊而多余的注疏玷污最美的书籍最美的页边，还竭尽所能叫人对一切美好之物生出经久的厌恶。一座修道院教堂沦作马厩或面包房，固然是可悲的景象；可更可怜的，是一件杰作被评注者的笔溅满污点，还有他那刺眼的“参见”（cf.）。

===Original===
For my part I chose the glorious career of scholar in its ancient sense; I longed to possess encyclopaedic learning, to grow old amongst books, to distil day by day, and year after year, the inmost sweetness of all worthy writings. I was not rich enough to collect a library, and I was therefore forced to betake myself to the Reading-Room of the British Museum.

===Chinese===
至于我，我选的是古义上学者那桩辉煌的生涯；我渴望拥有百科全书式的学识，渴望在书堆里老去，日复一日、年复一年，把一切值得阅读的文字最内在的甘美蒸馏出来。我的财力不够聚起一座藏书楼，于是只得投奔大英博物馆的阅览室。

===Original===
O dim, far-lifted and mighty dome, Mecca of many minds, mausoleum of many hopes, sad house where all desires fail. For there men enter in with hearts uplifted, and dreaming minds, seeing in those exalted stairs a ladder to fame, in that pompous portico the gate of knowledge; and going in, find but vain vanity, and all but in vain. There, when the long streets are ringing, is silence, there eternal twilight, and the odor of heaviness. But there the blood flows thin and cold, and the brain burns adust; there is the hunt of shadows, and the chase of embattled phantoms; a striving against ghosts, and a war that has no victory. O dome, tomb of the quick; surely in thy galleries where no reverberant voice can call, sighs whisper ever, and mutterings of dead hopes; and there men’s souls mount like moths towards the flame, and fall scorched and blackened beneath thee, O dim, far-lifted, and mighty dome.

===Chinese===
昏暗的、高擎的、威严的穹顶啊，多少心智的麦加，多少希望的陵寝，一切欲望归于幻灭的愁宫。人们怀着昂扬的心、做梦的头脑走进去：把那些高贵的台阶看作通向声名的天梯，把那座浮华的门廊看作知识之门；而进门之后，寻见的唯有虚妄的虚荣，所得几乎等于零。长街喧响的时辰，那里是寂静；那里是永恒的薄暮，是沉滞的气味。可在那儿，血液流得稀薄而冰冷，头脑烧得焦干；那是影子的狩猎，是结阵幻影的逐驰；是与幽灵的角力，是一场永无胜利的战争。穹顶啊，生人之墓；在你那些连呼喊也激不起回声的廊庑里，想必叹息永在低语，死去的希望永在喃喃；在那里，人的灵魂像飞蛾一般升向火焰，烧灼熏黑，坠落在你足下——昏暗的、高擎的、威严的穹顶啊。

===Original===
Bitterly do I now regret the day when I took my place at a desk for the first time, and began my studies. I had not been an habitué of the place for many months, when I became acquainted with a serene and benevolent gentleman, a man somewhat past middle age, who nearly always occupied a desk next to mine. In the Reading-Room it takes little to make an acquaintance, a casual offer of assistance, a hint as to the search in the catalogue, and the ordinary politeness of men who constantly sit near each other; it was thus I came to know the man calling himself Dr. Lipsius. By degrees I grew to look for his presence, and to miss him when he was away, as was sometimes the case, and so a friendship sprang up between us. His immense range of learning was placed freely at my service; he would often astonish me by the way in which he would sketch out in a few minutes the bibliography of a given subject, and before long I had confided to him my ambitions.

===Chinese===
如今我痛悔那一天——我初初在那儿占得一席、开始用功的那一天。我成为那地方的常客不过数月，便结识了一位安详慈祥的绅士；此人年过中年，几乎总坐在我邻座。在阅览室里，结识一个人不需要多少机缘：一次偶然的援手，一句关于目录检索的指点，再加上终日比邻而坐的人们之间寻常的客气；我便是这样认识了那位自称利普修斯医生的人。渐渐地，我来时盼他在座，他偶尔缺席，我便若有所失，友谊便这样生出了。他那浩瀚的学识听凭我随意驱使；他常常在几分钟里勾勒出一门学科的书目，令我惊叹不已；不久，我便把自己的抱负倾吐给了他。

===Original===
“Ah,” he said, “you should have been a German. I was like that myself when I was a boy. It is a wonderful resolve, an infinite career. ‘I will know all things’; yes, it is a device indeed. But it means this﻿—a life of labor without end, and a desire unsatisfied at last. The scholar has to die, and die saying, ‘I know very little.’ ”

===Chinese===
“啊，”他说，“你本该生作个德国人。我少年时也是这样。这是个了不起的决断，一条无穷无尽的路。‘我要知道一切’；是啊，这确乎是一句箴言。可它意味着——一场没有尽头的劳碌人生，和一个终究不能满足的欲望。学者不得不死，死时还得说：‘我所知甚少。’”

===Original===
Gradually, by speeches such as these, Lipsius seduced me: he would praise the career, and at the same time hint that it was as hopeless as the search for the philosopher’s stone, and so by artful suggestions, insinuated with infinite address, he by degrees succeeded in undermining all my principles. “After all,” he used to say, “the greatest of all sciences, the key to all knowledge, is the science and art of pleasure. Rabelais was perhaps the greatest of all the encyclopedic scholars; and he, as you know, wrote the most remarkable book that has ever been written. And what does he teach men in this book? Surely, the joy of living. I need not remind you of the words, suppressed in most of the editions, the key of all the Rabelaisian mythology, of all the enigmas of his grand philosophy, *Vivez joyeux*. There you have all his learning; his work is the institutes of pleasure as the fine art; the finest art there is; the art of all arts. Rabelais had all science, but he had all life too. And we have gone a long way since his time. You are enlightened, I think; you do not consider all the petty rules and bylaws that a corrupt society has made for its own selfish convenience as the immutable decrees of the eternal.”

===Chinese===
渐渐地，凭着这一类话，利普修斯把我诱惑了：他称许这条道路，同时又暗示它像寻访贤者之石一样无望；就这样，凭着艺术化的暗示，凭着无比圆熟的机锋，他一点一点瓦解了我的全部操守。“归根结底，”他常说，“一切科学中最大的科学，一切知识的钥匙，是快乐的科学快乐的艺术。拉伯雷也许是百科全书式学者中最伟大的一位；而您知道，他写出了古往今来最了不起的一部书。他在这部书里教人什么呢？无非是生之欢愉。我不必向您提起那几个字——多数版本都删去了的那几个字，拉伯雷神话的钥匙，他宏大哲学一切谜底的总钥匙：快活地活着（*Vivez joyeux*）。他的全部学问尽在于此；他的著作便是把快乐当作至高美术的法典；快乐是最精美的艺术，是众艺之艺。拉伯雷拥有全部科学，也拥有全部人生。而我们，从他那时起又走出很远。我想您是开明的；您不至于把腐败社会为一己私便订下的种种琐屑规条，当作永恒者不可更易的敕令吧。”

===Original===
Such were the doctrines that he preached; and it was by such insidious arguments, line upon line, here a little and there a little, that he at last succeeded in making me a man at war with the whole social system. I used to long for some opportunity to break the chains and to live a free life, to be my own rule and measure. I viewed existence with the eyes of a pagan, and Lipsius understood to perfection the art of stimulating the natural inclinations of a young man hitherto a hermit. As I gazed up at the great dome I saw it flushed with the flames and colors of a world of enticement, unknown to me, my imagination played me a thousand wanton tricks, and the forbidden drew me as surely as a loadstone draws on iron. At last my resolution was taken, and I boldly asked Lipsius to be my guide.

===Chinese===
他布的就是这一套道；靠着这类阴险的论证，律上加律，例上加例，这里一点，那里一点，他终于把我造成了与整个社会制度为敌的人。我那时常常渴望有个机会挣断锁链，过自由的日子，自己做自己的规矩与尺度。我用异教徒的眼睛看待人生，而利普修斯深谙如何挑动一个素来离群索居的青年天然的情欲，此道他做得炉火纯青。我仰望那大穹顶，看见它染上一个诱惑世界的火与色——那世界于我全然未知；我的想象同我玩了一千种放肆的把戏，禁果吸引我，就像磁石吸引铁一样确凿。终于我拿定了主意，壮起胆来求利普修斯做我的引路人。

===Original===
He told me to leave the Museum at my usual hour, half past four, to walk slowly along the northern pavement of Great Russell Street, and to wait at the corner of the street till I was addressed, and then to obey in all things the instructions of the person who came up to me. I carried out these directions, and stood at the corner looking about me anxiously, my heart beating fast, and my breath coming in gasps. I waited there for some time, and had begun to fear I had been made the object of a joke, when I suddenly became conscious of a gentleman who was looking at me with evident amusement from the opposite pavement of Tottenham Court Road. He came over, and raising his hat, politely begged me to follow him, and I did so without a word, wondering where we were going, and what was to happen. I was taken to a house of quiet and respectable aspect in a street lying to the north of Oxford Street, and my guide rang the bell, and a servant showed us into a large room, quietly furnished, on the ground floor. We sat there in silence for some time, and I noticed that the furniture, though unpretending, was extremely valuable. There were large oak-presses, two bookcases of extreme elegance, and in one corner a carved chest which must have been medieval. Presently Dr. Lipsius came in and welcomed me with his usual manner, and after some desultory conversation, my guide left the room. Then an elderly man dropped in and began talking to Lipsius; and from their conversation I understood that my friend was a dealer in antiques; they spoke of the Hittite seal, and of the prospects of further discoveries, and later, when two or three more persons had joined us, there was an argument as to the possibility of a systematic exploration of the pre-Celtic monuments in England. I was, in fact, present at an archaeological reception of an informal kind; and at nine o’clock, when the antiquaries were gone, I stared at Lipsius in a manner that showed I was puzzled, and sought an explanation.

===Chinese===
他吩咐我照常在四点半离开博物馆，沿大拉塞尔街北面的人行道缓步而行，在街角等候，直到有人向我搭话；然后，来的人如何吩咐，我便事事照办。我一一照办了，站在街角焦灼地四下张望，心跳得飞快，气息一口口喘。我等了有一阵，已开始害怕自己被人捉弄了，忽然觉察对面托特纳姆宫路的人行道上，有位绅士正带着显而易见的兴致望着我。他走了过来，举了举帽，客气地请我随他走；我一言不发地照办了，心里纳闷我们要去哪里，又会发生什么。我被带到牛津街以北一条街上的一所宅子前，外观安静体面；我的引路人按了铃，一名仆人把我们让进楼下一间陈设素净的大屋。我们默默坐了一阵，我留意到屋里家什虽不招摇，却件件贵重：有几口高大的橡木柜，两部极雅致的书橱，墙角一只雕花木箱，一看便知是中世纪旧物。不多时利普修斯医生进来了，以他惯常的态度欢迎我；闲谈几句之后，我的引路人便离了屋。接着一位老者踱进来，同利普修斯攀谈起来；从他们的谈话里我才明白，我这位朋友是个古玩商；他们谈起赫梯（Hittite）印章，谈起进一步发现的前景；随后又来了两三个人，大家争论起对英国前凯尔特遗迹作系统勘查是否可能。实际上，我是在出席一场非正式的考古招待会；九点钟，古物学家们散去，我盯着利普修斯，一脸的困惑显然是求他解释。

===Original===
“Now,” he said, “we will go upstairs.”

===Chinese===
“现在，”他说，“我们上楼去。”

===Original===
As we passed up the stairs, Lipsius lighting the way with a hand-lamp, I heard the sound of a jarring lock and bolts and bars shot on at the front door. My guide drew back a baize door, and we went down a passage, and I began to hear odd sounds, a noise of curious mirth, and then he pushed me through a second door, and my initiation began. I cannot write down what I witnessed that night; I cannot bear to recall what went on in those secret rooms fast shuttered and curtained so that no light should escape into the quiet street; they gave me red wine to drink, and a woman told me as I sipped it that it was wine of the Red Jar that Avallaunius had made. Another asked me how I liked the Wine of the Fauns, and I heard a dozen fantastic names, while the stuff boiled in my veins, and stirred, I think, something that had slept within me from the moment I was born. It seemed as if my self-consciousness deserted me; I was no longer a thinking agent, but at once subject and object. I mingled in the horrible sport and watched the mystery of the Greek groves and fountains enacted before me, saw the reeling dance, and heard the music calling as I sat beside my mate, and yet I was outside it all, and viewed my own part an idle spectator. Thus with strange rites they made me drink the cup, and when I woke up in the morning I was one of them, and had sworn to be faithful. At first I was shown the enticing side of things. I was bidden to enjoy myself and care for nothing but pleasure, and Lipsius himself indicated to me as the acutest enjoyment the spectacle of the terrors of the unfortunate persons who were from time to time decoyed into the evil house. But after a time it was pointed out to me that I must take my share in the work, and so I found myself compelled to be in my turn a seducer; and thus it is on my conscience that I have led many to the depths of the pit.

===Chinese===
上楼时，利普修斯提着一盏手灯照路，我听得前门一声刺耳的上锁声，门闩横木次第落上。引路人拉开一扇毡门，我们顺一条走廊走下去，我开始听见古怪的声响，一阵古怪的欢闹；随后他把我推进第二道门，我的入会便开始了。那晚我亲眼目睹的一切，我写不下来；那些密室里进行的事——百叶紧闭、帷幕重遮，不叫一线光亮漏到安静的街面上——我不忍回想。他们给我红酒喝，一位女子在我啜饮时告诉我，这是阿瓦隆尼乌斯（Avallaunius）酿的红坛之酒。另一位问我喜不喜欢法翁之酒（Wine of the Fauns）；我听了十几个狂想的名字，那酒在我血管里沸腾，我想，它搅醒了在我体内自出娘胎那一刻起便沉睡着的东西。仿佛我的自我意识弃我而去；我不再是一个思考着的主体，而是同时成了主体与客体。我混入那可怖的嬉游，眼看希腊林泉的秘仪在我面前搬演：看那令人眩晕的轮舞，坐在我的伴当身旁听那声声召唤的音乐；而我却又超然于这一切之外，像个无所事事的看客，观看自己扮演的角色。就这样，他们用奇异的仪轨灌我饮尽了那只杯盏；待到早晨醒来，我已是他们中的一员，并且立誓效忠。起初，给我看的都是事物诱人的一面。他们嘱咐我纵情享乐、除快乐外一概不问；利普修斯更亲自向我指点：最锋利的享受，便是观看那些不幸者的恐怖——他们时不时被诱进那所邪恶的宅子。可过了些时候，他们向我点明，这营生我也须担一份；于是我发觉自己轮到了去做诱骗人的那一个；因此，我的良心上记着：我亲手把许多人领到了深渊的坑底。

===Original===
One day Lipsius summoned me to his private room, and told me that he had a difficult task to give me. He unlocked a drawer, and gave me a sheet of typewritten paper, and had me read it. It was without place, or date, or signature, and ran as follows:﻿—

===Chinese===
一天，利普修斯把我召进他的私室，说他有一桩棘手的差事交给我。他开了一只抽屉的锁，递给我一页打字的纸，叫我读。那纸上没有地址，没有日期，没有署名，内容如下：——

===Original===
> “Mr. James Headley, F.S.A., will receive from his agent in Armenia, on the 12th inst., a unique coin, the gold Tiberius. It bears on the reverse a faun, with the legend **Victoria**. It is believed that this coin is of immense value. Mr. Headley will come up to town to show the coin to his friend, Professor Memys, of Chenies Street, Oxford Street, on some date between the 13th and the 18th.”

===Chinese===
> “詹姆斯·黑德利先生（James Headley，F.S.A.）将于本月12日收到其亚美尼亚代理人寄到的一枚孤品钱币——提比略金币。币背面为一法翁，铭文为“胜利”（**Victoria**）。据信此币价值连城。黑德利先生将于13日至18日之间择日进城，携币往访其友——牛津街奇尼街的梅米斯教授。”

===Original===
Dr. Lipsius chuckled at my face of blank surprise when I laid down this singular communication.

===Chinese===
我放下这份古怪的文书时一脸茫然惊愕，利普修斯医生见了，暗自窃笑。

===Original===
“You will have a good chance of showing your discretion,” he said. “This is not a common case; it requires great management and infinite tact. I am sure I wish I had a Panurge in my service, but we will see what you can do.”

===Chinese===
“这回您有的是机会显露您的干练，”他说。“这不是寻常案子；它需要极周密的调度与无穷的机变。说实话，我倒愿意手底下有一个巴汝日（Panurge）听差；不过，且看您能做出什么来。”

===Original===
“But is it not a joke?” I asked him. “How can you know, or rather how can this correspondent of yours know that a coin has been despatched from Armenia to Mr. Headley? And how is it possible to fix the period in which Mr. Headley will take it into his head to come up to town? It seems to me a lot of guess work.”

===Chinese===
“这不会是个玩笑吧？”我问他。“您怎么能知道——或者说，您这位通信者怎么能知道，一枚钱币已从亚美尼亚寄给黑德利先生？又怎么可能定准黑德利先生哪天心血来潮要进城？依我看，这满纸都是瞎猜。”

===Original===
“My dear Mr. Walters,” he replied; “we do not deal in guess work here. It would bore you if I went into all these little details, the cogs and wheels, if I may say so, which move the machine. Don’t you think it is much more amusing to sit in front of the house and be astonished, than to be behind the scenes and see the mechanism? Better tremble at the thunder, believe me, than see the man rolling the cannon ball. But, after all, you needn’t bother about the how and why; you have your share to do. Of course, I shall give you full instructions, but a great deal depends on the way the thing is carried out. I have often heard very young men maintain that style is everything in literature, and I can assure you that the same maxim holds good in our far more delicate profession. With us style is absolutely everything, and that is why we have friends like yourself.”

===Chinese===
“亲爱的沃尔特斯先生，”他答道，“我们这里不做瞎猜的营生。那些细枝末节——恕我打个比方，那些推动机器的齿轮与车轮——若要我一一细说，怕要闷坏您。您不觉得，坐在戏台前面吃惊，比绕到幕后看机关更有趣么？相信我，宁可对着雷声发抖，也不要去看那滚炮弹的人。不过说到底，您不必为“怎么”与“为何”操心；您有您的一份。当然，我会给您完密的指示，可事情办得如何，很大程度全在手法。我常听很年轻的人主张：文学里风格便是一切；我可以向您保证，同样的格言，在我们这门远为精微的行当里同样成立。在我们这里，风格绝对便是一切；这正是我们所以要结交您这样的朋友。”

===Original===
I went away in some perturbation; he had no doubt designedly left everything in mystery, and I did not know what part I should have to play. Though I had assisted at scenes of hideous revelry, I was not yet dead to all echo of human feeling, and I trembled lest I should receive the order to be Mr. Headley’s executioner.

A week later, it was on the sixteenth of the month, Dr. Lipsius made me a sign to come into his room.

===Chinese===
我心绪不宁地走了；毫无疑问，他是故意把一切留在五里雾中，我全然不知自己要扮演什么角色。我虽曾亲历种种可憎的纵饮场面，却尚未对人类情感的一切回响麻木，我发着抖，生怕接到的命令，是去做黑德利先生的刽子手。

一星期之后，正是当月第十六日，利普修斯医生向我示意，叫我进他的房间去。

===Original===
“It is for tonight,” he began. “Please to attend carefully to what I am going to say, Mr. Walters, and on peril of your life, for it is a dangerous matter﻿—on peril of your life I say, follow these instructions to the letter. You understand? Well, tonight at about half-past seven you will stroll quietly up the Hampstead Road till you come to Vincent Street. Turn down here and walk along, taking the third turning to your right, which is Lambert Terrace. Then follow the terrace, cross the road, and go along Hertford Street, and so into Lillington Square. The second turning you will come to in the square is called Sheen Street; but in reality it is more a passage between blank walls than a street. Whatever you do, take care to be at the corner of this street at eight o’clock precisely. You will walk along it, and just at the bend, where you lose sight of the square, you will find an old gentleman with white beard and whiskers. He will in all probability be abusing a cabman for having brought him to Sheen Street instead of Chenies Street. You will go up to him quietly and offer your services; he will tell you where he wants to go, and you will be so courteous as to offer to show him the way. I may say that Professor Memys moved into Chenies Street a month ago; thus Mr. Headley has never been to see him there, and moreover he is very shortsighted, and knows little of the topography of London. Indeed he has quite lived the life of a learned hermit at Audley Hall.

===Chinese===
“就在今晚，”他开口道。“请留神听我要说的话，沃尔特斯先生，这可是性命攸关的事——我再说一遍，性命攸关——务须一字不差地照办。明白了么？好。今晚七点半左右，您要不动声色地沿汉普斯特德路缓步北上，走到文森特街。拐进去，沿街走，右手第三条巷子是兰伯特台（Lambert Terrace）。顺着台子走，穿过马路，沿赫特福德街（Hertford Street）走，便进了利灵顿广场（Lillington Square）。广场上您遇到的第二个路口叫希恩街；其实那与其说是街，不如说是两堵秃墙之间的一条夹道。无论如何，务必在八点整到那条街的街角。您沿街走，就在拐弯处——望不见广场的那个弯——会见到一位白须白髯的老先生。十有八九，他正在痛骂车夫把他拉到了希恩街，而不是奇尼街。您悄悄走上前去，问有什么可效劳；他会说他要去哪里，您便客气之至，主动提出引路。我可以说一句：梅米斯教授（Professor Memys）一个月前才搬进奇尼街；因此黑德利先生从没去那里看过他；况且他深度近视，对伦敦的地理几乎一无所知。的确，他在奥德利堂（Audley Hall）过的完全是学问隐士的日子。

===Original===
“Well, need I say more to a man of your intelligence? You will bring him to this house; he will ring the bell, and a servant in quiet livery will let him in. Then your work will be done, and I am sure done well. You will leave Mr. Headley at the door, and simply continue your walk, and I shall hope to see you the next day. I really don’t think there is anything more I can tell you.”

===Chinese===
“得啦，对您这样聪明的人，还用多说么？您把他领到这所宅子；他会按铃，一个衣着素净号衣的仆人会放他进门。那时您的差事便完了，而且我确信办得漂亮。您把黑德利先生留在门口，径自继续散步便是；我指望明天还能见着您。我真想不出还有什么可嘱咐的了。”

===Original===
These minute instructions I took care to carry out to the letter. I confess that I walked up the Tottenham Court Road by no means blindly, but with an uneasy sense that I was coming to a decisive point in my life. The noise and rumor of the crowded pavements were to me but dumb-show. I revolved again and again in ceaseless iteration the task that had been laid on me, and I questioned myself as to the possible results. As I got near the point of turning, I asked myself whether danger were not about my steps; the cold thought struck me that I was suspected and observed, and every chance foot-passenger who gave me a second glance seemed to me an officer of police. My time was running out, the sky had darkened, and I hesitated, half resolved to go no farther, but to abandon Lipsius and his friends forever. I had almost determined to take this course, when the conviction suddenly came to me that the whole thing was a gigantic joke, a fabrication of rank improbability. Who could have procured the information about the Armenian agent, I asked myself. By what means could Lipsius have known the particular day, and the very train that Mr. Headley was to take? How engage him to enter one special cab amongst the dozens waiting at Paddington? I vowed it a mere Milesian tale, and went forward merrily, and turned down Vincent Street, and threaded out the route that Lipsius had so carefully impressed upon me. The various streets he had named were all places of silence and an oppressive cheap gentility; it was dark, and I felt alone in the musty squares and crescents, where people pattered by at intervals, and the shadows were growing blacker. I entered Sheen Street, and found it, as Lipsius had said, more a passage than a street; it was a byway, on one side a low wall and neglected gardens and grim backs of a line of houses, and on the other a timber yard. I turned the corner, and lost sight of the square, and then to my astonishment I saw the scene of which I had been told. A hansom cab had come to a stop beside the pavement, and an old man carrying a handbag was fiercely abusing the cabman, who sat on his perch the image of bewilderment.

===Chinese===
这些细密的指示，我一字不落地小心照办了。我承认，走上托特纳姆宫路时，我绝非心无旁骛，而是惴惴地感到自己正走向一生的一个关口。拥挤人行道上的喧嚣与传闻，于我不过是一出哑剧。布置给我的差事，我在心里一遍又一遍、无休无止地盘旋，反复自问可能的结果。走近该拐弯的地方，我问自己，危险是否已潜行在我脚边；一个冰冷的念头击中了我：我已被怀疑、被监视；每一个多看我一眼的路人，在我眼里都是一名警官。时限将尽，天色黑透，我踌躇着，一半决意不再向前，就此永远抛开利普修斯和他的党羽。我几乎已打定这个主意，忽然又笃定地想：整件事不过是一个天大的玩笑，一件荒诞不经的编造。谁能打听到亚美尼亚代理人的消息？我问自己。利普修斯凭什么知道那个具体的日子，知道黑德利先生要乘的那一班火车？又凭什么能使他在帕丁顿（Paddington）几十辆待雇的马车中，偏偏钻进某一辆？我断定这不过是一则爱尔兰式的荒唐故事，便欢欢喜喜向前走去，拐进文森特街，把利普修斯细心叮咛的路线一一穿行过去。他点名的那些街道，处处透着沉寂与一种叫人透不过气的廉价体面；天黑了，在那些霉气沉沉的广场与新月街上，我形单影只，偶尔有人啪嗒啪嗒走过，影子愈染愈黑。我走进希恩街，发现它果然如利普修斯所言，与其说是街，不如说是夹道：一条僻路，一边是矮墙、荒圃和一排房子的阴沉背面，另一边是一座木料场。我转过街角，广场从视野里消失，随即——叫我愕然的是——我看见了预告的那一幕：一辆双轮马车停在人行道旁，一位提着旅行包的老人正痛骂车夫，车夫高坐在车座上，一脸的茫然无措。
===Original===
“Yes, but I’m sure you said Sheen Street, and that’s where I brought you,” I heard him saying, as I came up, and the old gentleman boiled in a fury, and threatened police and suits at law.

The sight gave me a shock; and in an instant I resolved to go through with it. I strolled on, and without noticing the cabman, lifted my hat politely to old Mr. Headley.

===Chinese===
“没错，您刚才分明说的是希恩街，我就把您拉到这儿了，”走近时我听见车夫这么说；老先生怒火中烧，口口声声要叫警察、要打官司。

这情景叫我心头一震；转瞬间我便横下心来干到底。我信步向前，眼皮也不抬那车夫一下，只向黑德利老先生彬彬有礼地举了举帽。

===Original===
“Pardon me, sir,” I said, “but is there any difficulty? I see you are a traveller; perhaps the cabman has made a mistake. Can I direct you?”

The old fellow turned to me, and I noticed that he snarled and showed his teeth like an ill-tempered cur as he spoke.

“This drunken fool has brought me here,” he said. “I told him to drive to Chenies Street, and he brings me to this infernal place. I won’t pay him a farthing, and I meant to have given him a handsome sum. I am going to call for the police and give him in charge.”

At this threat the cabman seemed to take alarm. He glanced round as if to make sure that no policeman was in sight and drove off grumbling loudly, and Mr. Headley grinned savagely with satisfaction at having saved his fare, and put back one and sixpence into his pocket, the “handsome sum” the cabman had lost.

===Chinese===
“恕我冒昧，先生，”我说，“可是遇上了什么难处么？我看您是远来的客人；兴许是车夫弄错了。要我给您指路么？”

老头儿转向我；我注意到，他一开口便龇牙咧嘴，像条恶脾气的癞狗。

“这个喝醉的蠢货把我拉到这儿来了，”他说。“我叫他赶车去奇尼街，他却把我拉到这个鬼地方。我一个子儿也不给他——我原本还打算赏他一笔可观的车钱呢。我要叫警察来，把他扭送究办。”

听得这话，车夫似乎着了慌。他四下一望，像是要确认附近没有警察，便高声咕哝着驾车走了。黑德利先生省下了车钱，狞笑着心满意足，把一先令六便士放回口袋——那便是车夫丢掉的“可观的一笔”。

===Original===
“My dear sir,” I said, “I am afraid this piece of stupidity has annoyed you a great deal. It is a long way to Chenies Street, and you will have some difficulty in finding the place unless you know London pretty well.”

“I know it very little,” he replied. “I never come up except on important business, and I’ve never been to Chenies Street in my life.”

“Really? I should be happy to show you the way. I have been for a stroll, and it will not at all inconvenience me to take you to your destination.”

“I want to go to Professor Memys, at number 15. It’s most annoying to me. I’m shortsighted, and I can never make out the numbers on the doors.”

“This way if you please,” I said, and we set out.

===Chinese===
“亲爱的先生，”我说，“这件蠢事想必叫您恼火透了。从这里到奇尼街路可不近；若不是对伦敦相当熟，要找到那地方怕还有些难。”

“我对伦敦知之甚少，”他答道。“没有要事我从不进城，奇尼街这辈子还没去过。”

“真的么？我很乐意给您引路。我正散步散到这儿，送您到目的地，一点儿也不费事。”

“我要去梅米斯教授那里，15 号。这真叫人烦透了。我眼神不济，门牌号码从来认不出来。”

“那么请这边走，”我说，我们便上了路。

===Original===
I did not find Mr. Headley an agreeable man; indeed, he grumbled the whole way. He informed me of his name, and I took care to say, “The well-known antiquary?” and thenceforth I was compelled to listen to the history of his complicated squabbles with publishers, who had treated him, as he said, disgracefully. The man was a chapter in the Irritability of Authors. He told me that he had been on the point of making the fortune of several firms, but had been compelled to abandon the design owing to their rank ingratitude. Besides these ancient histories of wrong and the more recent misadventure of the cabman, he had another grievous complaint to make. As he came along in the train, he had been sharpening a pencil, and the sudden jolt of the engine as it drew up at a station had driven the penknife against his face, inflicting a small triangular wound just on the cheekbone, which he showed me.
